FOREIGN ASSISTANCE ACT OF 1966 WEDNESDAY, MARCH 16, 1966 HOUSE OF REPRESENTATIVES, COMMITTEE ON FOREIGN AFFAIRS, 

Washington, D.C. 

The committee met at 10:25 a.m. in room 2172, Rayburn House

Office Building, Hon. Thomas E. Morgan (chairman) presiding. 

Chairman MORGAN. The committee will come to order. 

The committee meets this morning in open session on H.R. 12449, to further amend the Foreign Assistance Act of 1961; and H.R. 12450, to promote the foreign policy, security, and general welfare of the United States by assisting peoples of the world in their efforts toward internal and external security.

Our witness this morning is the Honorable David E. Bell, Agency for International Development. 

Mr. Bell, you have a prepared statement. You may proceed, sir.

STATEMENT OF HON. DAVID E. BELL, ADMINISTRATOR, AGENCY FOR INTERNATIONAL DEVELOPMENT 

Mr. BELL. Thank you, Mr. Chairman and members of the committee. Thank you for the privilege of appearing before this committee to discuss the President's recommendations for the foreign assistance program.

Our programs of economic assistance are designed to serve a basic goal of U.S. foreign policy: to enlarge the security and well-being of the United States in a world of free nations increasingly able to provide a better life for their peoples. This goal derives from a central fact of our experience in this century: American security and growth are directly related to freedom, security, and progress in other nations. 

Progress and freedom in the less developed countries are hindered by Communist aggression and subversion and by hunger, ignorance, and disease.

Since World War II, the United States has worked with other free nations to help maintain independence and security and to help build lasting economic and social growth. These joint efforts have brought success in Western Europe and in a number of other countries, including Japan, Greece, Israel, and Taiwan. Impressive progress is now being made in other countries, such as Korea, Brazil, and Chile, with the help of American assistance. Thus, experience has demonstrated that aid coupled with strong self-help efforts by recipients-can bring lasting benefits to the countries we help and to our own national interest. 1

FOREIGN ASSISTANCE ACT OF 1966 

The President's proposals for fiscal year 1967 will carry forward our economic assistance efforts more purposefully than ever before. 

The President has proposed A 5-year authorization to provide more efficient planning of assistance programs and the greater self-help incentives. 

Rigorous application of self-help criteria.

Major new initiatives in agriculture, health, and education. 

Continued efforts to mobilize private resources for development. 

Intensified support for security and economic and social development in Vietnam and elsewhere in southeast Asia. 

These proposals are based on the review of U.S. foreign assistance efforts called for by the Congress and undertaken last fall at the President's direction. The review covered a wide range of basic issues and examined in depth the major country assistance programs. 

I shall discuss in this statement some of the major elements of the President's proposals. Much additional information is included in our detailed summary presentation volume, copies of which have been made available to the members of this committee. 

The President has proposed a 5-year authorization for economic assistance for the fiscal years from 1967 through 1971. 

The 5-year authorization request carries forward the principle adopted by the Congress in 1961 and 1962 in enacting multiyear authorizations for development loans and for the Alliance for Progress. 

Both of these authorities end within the current year. 

Multiyear authorization will enable AID to strengthen self-help incentives by making clear U.S. willingness to respond to sustained development efforts by recipients. It will provide a basis of stability and continuity for planning and carrying out economic aid programs. 

The multiyear authorization will not mean that the Congress will have less opportunity to examine and review the aid program. Quite the contrary, the President has requested an amendment to section 634(d) of the act to provide for a full and detailed annual presentation of the program to this committee and the Senate Foreign Relations Committee, if it is requested. 

Our operations will continue to be fully open to this committee and its members at all times. Moreover, I would hope that a long-term authorization would give the committee more time to examine the aid program in greater depth, both in Washington and the field. 

A detailed breakdown of the authorization requests and the new appropriations requests was to have been attached as an annex of this statement. Through a slip in the reproduction process it is still in reproduction and will be along later in the morning. 

The same material is put forward on page 1 of the presentation book that is before each member of the committee.

For fiscal year 1967 AID programs, the President is requesting $2,469 million in new appropriations, $6 million more than the amount appropriated for the current year, including the $415 million supplemental request the committee recently recommended. The principal requests include $747 million for "Supporting assistance," $665 million for "Development loans," $231 million for "Technical cooperation grants" and $543 million for Alliance for Progress loans and grants. 

The request for fiscal year 1967 is based-as in the past 2 years-

FOREIGN ASSISTANCE ACT OF 1966 

on minimum estimates of requirements. In preparing this request we made a thorough and comprehensive examination of proposed fiscal year 1967 levels of assistance for each country and program, taking fully into account increased requirements for the Vietnam program, and the President's instructions to request only the minimum funds clearly necessary to advance important U.S. foreign policy interests. 

Reductions to minimum levels were made in proposed programs, and the request before you reflects these reductions. If the minimum amounts we are requesting should prove insufficient, we will seek additional funds from the Congress.

In addition to the economic assistance funds for fiscal year 1967, the President is requesting $917 million in new appropriations for the military assistance program. I strongly support the request for these funds which will strengthen the capacity of allied and friendly countries to guard against aggression and subversion. 

The key to successful development assistance is self-help and a cooperative relationship between donors and receipients. We have learned from two decades of experience that our aid can only supplement efforts of the recipients themselves. Unless these nations undertake strong measures through sound policies, through careful and concentrated use of their own resources on development priorities, and through enlisting the participation of their people in the development process-U.S. aid cannot accomplish major results. The President has made it clear that we will not provide major assistance to those nations that do not help themselves.

As the Alliance for Progress gains momentum, we are particularly encouraged by the strong self-help measures being undertaken in a number of Latin American countries. While progress in that continent remains uneven, a number of countries are undertaking exceptionally strong measures in such fields as tax reform, educational reform, agricultural improvement, and housing. We are also impressed by the progress being made among Guatemala, El Salvador, Honduras, Nicaragua, and Costa Rica in forming a strong Central American economic community. Our assistance to these countries is designed to facilitate their efforts to accelerate economic integration of the region. 

As in recent years, AID's development lending is concentrated in countries undertaking major development efforts; in fiscal 1967, 84 percent of the anticipated development loans, including the Alliance for Progress are expected to go to eight countries-Brazil, Chile, Colombia, India, Korea, Nigeria, Pakistan, and Turkey.

AID has found that program loans an important part of our development loan request can be particularly effective in inducing self-help measures and better economic performance by recipient countries. Program loans, which are concentrated in a few key countries, finance imports from the United States of raw materials, spare parts, and equipment needed to maintain development momentum. 

These loans are normally coupled with an agreement on the part of the recipient to make needed improvements in overall economic policy and performance. Often program loan funds are released in quarterly segments, following careful review of the country performance toward agreed-upon goals. The cases of Pakistan, Colombia, Korea, and 

Brazil offer illustrations of the varied and flexible ways in which pro-

FOREIGN ASSISTANCE ACT OF 1966 

gram loans can help achieve economic progress under sound self-help programs. 

Our proposed program for fiscal year 1967 provides for new emphasis in agriculture, health, and education. 

As a result of the review of foreign assistance programs last fall, the President instructed us to place renewed and stronger emphasis on the basic fields of agriculture, health, and education. 

In the field of agriculture, the President has presented, in the For eign Assistance Act and in the Food for Freedom Act, coordinated recommendations for achieving more rapid growth in food supply in the developing countries, and for food and nonfood aid to that end. 

The objective is to help each developing country, as soon as possible, to gain enough economic strength either to produce the food it needs or to purchase it commercially.

AID's part in achieving this objective is to provide technical and capital assistance, both through use of the aid appropriations and through our guarantees and other authorities to support private 

American investment abroad.

For fiscal year 1967, AID expects to increase by more than onethird the appropriated funds devoted to agriculture to a level of $455 million. To this will be added some $57 million in voluntary contributions to international organizations for activities in agriculture, making a total of $512 million. 

First priority will be increased financing for fertilizer imports and for building of fertilizer plants. About one-third of the $512 million is expected to be used for this purpose. In addition, AID currently has before it applications for investment guarantees covering $250 million in planned American private investment in fertilizer production overseas.

Major efforts also will be made to expand agricultural extension service activities, provide training for more agricultural technicians, develop new irrigation facilities, build farm-to-market roads, encourage sound programs of land reform, and expand agricultural credit facilities.

In the field of education, the President has proposed a substantial increase in AID's activities in helping improve educational systems in developing countries. He has also proposed a new International 

Education Act which would authorize the Department of Health, Education, and Welfare to support expanded international studies and programs by American schools and colleges. 

So far as AID is concerned, we expect in fiscal year 1967 to devote about $155 million to education, a 50-percent increase over currentyear programs. Including voluntary contributions to international organizations for activities in education, the total will be $176 million. 

These funds will cover a wide range of activities, including assistance for educational planning, teacher training, vocational education, and higher education.

In the field of health, again the President is recommending increased activities by AID, plus a new International Health Act which will authorize the Department of Health, Education, and Welfare to enlarge the work of the Public Health Service in this field. For fiscal year 1967, AID expects to obligate about $154 million.

In addition, contributions to international organizations for health activities will total some $24 million. 

In fiscal year 1967 AID plans to step up its activities to- Combat debilitating diseases, particularly malaria, smallpox, measles, and cholera.

Seek new ways to combat malnutrition which takes its worst toll on preschool children.

Train health technicians. 

Cooperate with other nations and international organizations to assist developing countries seeking help in population programs. 

For fiscal year 1967 we expect even greater participation of both local and U.S. private resources in the development process. 

A vigorous private sector is vital to successful development. AID program loans and capital and technical assistance help spur development in the private sector. For example, program loans make it possible for hundreds of small privately owned enterprises in Chile, Pakistan, Brazil, Turkey, and other countries to get American materials and equipment needed for new investment and to utilize existing plant capacity. In many less developed countries, AID-financed 

American experts more than 400 in total-are working to help establish cooperatives, credit unions, or savings and loan associations. 

The participation of U.S. private resources in the development effort continues to grow. As part of the request for a 5-year authorization, we are requesting expansion of our investment guarantee authorities. 

By the end of calendar 1965 guarantees outstanding totaled nearly $2.6 billion. Continued rapid growth is expected. We are in the process of making further improvements in this program, several of them recommended in the report of the Advisory Committee on Pri vate Enterprise in Foreign Aid. 

Today 72 countries have agreed to institute the investment guarantee program, including Ceylon and British Honduras, added since January 1. The Foreign Assistance Act now includes a prohibition against assistance to any country which fails to enter an investment guarantee agreement with the United States by December 31, 1966. For a few aid recipients the internal political situation inhibits the institution of these programs. It is unlikely that agreements could be reached in the near future. Important U.S. interests are at stake in certain of these countries.

Therefore, the President is requesting that section 620 (1) of the act be amended to enable him to weigh overall U.S. objectives in determining whether or not assistance should be continued. 

AID continues to increase its use of private American skills and talents including universities, business firms, labor unions, and cooperatives. To further this policy we are requesting a new section 

211(d) of the Foreign Assistance Act to provide specific authority for AID to make grants on a selective basis to American colleges and universities to develop greater capacity for technical assistance to less developed countries.

ReWe will continue to support the many private voluntary efforts engaged in assistance activities overseas. These efforts include the voluntary relief agencies, the International Executive Service Corps program, and the Partners of the Alliance.

Vietnam will be the largest recipient of AID assistance in fiscal year 1967. Five hundred and fifty million dollars in supporting assistance is planned, more than 70 percent of the total supporting assistance request of $747 million.

The AID program assists the Vietnamese economy sustain the war effort in the face of serious inflationary pressures which could endanger basic economic and political stability. AID assistance, together with strong efforts of the Vietnamese Government, helps restrain these pressures by financing a higher level of essential imports to meet increasing demand for goods.

The AID program also supports the Government of Vietnam's vitally important rural construction efforts. About 85 percent of the 

Vietnamese live in rural areas; their security and progress are the key to success in the effort to defeat the purposes of the Vietcong. The aim of the combined security and development program in the villages there is to gradually expand the area of the countryside within which people can live reasonably secure against assassination and terror and can look forward to some progress toward economic and social improvement.

AID also is providing increased support to the Government's refugee relief programs, coordinating assistance from U.S. sources and other countries and helping develop long-range programs of resettlement and rehabilitation. In addition, substantial funds are required to break logistics bottlenecks in the economy-inadequate port facilities, coastal shipping, airlift capacity, and the like.

The uncertain situation in Vietnam makes it impossible to make firm projections of requirements for future programs. Therefore, the 

President is requesting authority for such supporting assistance funds as may be necessary for Vietnam. We also are requesting authority to use supporting assistance funds made available for use in Vietnam to meet special administrative expenses for the Vietnam program. 

The struggle in Vietnam has intensified the urgency of efforts to eliminate root causes of unrest in southeast Asia. Although this part of the world is potentially rich, realization of this potential will require intensified development efforts by the southeast Asian countries themselves.

On April 7, 1965, the President pledged U.S. assistance, invited other developed countries to participate, and called upon the countries of southeast Asia "to associate themselves in a greatly expanded cooperative effort for development."

To carry forward these efforts the President requested and received congressional approval for U.S. participation is the Asian Development Bank. The Bank will be a significant step toward regional cooperation. 

We also are requesting new legislation for regional development.

Title VIII-Southeast Asia Multilateral and Regional Programs will authorize the President to furnish assistance to promote social and economic development through multilateral institutions and programs or projects which serve regional development purposes. No appropriations are being specifically requested under this title for fiscal year 1967. Support for these regional activities will be provided from funds appropriated to other accounts.

The President's foreign assistance programs for fiscal year 1967 will help strengthen our own security.

They state clearly that the United States is prepared to commit its resources to help those nations that will help themselves. 

If these nations respond in a spirit of cooperation, I believe we can achieve further progress in the struggle to overcome hunger and poverty, to achieve freedom and peace. 

Mr. Chairman, I now have annex A, which I will submit for the record, and I would be happy to respond to any questions the committee might have.

Chairman MORGAN. Thank you, Mr. Bell. 

Without objection the annex will appear in the record at this point.

Chairman MORGAN. Thank you, Mr. Bell.

Mr. Bell, the economic authority under this bill for fiscal year 1966 was supposed to be restudied and redirected based on minimum requirements. In my examination of the presentation books last night it revealed to me that three-fourths of the funds programed for 1967 go to approximately 19 countries getting over $20 million apiece. 

Last year's book showed three-fourths of the funds programed for 17 countries getting over $20 million and this year's presentation they are pretty much the same countries. 

I have come to the opinion that the program you are proposing today is and does the same things that the program presented last year was supposed to do. Is there much difference?

Mr. BELL. There are two or three important differences, Mr. Chairman. The first, of course, is that there is a very large increase during the present year and in fiscal 1967 in supporting assistance funds, primarily for Vietnam. As you will note, sir, in the totals of our request-which are on the top of page 4 of my statement-for the first time in several years, the request for supporting assistance is the largest appropriation request by funding category before the committee.

That request reflects a development which we are not happy with at all. We had been very pleased over the previous several years to be able gradually to reduce the amounts going into supporting assistance and to increase the amounts going into development assistance. 

Because of the necessary expansion in our activities in Vietnam, and to a lesser extent elsewhere in southeast Asia, that trend has been temporarily reversed. The first and major difference, therefore, between this year's presentation and last year's is that because of the situation in Vietnam we have had to increase greatly the request for supporting assistance.

With respect to the rest of the program, the principal change is the one that you refer to and that I have spotlighted in my statement; namely, that in our development efforts, under the President's instructions, we are placing considerably stronger emphasis on the basic role of agriculture and education and health activities. We are not abandoning at all other important developmental types of activities, but we are increasing the emphasis on those three fields. 

The magnitude of the program elsewhere in the world apart from southeast Asia is approximately the same as what we are authorized to do in the present year under the programs which the Congress approved last fall.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Bell, 12 of these countries that were programed last year appear to be getting more this year than they were programed to get in fiscal year 1966. Are these increases due to the educational programs and agriculture increases or are there some other reasons for these 12 countries getting more funds than they got in 1966 ?

Mr. BELL. There are several reasons for that, Mr. Chairman, all of which can be gone into in such detail as the committee wishes. In general the increases in the countries where there are increases do reflect the increased emphases particularly on agriculture. Much more money typically goes into agriculture than into education and health. 

There are also special circumstances.

For example, in the case of the Indian subcontinent, our assistance activities for India and Pakistan have been essentially interrupted during the past 6 or 8 months for reasons with which the committee is very familiar. They are gradually and carefully now being resumed.

We do not expect to commit for aid to these countries all of the funds which were appropriated for that purpose. The material before you in the book shows the amount that we expect to be carried forward and how that is related to next year's program. But the program for India and Pakistan is expected to be larger in fiscal year

1967 as against fiscal year 1966 for that special reason. There are other similar special cases in various other parts of the world. 

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Bell, in the reexamination, restudying this program for fiscal year 1967, was any effort made to reduce our foreign aid to any of these countries in order to have money to meet our increased expenditures in South Vietnam?

Mr. BELL. Yes, sir; there was. The requests that came in from our missions in each country-from our ambassadors who have been instructed to consider on a hardheaded basis what would be desirable from the point of view of U.S. interest in each country-came to a substantially larger total than is here before this committee. 

We made a series of careful reviews and reductions in the various stages of the budget process last fall. The committee sees before it a very much stripped-down version of what perfectly responsible and serious people honestly believed had been important to the United States.

In applying the difference between important and necessary or essential, we did in fact reduce the program request in a great many cases. 

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Bell, what would be the authorization request this year, $3,367 million Mr. BELL. This would be both economic and military. 

Chairman MORGAN. Total authorization request. What was the final appropriation last year with the supplemental authorization and appropriation that has already passed the Congress for fiscal year 

1966 ?

Mr. BELL. I have before me, Mr. Chairman, the economic assistance figure which was, including the $415 million supplemental, $2,463 million, plus $1,170 million for military assistance, making $3,633 million.

Chairman MORGAN. Three billion- 

Mr. BELL. Yes; $3,633 million appropriated for fiscal year 1966 for economic and military assistance.

There is one important difference in coverage, however, between these two figures. It relates to the military assistance program. As originally put before the Congress a year ago and enacted last summer, the fiscal 1966 military assistance program included some funds for Vietnam. At the present time, as the committee knows, in the supplemental bill which the Congress is in process of passing, the additional funds needed for equipment and training for Vietnamese troops are not being carried as part of the military assistance program but as part of the regular Department of Defense program. For fiscal 1967 in the military assistance funds which would be authorized by the bill before this committee there is nothing for Vietnam. Therefore, you would have to make a special calculation to take out of the appropriations for fiscal year 1966 as passed by the Congress, the amount for Vietnam's MAP before you could get the comparable figures. I would be glad to prepare such a tabulation if it would be helpful to the committee and present it for the record. Would that be useful, sir? 

Chairman MORGAN. Is there any military assistance in this present request this year for South Vietnam? 

Mr. BELL. No, sir; there is not.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Bell, then why the great amount requested for military assistance in your presentation today, which is $917 million, not including South Vietnam. Isn't that an increase in our military assistance programs to other countries around the world? 

Mr. BELL. I don't think so. Of the $1,170 million which was requested and appropriated for military assistance, there was $258 million for Vietnam. If you take that amount out of the appropriation for last year you come to $912 million, if my quick subtraction is correct, which is almost exactly the same as now requested for non- Vietnam military assistance programs. It is about the same, Mr. 

Chairman, not an increase.

Chairman MORGAN. As a result of moving the military assistance to 

Vietnam over to the DOD budget there has not been any substantial increase in the military programs to other countries around the world? 

Mr. BELL. No, sir. 

Chairman MORGAN. Thank you, Mr. Bell. 

Mrs. Bolton. 

Mrs. BOLTON. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. I have no questions at this time.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Zablocki. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

I, too, have no questions at this point. I had requested that a detailed memorandum as to the expenditures by line item of U.S. assistance in certain fields be made available to me. This is being prepared, I understand. Also, I am particularly anxious that we will have additional justification for the 5-year authorization that has been requested, especially as it concerns the contingency fund. 

Mr. BELL. Yes, sir. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Thank you.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Frelinghuysen. 

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

Mr. Bell, as always it is a pleasure to hear you testify. I must say your reference to the money which was actually made available last year, and your request this year, sound to me suspicious. It sounds as if you had started off with an arbitrary ceiling, deciding how much you could ask on the basis of what had been asked last year.

As an example, the request this morning is only $6 million more than last year, and for military assistance you say it is exactly the same. 

Mr. BELL. Not quite. 

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. Within a few million. 

Mr. BELL. Yes.

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. When you are talking about $900 million it is mighty close to exactly the same figure. This is what the Eisenhower administration used to be criticized for. You seem to be determining needs simply on the basis of what was actually made available last year. This is too reminiscent of the testimony of Secretary 

McNamara a couple of years ago. He said Congress had made it crystal clear that it would not provide more than a billion dollars for military aid so he was not requesting more even though he needed it. 

Mr. BELL. I am offering no such position, Mr. Frelinghuysen. In the course of the budget presentation last fall, the President made it

repeatedly clear to me, and to other agency heads, that he felt it was necessary, in order to have a sensible budget, to restrict to the absolute minimum those expenditures both domestic and foreign not related to the Vietnam situation. What you see before you reflects two judgments which were reached in that budget process. One, it reflects almost exactly the same amount of money being asked for Vietnam for economic assistance in fiscal 1967 as we will have for fiscal 1966 when the supplemental has been enacted. We could be wrong on that, sir. That is the best guess we can make today as to what it will take for Vietnam. We have a very major effort underway there, as you know. We do not expect or see the necessity for it to be enlarged, but we see the necessity for it to be continued. Therefore, the Vietnam figures in fiscal years 1966 and 1967 are the same. 

With respect to the rest of the program, by a very severe judgment applied country by country, checked repeatedly and continuously with the Bureau of the Budget and the President's staff, and gone over by the President personally, it was agreed that we could in fact sustain the American interests in the other areas of the world and Vietnam by holding to the figures at the level which were appropriated by the Congress for fiscal 1966. That was not at all putting that judgment on the Congress. That was the President's judgment and mine. 

We are saying to you: It is our judgment that we will be able to carry forward the necessary American interests to be served by economic aid in Africa, in Latin America, and in the other areas of Asia outside of Vietnam within a total figure which is almost exactly the same as what is being asked for in fiscal 1966.

There are variations among countries. We do have the special circumstances which I mentioned to the chairman that we expect to have some carryover funds, particularly for south Asia, which will enable our actual program in fiscal 1967 in that part of the world to be somewhat larger than our expected program in fiscal 1966. It is that kind of a judgment which was reached. It was neither an arbitrary ceiling nor any feeling of trying to outguess what the Congress would want.

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. I am interested in your explanation, but my 

5 minutes are going to run out. I don't understand how you found additional funds to increase your programs of assistance for education, food, and health. The chart on page 42 seems to show an increase in almost every area except the contingency fund. I find-and again maybe you can check my figures that there is an increase proposed of $119 million for food programs, $67 million increase for education, and $64 million for health. This comes to a total increase of about $250 million. From what programs were cuts made to arrive at the same total in your request now as you actually received last year? 

Mr. BELL. There are two parts to the answer to that, Mr. Frelinghuysen.

First, this reflects in substantial measure the application to these fields of funds appropriated for development loans which are not earmarked for any particular substantive purpose. We have development loan funds available to us for sensible economically warranted projects. We are trying to focus those funds on agricultural projects, particularly fertilizer projects. It is our guess we will be able to shift

and apply more of the funds to that field next year than we have in the present year, or the past year. So that is one element of the change. 

Within a given total of funds for development loans, we will be putting more into agriculture.

The second element of the explanation is a similar one, but related to technical assistance kinds of activities. We are in fact proposing some enlargement of our total technical assistance efforts. If you will look at the overall summary table on pageyil. Barto 

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. There seems some enlargement in every program. The only place you have really skimped is the contingency fund which I would think is unrealistic. What is the purpose of shortchanging the contingency fund, with the developments in Indonesia, Ghana, and the Lord knows where else in the next fiscal year?

Mr. BELL. It is not the only fund, Mr. Frelinghuysen, in which there is anticipated some decline in the total amount. 

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. I don't see any other.

Mr. BELL. Right. 

Mr. WHALLEY. You also say on page 13 the United States is prepared to commit its resources to help those nations which will help themselves. Wouldn't it be better to add "as well as help the United 

States?" The United States has given billions of dollars in AID to 

France, and France certainly helped itself. Today France fails to support NATO, SEATO, and South Vietnam, all of which the United States is interested in. It should be a two-way street instead of oneway.

Mr. BELL. We would obviously be pleased if the French were supporting us on various policy issues on which they are not. However, sir, I think it would be legitimate to suggest that the aid program to France is not called in question by that fact. We provided assistance to France after World War II in order to restore the French economy and the French military forces so France could continue as a strong and independent and free country. Those purposes were achieved. We did not have any grounds to anticipate, and I am sure those who were in office at the time did not anticipate, that ever after on every issue the French would agree with us as an independent country; by definition you would expect them not to agree with us on some issues. 

I repeat, it certainly does not make me happy that they do not agree with us more than they do. I do not think you can assert that our aid program to them is in any sense a failure. It was a success. It did what it was supposed to do. The United States is much better off because France is a strong, free country rather than if it had become, as it could have after World War II, a Communist country. 

Mr. WHALLEY. Forgetting France, we still say we will help any nation that will help itself. I think we should put something in that will also help the United States, at least some of the time. 

Mr. BELL. The phrase that you refer to is shorthand. We are interested in supporting countries, assisting them to remain truly free and independent, and to work constructively toward world peace. This means that we would anticipate that in the great majority of cases we and they could work together effectively. I agree with you, sir, it is shorthand. 

Mr. WHALLEY. Thank you, sir.

Mr. HAYS. I know all that, but why are you doubling the aid to each one of them more than doubling?

Mr. BELL. We are greatly enlarging it or planning substantially to enlarge it in fiscal 1967 compared to fiscal 1966. If you will look back at the 1965 figures, the ratio is not nearly the same. 

Mr. HAYS. I looked at that. My question is, Why are you doubling it over 1966 ?

Mr. BELL. We regard fiscal 1966 as unusually low because of the special circumstances that existed. We anticipate, maybe wrongly, that we will be working effectively and cooperatively with both countries, and both of them will be concentrating, as we think they should, on economic growth and improvement, and achieving an increasingly peaceful situation rather than the opposite between the two of them. 

President Ayub, as you know, was over here visiting President Johnson a month or so ago. Mrs. Gandhi will be here this month. We think they have been getting back on a basis of much better relations between the two of them and a much stronger and more appropriate concentration on economic and social progress.

Mr. HAYS. I haven't been able to find it in all this voluminous book, but are we increasing military aid to each one of them in this coming year?

(The information follows:) U.S. ASSISTANCE TO BRAZIL DURING CASTELLO BRANCO ADMINISTRATION 

From April 1, 1964, to December 31, 1965, approximately $613 million in assistance to Brazil was authorized from U.S. Government sources. AID accounted for almost $400 million of this amount, and the food-for-peace program for some $150 million.

Of the remaining $63 million, military assistance accounted for $29 million, with the balance represented by loans from the Export-Import Bank and the Social Progress Trust Fund, and by program costs of the Peace Corps. 

A new AID program loan signed on February 10, 1965, to finance the importation of essential commodities from the United States into Brazil adds $150 million, bringing the total to approximately $763 million. 

Mr. GROSS. I am told that when the last $150 million went out to 

Dictator Branco, the Brazilian Government ordered 82 diesel locomotives from East Germany. Can this be possible?

Mr. BELL. I haven't heard of it, sir. I will investigate it and put something in the record on it. 

Mr. GROSS. I wish you would. 

(The information follows:) BRAZILIAN PURCHASE OF EAST GERMAN LOCOMOTIVES 

The AID program loan agreements with the Brazilian Government of December 1964 and February 1966, each for $150 million, finance only commodities of U.S. origin. They are, of course, not being used, directly or indirectly to finance East German locomotives. 

Brazilian importers are purchasing locomotives from the United States and East Germany. A contract between the State of São Paulo and the East German 

Government for 76 diesel electric locomotives was announced on May 20, 1965, by the State of São Paulo. According to the announcement, the 

São Paulo State Railways issued a worldwide tender for 83 such locomotives in 1963 with the deadline for submission of bids, August 20, 1963. Bids were received from eight foreign firms and the first award was made to a Japanese group which was subsequently canceled after 6 months because of financing difficulties. A French group whose bid was second also failed to obtain the contract for similar reasons. In December 1954, the railways received a proposal from an East German Government agency to supply 76 locomotives worth $20 million.

Brazil's trade with the Soviet bloc is conducted on a barter basis, and Brazil has an interbank agreement with East Germany. Brazil customarily pays for imports with surplus commodities such as coffee and cocoa. 

It is understood that the Central do Brasil Railway is planning to order $17 million worth of American diesel locomotives for delivery sometime this year. 

Mr. GROSS. Has my time expired? 

Chairman MORGAN. The time of the gentleman from Iowa has expired. 

Mr. Selden.

Mr. SELDEN. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

Mr. Bell, you were present yesterday when I pointed out to the Secretary of State that some of the 82 nations who were represented at the recent tricontinent conference in Havana are presently receiving aid under our aid program. Do you feel that aid could be cut off under section 620 (i) of the foreign aid bill to any nations which were represented there by members of their official government?

Mr. BELL. This obviously, Mr. Selden, is a question more for the 

Secretary to give you a responsible answer than I. I have looked at the question. I believe the legal issues that would be involved are: first, were these delegates in fact official representatives of their governments and, second, was the conference fomenting aggression in the sense that is meant in section 620 (i). I am advised there is considerable doubt on both of those issues as a matter of law. 

My opinion is, sir, that we certainly do not appreciate whatever, in any way, even quasi-official representatives of countries with whom we are working participating in that kind of meeting. The question, therefore, it seems to me is whether or not 620 (i) applies, and it should be examined carefully by the lawyers. We should take other kinds of action which express our discontent with that sort of behavior. 

Mr. SELDEN. The reason I asked that question is because I feel that if section 620 (i) does not apply, it should be tightened up so that it will cover similar future situations. In any event, I would hope you would look into this matter carefully.

Certainly those who attended the Havana conference and were members of their official governments, whether they were sent there officially or unofficially, were there with the knowledge and consent of their governments, and this was a meeting that advocated subversive aggression against all the nations of the Western Hemisphere, most of whom we are aiding.

If section 620 (i) does not cover a situation of that nature, I would urge you to give us suggestions through your legal staff as to how this section can be tightened up so it will cover similar situations in the future. 

Mr. BELL. I will be glad to look into that. 

Mr. SELDEN. One other question. I note you point out that about a third of the funds to be authorized for agricultural purposes are going to be used for fertilizer imports and for building fertilizer plants. 

I think this is commendable.

Has AID looked into the TVA's facilities that are located at Muscle Shoals, Ala.? If so, what is the possibility of using those facilities? 

Mr. BELL. We have indeed worked quite closely with the TVA on fertilizer questions. We regard them as the leading authority in the 

United States on many aspects of fertilizer production and distribution and use. We have used their experts on a number of occasions, at our request, to examine the situation in a specific country-for example, Turkey, Korea-to see what it is the country will need, the content and nature of the fertilizer that would be appropriate for the growing conditions of that country.

We are in discussion with the TVA right now about a new type of concentrated and cheap combination urea phosphate fertilizer which they have developed to the laboratory stage which needs pilot plant stage development. I expect to be talking with the Bureau of the 

Budget and the White House and the TVA people on that shortly. 

Mr. SELDEN. Is there any possibility of utilizing those facilities at 

Muscle Shoals, Ala., for the production of the cheap fertilizer to which you referred as a result of funds appropriated in this bill? 

Mr. BELL. I doubt that, Mr. Selden, for the reason that if my knowledge is correct, the TVA production facilities are almost entirely

today limited to pilot plant type operations. They expect when they develop a good new product it will be put into commercial scale production by the private companies around the United States and those same private companies are beginning to develop plants in the developing countries.

We will be helping them sometimes by loans and grants. My guess would be that we would not be putting much, if any, money into the 

TVA's own fertilizer activities. 

Mr. SELDEN. I would appreciate it if you would get your staff to give me a letter as to the status of these negotiations. 

Mr. BELL. I would be delighted to. We work very closely with Mr. 

Wagner, Chairman of the Board, and Mr. Smith, a member of the 

Board. We have a high regard for them and believe we are getting excellent cooperation from them.

Mr. SELDEN. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Derwinski. 

Mr. DERWINSKI. Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

Mr. Bell, I am a little surprised at your emphasis on the need for a 5-year authorization. If my memory is correct, a year ago Mr. 

Rusk and you in stating your views vigorously defended the 1-year authorization. 

Mr. BELL. As against a 5-year authorization?

Mr. DERWINSKI. As against the 2-year, as proposed by the chairman of the Senate Foreign Relations Committee. Wasn't that a difference of opinion between the two Houses? 

Mr. BELL. I don't recall it that way.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Derwinski, there was a difference between the House and the Senate. As you know, I headed the House conferees. My position was firm on the 1-year authorization. I must say for Mr. Bell he wasn't for the Senate bill, but he was for the 

2-year authorization. 

Mr. BELL. I appreciate that, Mr. Chairman. 

Mr. DERWINSKI. Mr. Bell, there is one very your statement that I see as an inconsistency. shall be vigorous efforts in the self-help area. recipient countries will include India. interesting emphasis in 

You emphasized there 

You indicate later the

What indication do we have that the Indians are really trying to help themselves? For example, a great part of their problem is their tragic social system which works against their own best interests. 

Wouldn't that fall under self-help in attempting to improve their own complicated conditions? 

Mr. BELL. I would consider under the term "self-help" could be listed any significant change in any significant obstacle to their own development. We necessarily work primarily in the direct field of economic policy and economic programs. In the Indian case, for example, we have concentrated over the last year or so on the measures that they can take and are beginning to take which will lead to increased agricultural output.

This involves such things as a better incentive pricing system so the farmers will have an incentive to produce more. It involves a much freer hand for private fertilizer companies to invest and to distribute and to sell their commodities-not under tight, limiting Government

controls, but in much more open free market conditions. It involves credit measures, seeds, et cetera.

In addition to measures in the agricultural field we are concerned about their overall budget and fiscal policies, about the ways in which they support and open up the activities of the private sector in the industrial field, and so on. 

These activities do necessarily impinge upon elements of their social systems, attitudes that are traditional in their society, such as the extended family system. We expect that as the Indian leaders both public and private concentrate on these economic changes, they will necessarily have an effect in changing some of their social patterns. 

But these are matters of very great sensitivity, obviously, and it has been our practice, and I would expect it to continue to be our practice, to concentrate on the economic measures that are essential. And if there are conclusions to be drawn or effects to be felt in the sensitive social field, we desire to let the Indians draw those conclusions and bring about those effects in their own way and by their own means. 

You see what I am saying? 

Mr. DERWINSKI. Just two quick comments. First we have a paper prepared by committee staff which is entitled "Comparative Tables of 

Authorizations and Appropriations." I don't want you to comment on it, but I am making the comment that this comparative table should be labeled a conservative table. I am afraid you will be- 

Mr. BELL. Ought to be labeled what?

Mr. DERWINSKI. We are calling it a "comparative" table. I am saying it should be "conservative" because I am fearful you will be coming back for a fairly substantial supplemental authorization for the fiscal 

1967, as you did in fiscal 1966. Are my fears at all reasonable?

Mr. BELL. We have tried, Mr. Derwinski, to anticipate as best we can today the requirements for Vietnam and for other hotspots around the world. I certainly cannot promise you, sir, and would not want to assert that there won't be any more requirements. There may well be. We don't know of anything today that isn't covered by this money. I don't want to speculate on the probabilities or possibilities of change.

Mr. DERWINSKI. You indicate, Mr. Bell, that the President is placing special emphasis on intensified support in southeast Asia and particularly Vietnam.

Couldn't you tie the support to South Vietnam to indicate that those countries cooperating with us there in a more practical sense would qualify as AID recipients?

Mr. BELL. This is a very interesting issue that we deal with very frequently in many parts of the world. We hold quite firmly to the view that a general concurrence with the United States or agreement on specific policies, if it exists, does not itself qualify anybody for assistance. There is no country with whom we have more agreement than the British, but we aren't in the aid business with the British because they don't need it. We think the Malaysian Government is following sound and sensible policies and generally constructive in that part of the world, but thus far at least it is not clear that they require economic assistance on the concessional terms provided under this act. 

This situation could change, but there is no provision for aid to Malaysia in this bill, nor has there been in recent bills.

The criterion is both cooperative attitude and policies, and real need. 

Need is qualified, again, by a willingness to act in a self-help manner. 

In Vietnam or in any emergency situation there are limits on the extent to which you can ask for and get self-help activities. You will have seen in the statements made in Honolulu and at other times that we do in fact expect the Vietnamese Government to undertake in the economic field everything they can possibly undertake. For example, in combating inflation in Vietnam, the major steps to be taken are theirs and not ours.

Mr. DERWINSKI. Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

You are very familiar with this, sir.

Mr. ZABLOCKI (presiding). Mr. Farbstein. 

Mr. FARBSTEIN. Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

Mr. Bell, I want to quote to you from an item that appeared in this morning's Times. If you would rather not comment on what I am going to read to you, please tell me, because I don't want to catch you unawares, so to speak. But it says: "The GOP proposes guidelines on AID." 

I don't know whether you read that, but "25 in the House called for a wider dispersal of the benefits." And it suggests that the report, summarizing a 6-month study, called for new administrative and policy criteria to promote wider popular participation in both the governments of less developed countries and in the benefits of the U.S. AID program. 

It goes on to suggest that certain guidelines should be laid down and one of them is aid that diminishes or tends to discourage popular participation in governments not justified. 

I wish you would comment on that sentence. I will read it again : "Aid that diminishes or tends to discourage popular participation in governments not justified."

Mr. BELL. In general, I approve of the sentiment of that sentence. 

Our efforts are explicitly designed and intended to broaden participation in government and extend democratic processes, rather than

the contrary. I don't know what situation the authors of the sentence had in mind as a case in which we were not following that guideline. 

In general, I agree with them. 

In general, I thought that whole statement was quite a constructive statement. I would argue that many of the ideas are now, in fact, being applied. Others are new ideas. On a hasty reading, I thought they were worth following up and we are intending to do so. 

Mr. FARBSTEIN. As long as you contend you are going to follow up the recommendations of the GOP as contained in this report, I don't suppose there is anything for me to question you about. I think I will drop that entire thing.

Basically it would appear to me, however, that what we are seeking to do is to enlarge the security and the well-being both of the United States, as stated in your statement, and a world of free nations increasingly able to provide a better life for their peoples. We will not, will we, take any active participate actively in upsetting any governments, will we? 

Mr. BELL. No, sir,

Mr. FARBSTEIN. We are seeking to assist the people in those various countries, isn't that correct? 

Mr. BELL. Exactly.

Mr. FARBSTEIN. We hope that there will be participation by all the peoples of the government in their government by free elections and so on, but our prime purpose is to aid these underdeveloped countries and peoples so that they will be able to have a better life? 

Mr. BELL. Exactly. 

Mr. FARBSTEIN. Rather than to concern ourselves with the makeup of the government?

In other words, we won't turn down requests for food, simply because it is not a government, as we recognize, as a democratic government, or would like to have a democratic government? 

Mr. BELL. We have a bias, Mr. Farbstein, I think a clear and important and appropriate one, toward extending more broadly the democratic process. We, however, have to work with governments in countries as they are. It is our desire and intent to work with them in ways and under frameworks of cooperative agreements which will result in gradually extending and improving the participation of people in making decisions about their own affairs, in their own governments, and gradually extending and enlarging the democratic process around the world. It is a slow and frequently difficult process. 

As you put it quite correctly, we are not in a position to control it in these countries. They are independent countries and they will make up their own minds. We can take many steps, and we try to do so, which will encourage broadening the base of participation in political life and economic life, and broadening distribution of income, and broadening distribution of knowledge and of competence to participate in making their own decisions and joining in the natural life of their country. 

Mr. FARBSTEIN. There is one statement here that says no government government aid should be extended to any country that shows no interest in holding popular elections, establishing broad-or creating a civil service system based on merit. 60-929-66-pt. 1- -3

FOREIGN ASSISTANCE ACT T OF 1966 

This is a general statement which sounds very good, because I don't think that anybody should beat their wives. But supposing that we were to offer aid to a particular government, let's say, that is not a democratically ruled government, and that government refused to accept that aid unless it were passed through the hands of the government, as such. Should we then deny the people who really are starving in that government, who need food, or should we say: "Unless you become a democratic or republican government, one that is directly elected by the people, we won't give you any aid."

Mr. BELL. The statement you have quoted, in my opinion, is stated in much too absolute form. The objective is one we would agree with, all of us. To put it as flatly as is done in what you have just quoted-no aid in such and such a situation does not recognize the variety of real life and the circumstances that we have to deal with on a day-to-day basis. There are many cases in which we confront a question: Do we deal with this government under the conditions. that they are prepared to deal with us on, or don't we? 

Sometimes we don't, and sometimes we do. We don't have the choice of saying we would like to start from some other place, that we will just invent a democratic government for this country. We can't do that.

We have to work with the government that is there, and the institutions that are there. We can work toward change and we do. 

You are entirely right. Stated absolutely as that quotation does, it does not, in my opinion, recognize the nature of real life. 

Mr. FARBSTEIN. Thank you very much.

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Governor Thomson. 

Mr. THOMSON. Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

Mr. Bell, how are you getting along in your recruitment drive, urging people to go to Vietnam? Has anyone responded other than 

Members of Congress, so far?

Mr. BELL. Yes, Governor, we are doing quite well, as a matter of fact. We are getting a great many applications. We have quite high standards. These jobs are very tough. They require a number of important personality characteristics; stability under great pressures; ability to live with a minimum of amenities; a maximum of personal hardship, and so on. 

Mr. THOMSON. Just the ability to live there is something.

Mr. BELL. They have to function on top of that. 

Out of the hundreds that apply we get it worked down to usually tens or scores of people that we consider really qualified. We have been finding such tens and scores of people. We have a lot of them now in training, in language training and other kinds of studies. 

Many of them, for reasons of urgency, we have shipped out, and they are working in Vietnam right now.

My impression is that the response is good. We are finding substantial numbers of persons who are willing to go even under the very difficult circumstances that they know they have to look forward to. 

We make sure that we don't kid anybody. When they sign up with us, they know they are in for a very rough ride.

Mr. THOMSON. Returning to this authorization which you are requesting, in 7 out of the 12 categories you are requesting such funds as may be necessary. 

Mr. BELL. That is right. o

Mr. THOMSON. Is it your understanding that for 5 years this committee will have no further authority in the field of authorizing under these seven categories in the event the bill is passed in its present form?

Mr. BELL. It would be my expectation, sir, that the committee would keep a very close eye on how this mission was carried out. If at any time, it felt we were going beyond what the committee intended, or in any other way improperly carrying out our functions, the committee would tell us so directly and would have the option of passing an amendment to the statute putting any change into effect that it wished. 

I would think it would be entirely incorrect to interpret our request as a kind of blank check. That is not at all what we are asking. We believe that it would be simpler for everyone concerned, and more efficient, to have an authorization statute on the books which would carry for 5 years unless changed. However, under that authorization statute, there would be a continuous and close process of surveillance by this committee, field visits, Washington hearings, staff studies, everything else that the committee would wish to follow out. Any time the committee felt we were doing something wrong, we would be in communication, and obviously amendments could be passed at any time. 74

Mr. THOMSON. Now, I want to get back to this question about India. 

Specifically what is the Indian Government doing to affect the reforms that you think are essential? I understand that they have heavy taxes on the private sector. They apply quotas against the private sector. 

They insist on government control or turning over basic business to the public sector, and they are doing so many things that I am advised that Cooley loan funds, which are available in India, are not being taken up because of American know-how and machinery and management is not going there under the present oppressive conditions that the Indians are applying. 

Mr. BELL. Fortunately the situation is not that bad, Governor. We have made a substantial number of Cooley loans. The largest Cooley loan we made anywhere in the world was to an American company operating in India, International Minerals & Chemicals. They are building a fertilizer plant.

We made them a $23 million loan. The plant is under construction. 

We will be glad to put into the record the precise cost and the amounts to which we have made Cooley loans in the last 2 or 3 years. There are a significant number of American firms who have found it possible to operate in India.

This is not to say it is easy. The businessmen that you have heard from, as I have, are entirely right in saying that the Indian Government, based on our point of view and based on our judgment, does not provide nearly as favorable a climate for private activity either for

their own businessmen or for foreign businessmen as we think is in their own interest.

Mr. THOMSON. Have they so far taken any steps to relax 

Mr. BELL. The key point is that this has been changing and is changing. The situation in India today is far different than 10 years ago. The process is slow and gradual, but by now there is a marked difference. While 10 years ago, for example, it was the policy of the Indian Government that there could be private fertilizer plants the environment was such as to discourage private investment in fertilizer. Today they are really trying to get private fertilizer plants. 

There are a number of things they are trying to get more of. 

Mr. THOMSON. Specifically what have they done to relax their policies that prohibited the private sector Mr. BELL. They do now permit the establishment of private fertilizer factories under more favorable circumstances,

Let me add a couple of other illustrations, however, because that in itself is just one element of the network of redtape within which businessmen have felt that they were operating. 

Mr. THOMSON. We heard complaints as recently as last December in this particular area.

Mr. BELL. One of the changes I think they made in December involves a policy that fertilizer companies, private companies, could only sell their products under tight price controls, and within areas which were laid out and defined by geographic areas. And this was done by the Government.

Both of those policies were revoked. Now a private fertilizer company can sell anywhere it wants to in India and for any price. the market will bear. This is elementary commonsense to us. It did require a change in Indian Government policy, but the change was made. A third illustration involves credit to farmers to buy fertilizer which has been very troublesome. The Indian Government has now authorized and the Central Bank of India is effectuating a special plan to enlarge the availability of credit for the procurement and use of fertilizer-production credit, as we would think of it. 

While I do not intend to argue at all that the situation is anywhere near as good as we want to see it, I am arguing and do argue that change has been underway. Change has accelerated recently.

We believe that it is possible under today's conditions for American businessmen to invest effectively in India, and we expect the conditions to become progressively more favorable, rather than the reverse. 

Mr. THOMSON. One question on Vietnam.

How is the war against profiteering and black-marketing coming in Vietnam! 

Mr. BELL. It is not a question I can answer in one sentence, Governor.

The U.S. Government is continuing to increase its own commitment of auditors, police advisers, et cetera. We are continuing to improve our own assistance to the Vietnamese Government in improving its police services. We have en route they should be arriving this weekseveral customs inspectors from the U.S. customs service, who will be working as a team with the Vietnamese customs service, to improve that element of the control system.

We are working with them on improving tax administration. All of this effort is strong and is rising. Nevertheless, there is still a great deal to be done. You can walk down the street in Saigon and see hair spray, whisky, and so on, which obviously came from American PX's. It was either stolen or was sold by some military or other person who had access to the PX.

We are trying to track down all of these elements of leakage or graft or whatnot.

The aid program, itself-the economic aid program-is perhaps under better control than any other element of the situation for two reasons: First, we limit our function, as you know, by and large, to a limited list of commodities. We follow those commodities closely. We don't finance whisky or hair spray. We finance fertilizer and rice and iron and steel products, things like that. They are somewhat easier to keep track of.

at 1 Second, insofar as the financing is concerned, our system is to pay the exporter. Our dollars don't go through anybody's hands in Vietnam. The things are ordered and then we pay. A check goes to an 

American exporter or occasionally an exporter from another country. 

They don't go through any financial process in Vietnam itself. 

For these reasons we think the American economic aid-financed commodities are probably more tightly controlled than anything else that is going on in Vietnam at the present time. Even they are certainly subject to various kinds of leakages and losses, and we must continue and are increasing our systems of policing and so on, as the Department of Defense is, on all the matters under their control.

Mr. BELL. Mr. George Woods, the president of the World Bank, has said that the judgment of the Bank's staff-a very experienced and an able staff is that if the developing countries undertake the appropriate self-help measures it is their judgment that on the average over the next 5 years $3 to $4 billion more than is now being provided by the countries and the international agencies could effectively be applied to the development problem. It is very important to note his qualification: If the countries do what they can for themselves. 

Some countries obviously are not. He means by that that they follow sensible fiscal and monetary policies, fight inflation, and so on. 

He also means they organize their own governmental and private organizations and institutions so that they can carry out instruction in education and other projects. 

There are two other qualifications or comments that I would make on his statement. The first and most important one is that he is talking about a need for capital of all types, public and private. There are developing countries which can in our judgment afford to obtain their capital on commercial terms. Those countries are not included in our AID program because they don't need to be included in our AID program. In Mr. Woods' $3 or $4 billion a year, for example,

there will certainly be some amount of money-I don't know how much-which the World Bank staff calculates as the requirements for capital for Taiwan. We agree Taiwan does need more capital and probably rising amounts of capital as the standard of living of their people is increasingly enlarged. 

We also feel, and I am sure Mr. Woods would agree with us, that 

Taiwan is now sufficiently strong economically so they don't have to obtain that capital on concessional terms. So within his $3 or $4 billion additional amount of money, there is some portion of it which can be met through hard loans from the World Bank and other sources. What he is saying is therefore not the same thing as saying the world needs to provide $3 or $4 billion of aid as we understand aid in relation to the bill that is before this committee. 

Finally, and this is the least important of these comments, I think there must be some technical difference of judgment as between his staff and ours. I would not expect it to be very large. This does rest on a guess as to how many projects can be developed in each country over a given period of time. And we might be a little more skeptical or doubtful than his staff. But the main elements are the ones I have indicated. First of all, he has stated and would agree that capital in this amount might be forthcoming only if all the countries that need help will do everything they can for themselves. That is a desirable performance objective, but it is not necessarily a statement of the present situation. There are some that aren't doing all they could. Therefore, you would reduce his figure somewhat for that reason.

Secondly, his figure includes capital both on commercial and on concessional terms. Therefore, it is not to be directly compared with the amount of aid that we have. 

You mentioned this 1 percent target. The 1 percent target is a difficult figure to work with because it wasn't developed in relation to a careful estimate of the needs of the developing countries. It is a statement which the United States has subscribed to: that is, if the needs are there, 1 percent of the incomes, or the GNP of the advanced countries is not a burden for them to bear. 

We agree.

(The information follows:) REQUIREMENTS FOR INCREASED CHILEAN AGRICULTURAL OUTPUT 

The Frei government is publicly committed to increasing agricultural output and improving the standard of living of the rural population. A new sweeping agrarian reform bill was submitted to Congress on November 22, 1965. agriculture terms of trade are being improved. In order to improve the invest- 

The

ment climate in agriculture, the Government of Chile has permitted agricultural prices to increase more rapidly than industrial prices. Agricultural cooperatives are being strengthened, and detailed sector planning is in process. 

President Frei has announced plans to resettle some 100,000 farmers on their own land under the agrarian reform plan during his administration. In recent experience it has cost an estimated $4,000 each for resettling a limited number of farm families, but this amount may well be reduced in any broad-scale resettlement program. [Security deletion.] Additional investment is needed, but in all phases of agricultural development. 

Our efforts in agriculture are directed primarily to improving the general investment climate for agriculture. The AID program loan is designed to reduce inflation and help the Government of Chile to carry out basic monetary and fiscal policies necessary for economic development which will provide the incentive for increased investment in agricultural production. Commitments from the Government of Chile are obtained on the implementation of land reform, development of a multiyear agricultural plan, development of plans for modernizing pricing policy and marketing systems, and to continue to improve the agricultural terms of trade. We provide technical assistance to the Government of Chile under the Chile-California program to assist them in establishing the necessary institutional bases and planning systems to carry out these structural changes. We have also provided capital assistance directed toward increased agricultural production-an IDB-SPTF supervised agriculture loan of $6.5 million in 1962, a $3.3 million AID loan to rural electric cooperatives, and a $3.6 million AID loan for fertilizer imports; the latter two were provided in 1965. 

AID does not have a detailed estimate of the monetary requirements needed to achieve a 5-percent growth rate in agricultural production. However, a major job of the Chile-California program group is to work with the Chileans in the development of an agricultural investment plan. AID believes that such a growth rate is an attainable goal and would meet Chile's food requirements. 

The general activities necessary, by Chile assisted by AID when necessary, to achieve this goal are considered to be:

I. AGRICULTURAL POLICIES 

AID will continue to assist the Government of Chile in establishing and imple menting agricultural policies regarding realistic prices, stabilization, and especially investment incentives. These general program policies are now beginning to be implemented.

II. MARKETING 

The AID program will accelerate its activities aimed at an improved marketing system with efforts to reduce the spread between farm prices and the final consumer price. For example, a carefully worked out Government program of price supports, offering farmers a reasonable minimum price for major crops, could reduce speculation and force middlemen buyers to meet the minimum price. 

III. AGRARIAN REFORM 

The present uncertainty with regard to Chile's agrarian reform remains a strong disincentive to investment in the agricultural sector. Implementation of a known program will be given the highest priority as soon as the new legislation is enacted.

IV. AGRICULTURAL CREDIT 

Farm credit is not being made available in sufficient amounts nor probably being used efficiently. As the IDB loan of $6.5 million is effectively utilized, another loan of this type should be provided. 

V. AGRICULTURE INPUTS 

The present cost of inputs into the agricultural sector are too high in relation to farm prices. Fertilizer, seeds, pesticides, and simple farm equipment should be made available to farmers at the lowest possible prices in order to spur additional agricultural investment. Fertilizer use should be increased as rapidly as possible.

As Chilean plans for increasing agricultural production become more specific and therefore capable of being implemented effectively, the United States is prepared to provide appropriate technical, and if necessary, financial assistance.

(The information follows:) LONG-RANGE PLANS FOR UTILIZATION OF AID VIETNAM PERSONNEL 

AID had 668 direct-hire employees in Vietnam at the end of February 1966. Of these, 40 percent were assigned outside Saigon. Requirements for additional people will raise the total to about 1,050 in succeeding months. 

Many AID employees there now have signed up for an additional tour of duty. 

Almost half of those whose current tour is up this year have asked to go back again. AID intends that those who do not want to return to Vietnam be continued in other constructive and satisfying assignments with the AID if they so desire. However, a substantial number have indicated they will leave AID after their Vietnam assignments to resume previous careers. 

AID has assigned staff in the Office of Personnel to analyze the problems of utilizing and further developing the talents of employees who have served in Vietnam in the context of individually prepared career plans. We are in close and continuing communication with the mission in Saigon on this. 

For many returning Vietnam employees AID will develop individual career plans, providing a sequence of assignments and locations, and any advisable training or retraining. This will help provide continuing careers for many who are doing types of work common to many of our overseas programs, such as technical advisers in education, health, and agriculture. 

However, there are other kinds of work and assignments that have been developed for the Vietnam program situation which do not clearly or directly relate to other AID activities outside southeast Asia. This category is exemplified by those critically important and useful provincial representatives and their assistants who work directly with Vietnamese villagers, local government leaders and officials. Some 75 such men will be completing assignments over the next 2 years. AID is studying the personal and vocational attributes of this group, so that we may not only move them on to other suitable assignments or special training, but also consider the possible adaptation of such attributes and techniques to meet needs elsewhere.

(The information follows:) BAR TO ASSISTANCE FOR COUNTRIES WHICH COOPERATE TO SUBVERT LATIN AMERICA 

The amendment would be made to both H.R. 12449 and H.R. 12450. It would prohibit assistance to any country which "cooperates with the Government of Cuba, the Soviet Union, or the Peoples Republic of China by conference or joint action in advancing the plans of the Government of Cuba or either of these other countries for subversion of any countries or the peoples of the Western Hemisphere." The amendment would permit the President to determine to furnish assistance notwithstanding such cooperation. 

EXECUTIVE BRANCH POSITION 

Communist subversion in the Western Hemisphere continues to be a problem, but it is not one which exists exclusively in that area of the world. It is neither necessary nor desirable to single out one geographic area for special consideration. The United States opposes such subversion wherever it occurs. Through our bilateral military and police programs, we have made significant progress in helping Latin American States to develop their internal security capabilities to deal with Communist subversion. In addition, the Rio Treaty machinery is available to generate collective action to help a country check Communist subversion. 

The phrase "cooperates *** by conference or joint action," as used in this amendment, is very broad and ambiguous, raising serious problems of legal interpretation. We assume that the phrase means some substantial and verifiable act by a government toward the end of subverting Latin American peoples or governments. Since concrete acts of that type by governments in association with Cuba, the U.S.S.R., or Communist China would be most exceptional for any free world country, the amendment would probably have little or no ef-

fect. The only countries likely to be affected are Communist countries, which are already excluded from assistance by section 620 (f) of the Foreign Assistance Act (and comparable provisions in the bills now before the Congress). 

Thus, there appears to be little prospect that the proposed amendment would operate as an added sanction against subversive actions. 

For these reasons the executive branch opposes the amendment.
