I think we probably ask too much of your time. If we could combine our audience so you could give basically the same testimony before more than one committee, you might be making sense. But

I don't think it makes sense to argue that the elimination of the Foreign 

Affairs Committee as a port of call will relieve you of much responsibility, if as Mr. Bell suggests there is still going to be a close annual review anyway, if it is requested by the Speaker. Admittedly there would be no necessity to come back for an annual review. Also, you are probably inviting trouble if you suggest that we look more closely into specific areas instead of the present across-the-board approach. 

We might keep you and your subordinates busy even if we don't have a broad review each year.

I am interested in just what your arguments are. The President himself suggests it is to signify the depth of our commitment to help those who help themselves. Surely we could signify the depth of our commitment without a long-term authorization. If we couldn't,

I would suppose that the Alliance for Progress must have been grinding

to a halt because we have no assurance that it is going to be renewed this year. I don't think there is any justification in that argument. 

I might also say that you, Mr. Secretary, yourself gave the clearest indication of the capacity of the executive branch to act whether or not 

Congress approves. On page 15 of your report you mentioned that in a personal message, and I quote:

President Johnson said, "Recognizing that the fulfillment of our goals will require"- 

And I emphasize "require." "the continuation of the joint effort beyond 1971, I wish to inform the Conference and through you your respective governments, that the United States will be prepared to extend mutual commitments beyond the time period foreseen in the 

Charter of Punta del Este."

I am not saying this is not a wise course for us to pursue. I am not saying the executive branch should not be able to make such a statement without getting authorization from Congress. What I am saying is that I don't think the argument that we in Congress need relief from the nature of our responsibilities holds much water. I would hope that there is not going to be a crumbling of the position which the House took in opposition to the other body over long-term authorizations because this suggestion has been made. 

I would guess that the 5-year authorization is simply a bargaining point and you want something less. For the life of me I can see no justification for it. I recognize that I haven't asked you any questions yet. Perhaps you would like to make a comment. 

Secretary RUSK. Mr. Frelinghuysen, I would feel somewhat presumptuous if I were to support this proposal on the basis of the way the Congress spends its time. I recall a wandering minister in my youth who used to say: "Remember, when you point your finger at someone else, you have three fingers pointed at yourself."

I must confess I am concerned about the four hearings, and the four separate legislative procedures that are involved each year in the aid program, as time consuming for everybody concerned, including those of us in the executive branch. We were hopeful that we could find a way in which to use that time perhaps to somewhat better advantage.

But this is something we will be dicussing further with the committee. We would hope that you would take into full account the suggestions we make on this subject, and I am sure that the committee will give these its fullest consideration. It would not be for me to try to tell the Congress how to organize its own work. 

I do feel there are a good many things which could be usefully looked into which the time of you gentlemen here perhaps would not at present permit-and in considerable depth. I think there are things in the aid field, comparable, for example, to the problems affecting China, which a subcommittee of this committee is working on, and has been for some time, in considerable depth. 

I would hope some of those might be considered as alternatives to the present procedure. 

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. Would you care to comment on the reason why you included no ceiling in the authorization? Why is it advisable to bypass this committee with respect to the amounts that you might request?

Secretary RUSK. In some cases in the southeast Asia field, for example we don't yet have a sense of what the requirements might be, and in fact we are not asking for actual appropriations. In some cases there are presently ceilings, and our actual requests are substantially below them. For example, in development loans, the ceiling is $1,200 million. We are asking for appropriations for the next fiscal year of $665 million.

In the case of the contingency fund it would be somewhat difficult, I think, to establish, under a shifting world situation, a policy ceiling over a long-term period on a matter that would have to vary somewhat year by year. In the case of Vietnam it is difficult to anticipate what the ceiling there might be. 

Technical assistance: I think we ought to keep open the doors to our institutions here. The land-grant college system has much to contribute in this field. The orders of magnitude of funds are relatively small compared to the total program. I would suppose that these are matters in which the amounts involved would be a part of that annual review which we would anticipate this committee would hold, in any event, as to the aid program, and that they would be taken up finally at the appropriation stage. 

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. In connection with the contingency fund, why, if the world situation is continuing to change so rapidly, are you only asking for an authorization of $70 million when you expect to need $239 million this year?

Mr. BELL. We are asking as part of the 5-year authorization for a ceiling of $150 million a year during the 5-year period. In fiscal 1967 we are asking for only $70 million in new appropriations. 

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. Why is that? 

Mr. BELL. We think we have taken into account the very large increase in requirements for Vietnam in the supporting assistance appropriation itself. It is true that any figure for the contingency fund is necessarily an arbitrary one.

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. I fail to understand, if you need $100 million more for only a few months of this year for the contingency fund, why you anticipate you will only need $70 million for 12 months next year.

Mr. BELL. I think I said in an earlier hearing the $100 million we have asked for, for the remainder of this year we may not have to use. 

If we don't, it will be offset against the next year's appropriation request.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Hays. 

Mr. HAYS. Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

Mr. Secretary, one of the things I have always liked about you is the fact that you read history. You are not being very pragmatic or realistic when you are asking for a 5-year authorization from this committee. What you are really doing, you are asking us to bypass the Congress that is going to be elected in 1966 and the Congress that is going to be elected in 1968.

If there is any validity in the fact that Congressmen only have 2-year terms because they have to be close to the people, you are flying in the face of this validity, aren't you?

Secretary RUSK. We have had multiyear authorizations in some instances development loans and the Alliance for Progress. We

69% thought there might be some advantage in applying that same principle to some of these other areas of assistance. 

Mr. HAYS. I think if you got a 2-year authorization that would be about as much as you could hope for. I don't want you to be too disappointed. I am on your side. I think you are doing a good job. 

Secretary RUSK. I think you are reading a lot more history than the critics are reading, as far as that is concerned. 

Mr. HAYS. I have one problem that bothered me yesterday.

Mr. Bell and I waltzed around it a little, but didn't really come to grips with it. That is this business of India and Pakistan. 

When you get into this military book, so much is marked either "confidential" or "secret," you hardly know whether you can ask a question or not. But the index is not marked; it has India and 

Pakistan listed. If they are listed, that means they will get some military assistance.

Yesterday I pointed out to Mr. Bell we have more than doubled the amount of nonmilitary assistance you are going to give them. I can't say how much you have gone up on the military, but it is considerable. 

The question I asked him yesterday was: Was this a reward for going to war with each other, or a bribe not to do it any more? I think the American people are going to ask us a lot of questions about this. 

I wonder if in public session you could give some kind of reassurance as to that? The argument was made yesterday that we have to feed starving people. When you give them military assistance, you wonder if you are keeping them from starving so they can be around to use the weapons. 

Just how do these things mesh together? 

Secretary RUSK. Mr. Hays, in the first place no decisions have been taken with respect to military assistance under the program that is in front of you. These were illustrative figures developed some time ago on certain assumptions. These matters will require very considerable thought here as well as discussions with the two governments concerned and careful attention to the development of the situation in that part of the world.

There is the problem of China along the northern frontier. There are some other reasons which I have alluded to in executive session that affect the Pakistan side. I can assure you very definitely, Mr.

Hays, that we are not going to finance two sides of a war if these two nations insist on fighting each other. We are not going to do it. 

Whether or not there would be military assistance for India and Pakistan under this bill is still something to be considered further in our own executive branch, and with the committees, and indeed in very careful discussions with the two governments concerned. 

Mr. HAYS. Mr. Secretary, I think you are absolutely sincere in saying that we are not going to finance two sides of a war. 

But we did finance two sides of a war with these two countries previously and they didn't quit until they ran out of military equipment. we shut it off and they couldn't get any more, and they ultimately ran out and they had to quit. True,

What assurances do we have if we go back into this business of supplying them weapons that they will use them against China if the e necessity arises, and that they won't use them against each other?

That is the thing that troubles me, not only with India and Pakistan, but with all the Latin American countries as well. 

We have given them arms for years, and they have only used them against each other and their own people. 

Secretary RUSK. In the case of these two countries, sir, you have put your finger on the most important point involved. Quite frankly, I can't give you a categoric answer on that except to say that, when the fighting broke out between India and Pakistan, the unanimity of the Security Council and the combined influence of the principal members of the Security Council, including the United States, were very helpful factors in bringing these two countries to a peaceful basis, opening the way for the armistice, the withdrawal of troops, and other measures that have been taken.

I do believe also that this year has been a somewhat sobering influence for both sides there. I am encouraged to believe that the decisions, the agreements reached at Tashkent, will move some of these dangers into the background.

But these are very far-reaching questions, and I think it would be difficult for me to expand upon them more than I have already, partly because the situation does have to be watched from many points of view, and further discussions with everyone concerned will have to take place..

We are not at the moment planning-we have not taken a decision to utilize the funds that we have used for illustrative purposes in our presentation. 

Mr. HAYS. Mr. Secretary, I will say something perhaps that you can't.

It seems to me India had a great reservoir of good will in this country. Nobody quarreled with aid to India. This was something that America had a long tradition of in the private sector before we got into the business of the Government giving things away, but I would say they certainly dissipated a lot of that reservoir of good will when the war occurred. I would think for their own edification about one more instance and they would find themselves at a dry well. 

You don't need to comment on that if you don't want to.

Secretary RUSK. I think from the point of view of the interests of the American people and the United States, looked at solely from the point of view of our own national interest, there is an enormous difference to us between a subcontinent in which these two great nations live peacefully and quietly with each other, a subcontinent which would be secure, perhaps impregnable, if they were working closely together and in which social and economic development could proceed on the basis of cooperation between the two and on the basis of certain serenity. That is one possibility. But another is a subcontinent where they would be engaged in conflict with each other. 

It is not just a case of our meddling in someone else's affairs. This is an American interest that we feel we have a right to represent to the peoples and governments.

I think that came out clearly during the Security Council consideration of this matter. We would hope that, since this is a common

interest of many other countries as well-we are quite sure that these are factors that both sides take fully into account out there. 

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Whalley Mr. WHALLEY. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

Mr. Secretary, many people criticize the United States foreign aid program, believing that the United States is the only country that has such programs.

Can you tell us or supply for the record the other countries that have similar programs, the amount of money involved, the terms of loans and grants? 

Secretary RUSK. I would be glad to submit a full account of that for the record, Mr. Whalley. As you know, we have tried many ways, both bilaterally and through international institutions, to encourage contributions by others. I think we can be encouraged by the fact that total official bilateral commitments of the DAC 

members, the Development Assistance Committee of the OECD 

and commitments from multilateral agencies like the World Bank and EDC and others increased in 1964 by almost 20 percent, although actual disbursements remained at about the same level as in 1963. 

We have also been interested in the terms of such aid-terms of interest rates, grace periods, duration of loans-and we would be very glad to put into the record at this point a full account of what other countries are doing in this respect.

NOTE. The trend to lower interest rates on development loans by certain DAC members is believed to have continued in 1965, most notably in the case of the United Kingdom. In June 1965, the British Government announced a liberalized aid policy under which development loans would be made free of interest in appropriate cases. Since the institution of this policy, a substantial proportion of development loans extended by the United Kingdom have been interest-free. A decrease in the average interest rate on Canadian loans probably occurred in 1965 reflecting the first full year of operation of Canada's soft loan terms introduced in the preceding year.

Mr. WHALLEY. Is it possible for the United States to use U.S.owned local currency for foreign aid?

Secretary RUSK. Within certain limits. We do reinvest substantial amounts of those local currencies in development in the countries where they became available. Perhaps Mr. Bell could comment briefly on the way that works. 

Mr. BELL. Yes, sir. We do use U.S.-owned local currency. of the major ways we do so is through the Cooley loan program, which involves loans to American private businesses investing in the developing countries. The largest case is a fertilizer plant which an 

American firm is investing in India. We made a loan of some $17 million to that company.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Selden. 

Mr. SELDEN. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

Mr. Secretary, we are happy to have you again before the committee and appreciate the clear and articulate statement that you always give us.

While I have certain definite reservations as far as long-term, openend authorizations are concerned, I don't have the same fear as the chairman as far as presenting separate bills on the basis of the economic and military aid being separated. For about 10 years I have tried to get the committee to separate these programs. 

I think both programs should and can stand on their own feet. 

I was interested in your statement of what has taken place in the 

Dominican Republic. I am glad to hear you say that the U.S. forces

1 де יך and later inter-American forces in that country saved perhaps thousands of lives, not only of our nationals and of other nationals, but of the people of the Dominican Republic also. 

I wondered if you would also go so far as to say that the presence of those forces there, first the U.S. and later the inter-American forces, may well have averted a Communist takeover in that country? 

Secretary RUSK. After our troops went ashore for the purpose of protecting foreign nationals in a situation where local authorities could not accept the responsibility and indeed after some of the inter- American forces joined the situation, there were continuing indications that the most extreme elements on the island, particularly some of those in the downtown area, were in fact trying to take advantage of the situation and move the Dominican Republic toward a Castrotype solution.

It is always difficult to persuade some people that a danger which has been averted, actually existed. But I am convinced there was an attempt on the part of these extremist groups to seize control of the situation. The fighting perhaps started out of other motives. But we did hear from representatives of all, shall we say, the democratic groups in the country-all political parties and persuasions who were genuinely committed to democracy and it was perfectly apparent they were very much concerned about what was happening with some of these mobs and groups downtown to whom arms had been issued, or who had found a way to seize arms. 

That concern was expressed right across the political spectrum.

We feel it is better to avert these things where we can than to let them develop into a different kind of crisis situation and that those who believe in free choice and those who would like to see the country move toward a constitutional system should have a chance to give expression to it.

I have seen in the last few days reports of some demonstrations against the inter-American forces there by certain groups. But 

I think we ought to wait and let the Dominican people themselves express their judgment on that situation. I also have the impression that many of the Dominicans-and perhaps a large number of themhave been have become fed up, to put it mildly with the violence and terror which has existed on that island for so long, and that they will feel considerable relief if the OAS effort there does in fact open the way for them to have a more decent kind of government than they have had in decades, and to play the kind of role that a people ought to be able to play in the conduct of their own affairs. 

Mr. SELDEN. Needless to say, I have felt all along that had we not sent forces into the Dominican Republic, there probably would have been a Communist takeover of what may have started only as an attempt to overthrow the existing government. 

One other question, if I have time, Mr. Chairman.

During the executive session, Mr. Secretary, I pointed out that recent attendance at the tricontinent conference in Havana indicated that there was some governments to which we are now giving aid that had at least quasi-official representation at this conference. 

I referred to section 620 (i) of the Foreign Assistance Act which, in effect, states that any country that advocates military aggression

against the United States or against any country which receives U.S. assistance can have its aid terminated. 

I don't know whether you have had a chance to look into this or not. 

I am interested in knowing whether you feel section 620 (i) would cover a situation such as we had at the Havana conference. I understand there probably will be conferences semiannually, perhaps along this same line. If the Foreign Assistance Act does not cover this situation, I would like to know whether or not you would be opposed to strengthening this particular section in the foreign aid bill?

Secretary RUSK. Mr. Selden, I have had a chance to take a further look, but not a final look, at the question that you raised. It is an interesting and important question. The delegations to the conference included members from some government parties, but the delegations themselves did not purport to represent their governments. 

I think that the precise issue of 620(i) as presently drafted perhaps does not arise.

Let me go on beyond that to say that we were of course disturbed by some of the resolutions that came out of that conference, and deeply impressed with the immediate and overwhelming reaction of the Latin American countries themselves, on their own initiatives, to make it clear that they were not going to have any part of efforts to penetrate them through subversion or use of violence in Latin America.

I can assure you that if we find any of the governments to whom we are furnishing aid supporting or contributing or taking part in these subversive efforts by violence and intimidation in Latin America, this will bring about an immediate review by us of a good many relationships with such countries, because, as you well know, from your own association with the inter-American system for many years and as a participant in our conferences at Punta del Este in 1962 and onward, there is going to be solidarity on these issues and the United States will express itself that way. 

Mr. SELDEN. I understand the tricontinent conference has been invited to meet in Cairo next year or the next, at the invitation of 

Mr. Nasser. He sent a message of greetings to the conference and invited them to meet next in the United Arab Republic. 

I feel we should tighten up section 620(i) of the bill if developments of this nature continue.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Gross. 

Mr. GROSS. Mr. Secretary, I join with Mr. Frelinghuysen in being almost overwhelmed by your solicitude for this committee by virtue of your request for a 5-year program which would eliminate annual review by this committee.

How did you arrive at the $917 million for military assistance in this bill with Pakistan and India listed for this assistance and yet you express some doubt that either country would be included? 

Where do you expect to get the funds for Pakistan and India based upon the reservation just expressed, and in view of the $917 million figure in this bill?

Secretary RUSK. The program, as spelled out in the confidential presentations, is an illustrative program. We tried over a period of some months to estimate what the requirements might be. On that

particular question, Mr. Gross, beyond what I have already said this morning, I do think that it ought to be discussed further in closed session. I am a little handicapped in going into that in open session. 

Mr. GROSS. I wonder how you arrive at a figure of $917 million if you have doubts about the aid and assistance that you are going to give around the world. I will get back to that. 

Secretary RUSK. The $917 million is the total of the illustrative programs that we felt we might under certain conditions like to go ahead with.

Mr. GROSS. As to the 5-year authorization, do you advocate it for all departments of Government, including the Defense Department? 

Secretary RUSK. We have, for example, in the Defense Department a continuing authorization. We have in our normal State Department budget a continuing authorization. 

Mr. BELL. Open ended. 

Mr. GROSS. Do you or don't you advocate it for all other departments of Government, a 5-year program? A blanket authorization? 

Secretary RUSK. I wouldn't want to speak for the administration as a whole, and all the departments and agencies. That would be for the Bureau of the Budget to get into. I do advocate it for the foreign aid program. 

Mr. GROSS. I am well aware of that. 

Tell me, why is the United States engaged in a boycott of Rhodesia?

I would like to know as a matter of public record. 

Secretary RUSK. The Ian Smith group separated itself, or tried to separate itself, from Britain without having resolved the internal problem of effective_consideration for the some 4 million blacks in that country. The British Government that was responsible for the 

Government of Rhodesia refused to follow the procedures by which many dozens of new members of the United Nations have come into being because Britain felt that they had a responsibility for the interest, the well-being, and the future of all the people of Southern 

Rhodesia, not just the white group.

So from a structural point of view, we have not recognized the 

Ian Smith group as the Government of Southern Rhodesia. The international position of that group is that it is not a legal authority, that Britain continues to have responsibility for it; and in an effort to make that policy clear, Britain, and indeed the overwhelming majority of the United Nations, decided that pressures should be mobilized against the Ian Smith group until they found a much better answer than they had come up with.

Mr. GROSS. We took unilateral action to boycott as far as this country was concerned. That was not the United Nations. 

Secretary RUSK. It is a policy in which most countries have joined. 

In this context we took action; and we acted with respect to our own economic relationships with Southern Rhodesia. 

Mr. GROSS. Are there not thousands of whites in various countries of Africa? 

Secretary RUSK. Yes, sir. 

Mr. GROSS. Do you know of any white officials in any of those governments, and if so please identify them for me? 

Secretary RUSK. I would be glad to submit some material on that for the record. 794

For longer than this country has been in existence, is that not true? 

Secretary RUSK. Yes, sir.. 

Mr. GROSS. How many whites are recognized in that Government?

Secretary RUSK. In most of these African states, places like Kenya, Zambia, white people do live. Those who are settled citizens enjoy the usual rights of citizenship. Most of them are there engaged in business or mining or in teaching or activities of that sort. 

There are numbers still in the civil service of those countries. An effort has been made in most of the black African countries to make people of other races feel at home, to give them all the usual opportunities. I do not myself think, Mr. Gross, the situation in Southern 

Rhodesia can be properly described as stable. Here you have a group of about 223,000 members of the white community in Southern

Rhodesia surrounded by 4 million blacks in that country, and beyond that surrounded by a hostile continent. I just find it hard- 

Mr. GROSS. Mr. Rusk and I am sure you know it there are more seats allocated to African Negroes in the Rhodesian Parliament than there are in the British Parliament and there are more Negroes in the Rhodesian Parliament than in the U.S. Congress. 

Don't you think we ought to solve our own racial problems in this country before we start sticking our long noses into the racial problems in Rhodesia? Is there no place where we can stay out of this business? 

Secretary RUSK. I think, Mr. Gross, in this mid-20th century we must move as rapidly as possible to solve all these problems we have remaining in our own society. 

Mr. GROSS. Is their trouble the fact that they have not been on the foreign aid gravy train?

Secretary RUSK. That is not their problem. This problem was taken up last November in the United Nations Security Council.

At that meeting the Council adopted a resolution calling upon all states to refrain from-to desist from-sending arms and equipment and military material and to do their utmost to break all economic relations with Southern Rhodesia, including an embargo on oil and petroleum products.

This is a resolution with which practically all members of the 

United Nations are complying.

Mr. GROSS. In ordering this boycott you were in violation of the 

U.S. Constitution, and you know it, which gives to Congress sole authority to regulate foreign trade-

(The information follows:) EXECUTIVE AUTHORITY TO TAKE MEASURES AGAINST SOUTHERN RHODESIA DESCRIPTION OF MEASURES TAKEN 

On November 11, 1965, the Smith regime in Southern Rhodesia declared unilaterally its independence from the United Kingdom and purported to establish a new state of Rhodesia. The British Government considered this action unconstitutional and as having no legal validity. It took steps in accordance with its constitutional processes to restore British authority in Southern Rhodesia.

On November 20, the United Nations Security Council met for a second time to consider the situation in Southern Rhodesia. At that meeting, the Council adopted a resolution calling upon all states to refrain from actions that would assist and encourage the illegal regime and "in particular, to desist from providing it with arms, equipment and military materiel, and to do their utmost in order to break all economic relations with Southern Rhodesia, including an embargo on oil and petroleum products." The United Kingdom had, on November 11, banned all exports to Southern Rhodesia of military equipment, including spare parts and all aircraft. On November 12, Ambassador Goldberg announced that the United States would impose an arms embargo in support of the British action and of the Security Council resolution.

On November 20, 1965, the President directed discontinuance of the sugar quota for Southern Rhodesia for 1965, prohibited establishment of a sugar quota for 1966, and directed that the necessary steps be taken to see that no sugar originating in Southern Rhodesia would be imported into the United States thereafter (30 F.R. 15285). 60-929-66-pt. 1- -6

On December 17, the Government of the United Kingdom issued an order in Council making it illegal to supply or deliver, or to agree to supply or deliver, petroleum to Southern Rhodesia. The U.S. Government has at all times recognized the sovereignty and legal authority of the United Kingdom in Southern Rhodesia. Accordingly, our Government recommended that all U.S. companies comply with the British Government's order. In support of the British action, and again following the recommendations of the Security Council, the U.S. 

Government, on December 28, imposed export controls on the shipment of petroleum and petroleum products from the United States to Southern Rhodesia.

On January 20, 1966, the British Government issued a further order, prohibiting the export of chromite from Southern Rhodesia and certain ancillary transactions. 

We again recognized the authority of the United Kingdom in this matter and recommended that U.S. companies comply with the order.

On February 7, Her Majesty's government issued an order prohibiting the export of tobacco from Rhodesia and certain other transactions involving the sale of this year's crop of Rhodesian tobacco. We recommended that U.S. brokers who normally trade this tobacco comply with the order. 

A. Export controls

GOVERNMENT CONTROLS (1) Generally.-Section 3 of the Export Control Act of 1949, as amended, provides that: "To effectuate the policies set forth in section 2 hereof the President may prohibit or curtail the exportation from the United States, its territories, and possessions, of any articles, materials, or supplies, including technical data, except under such rules and regulations as he shall prescribe.' 

Section 2 of that act provides: 

"The Congress hereby declares that it is the policy of the United States to use export controls to the extent necessary. *** (b) to further the foreign policy of the United States and to aid in fulfilling its international responsibilities. ***”’

In the circumstances summarized above, the measures taken by the United States to limit exports to Southern Rhodesia are authorized by the quoted pro visions of the Export Control Act. 

(2) Arms. Section 414 of the Mutual Security Act of 1954, as amended, provides that: "(a) The President is authorized to control, in furtherance of world peace and the security and foreign policy of the United States, the export and import of arms, ammunition, and implements of war, including technical data relating thereto, other than by a U.S. Government agency. 

In the same set of circumstances, a U.S. embargo on shipment of arms to 

Southern Rhodesia is authorized by the quoted provision of section 414 of the 

Mutual Security Act. 

B. Sugar quota

Section 202(d)(1)(B) of the Sugar Act of 1948, as amended by the Sugar Act 

Amendments of 1965, provides that: "Whenever and to the extent that the President finds that the establishment or continuation of a quota or any part thereof for any foreign country would be contrary to the national interest of the United States, such quota or part thereof shall be withheld or suspended, and such importation shall not be permitted.' 

In the circumstances outlined above, the withholding of the 1966 sugar quota, and refusal to permit importation of the 1965 sugar quota, for Southern Rhodesia was authorized by section 202(d) of the Sugar Act. 

REQUESTS FOR VOLUNTARY RESTRAINTS 

The Executive does not need specific legislative authorization to request private cooperation in measures determined to be in the national interest.

Mr. FASCELL. Mr. Secretary, and Mr. Bell, for several years now since I have been on this committee we have worked very diligently and I must say somewhat successfully in recasting the foreign aid program of the United States toward certain basic general lines with which both you and Mr. Bell have cooperated. Among these objectives has been concentration of assistance; a new emphasis on the program; emphasis away from grants, toward loans; the establishment of self-help criteria for recipient countries, and long-range planning.

It seems to me that the agencies, both State and AID, have been extremely responsive to the investigations and committee decisions in reshaping the programs. I am happy to state that under the leadership of both of you that there is a new vibrancy and new force of direction as to policy and administration in the foreign assistance program.

It appears as if I have seen both of you every day in committee since January and also have seen you on television or listening to you on radio or heard you testify and I state with conviction and a deep sense of appreciation that I think the United States of America is extremely fortunate that we have Mr. Dean Rusk as Secretary of 

State and Mr. David Bell as Administrator of AID. 

I want to pursue something that Mr. Bell brought up in discussing some of our international problems. He cautioned that notwithstanding the intensity of our interest in southeast Asia and Vietnam, we are not unmindful of other commitments and problem areas around the world.

We have discussed these in executive session extremely carefully, particularly the problems in South America, which are emphasized as a result of the Communist tricontinent conference which recently took place in Havana.

This raises a question about what the United States can do and what it can't do in achieving its international objectives. We have tried in this committee every legislative action that I can think of. 

I have supported those initiated by others, I have initiated some myself, and I have cosponsored some. We

By legislative action we prohibited any program aid to Communist Cuba. We withheld assistance from any country that was going to offer assistance to Cuba. We authorized an embargo on trade. prohibited assistance to any country which had its ships and airlines transporting goods to and from Cuba. We closed U.S. ports to ships trading with Cuba.

Substantially as a result of our policies the economic situation in Cuba is extremely bad; their international credit is nonexistent. They have had to live, as they want to live, in the Communist orbit from an economic standpoint; this has been costly for the external Communists and less than satisfactory for the Cuban Communist Government.

While free-world shipping has declined, nevertheless one of our strongest allies and friends continues to do a considerable amount of trade. I have recently pointed this out again in some statements I made in the record yesterday.

The purpose of this discussion, of course, is to point out the difficulty which the United States has with this problem and the difficulty in policy and judgment we have. Nevertheless, Mr. Secretary, we have not changed our policy, have we, with respect to the elimination of communism in Cuba; the prevention of the spread of communism from Cuba as an operating base, throughout the rest of the Western 

Hemisphere? Is that correct? 

Secretary RUSK. That is correct, sir.

I would point out that this is not just a U.S. attitude. This is a hemisphere attitude. At the Punta del Este Conference in 1962 the hemisphere, by an overwhelming majority, expressed the view that the hemisphere was prepared to welcome the Cuban people back into the family of the hemisphere under conditions that were compatible with the basic commitments of the hemisphere. This has involved two things: One, the problems created by Cuba's association with the 

Soviet Union; secondly, attempts by Cuba to intervene in the affairs of other countries in the hemisphere. 

In July 1964 the hemisphere applied all of the remaining sanctions against Cuba short of the use of arms. They applied, as far as the hemisphere was concerned, all the available peaceful sanctions against Cuba. They warned Cuba at that time that any further activity of the sort that was then under consideration which was the shipment of arms caches into Venezuela, as you recall-that this kind of activity will not be accepted, and that this could involve the use of force. 

The attitude of the hemisphere on this point is very clear. 

Mr. FASCELL. I appreciate that. 

I am glad to hear that expression from our own Secretary of State.

The difficulty I see with the continuation of our policies is if some of our allies continue to trade with Cuba we are really defeating our own policy. For example, in the case of Great Britain, just on one item alone, which is of course very touchy in Florida since it deals with sugar and molasses--this may be parochial, but it points out what the British are doing in the trade area by buying up the entire 

Cuban molasses crop. Then from non-Cuban sources shipping molasses into the United States at a price below the U.S. domestic price. Thus we have two problems. The trade is contrary to the 

U.S. policy from an international standpoint, and the other is it creates domestic problems within our own industry. You have said, Mr. Secretary, as I have, that perhaps the only answer is an adverse reaction in this country by the consumer. Is it not true, Mr. Secretary, that our Government has made every possible representation to the United Kingdom and other allies to assist us in carrying out our policy regarding Communist Cuba and the Western Hemisphere? 

Secretary RUSK. That is right, sir. In most cases we have had good cooperation. There are some cases, to which you have alluded, where the policies involved and the legislative attitudes involved are different than ours, and we have not been able to proceed. 

Mr. FASCELL. It is vexing and parado: ical while we support the 

United Kingdom in Rhodesia, for which the Secretary this morning is being criticized, and yet we can't get that support from the United Kingdom on Cuba. It might be said that in the case of Rhodesia, the United States had a self-interest and therefore we acted without

real regard to whether the British would have requested us or not. 

I find it extremely difficult to conclude that it is not in the interest of the British people and the rest of the free world to eliminate the Castro Communist government and to stop the spread of communism in Latin America.

Mr. Secretary, on page 3, you say, "The basis for successful foreign aid is self-help." I know that has been our policy, with respect to foreign aid, for years. I refer to section 620 (i). I recognize the reluctance on the part of the executive branch to invoke 620 (i). I sometimes feel that, as a matter of fact, when such a provision is written, the Executive goes to work on how to avoid the effect of the provision during the life of the bill. There are circumstances, sir, with which you and I are both familiar, in which the aggressive military activities of recipient nations is clearly and demonstrably present, preventing them from making the full measure of self-help essential for successful development, but in no case has there been an invocation of section 620(i), and I would imagine no recommendation that it be invoked. May 

I have your comments?

Secretary RUSK. Yes. This 620 (i) is an important provision. Let me say that the most profitable way to utilize it is in advance: that is through the process of negotiation and discussion in our bilateral talks with the particular countries involved. It is somewhat like the 

Hickenlooper amendment on private investment. It is a powerful and helpful part of the legislation. It is most useful if it does not have to be used. An effort is made to apply the policy and to discuss the policy involved before it reaches a point where the clause itself has to be invoked.

I have gone into closed session, three or four instances where this situation has arisen in those terms; where you will recall that we were able to bring our influence to bear rather effectively, stemming out of the policy stated in 620 (i). 

There are some important, shall we say "legal" and technical, problems about its invocation in situations where the United States is reluctant to invoke it or where the issues are confused as to the identity of an aggressor in a particular dispute, where the great pressure is, rather than to find fault, to get the dispute settled. 

So we have some difficulties there about unilateral declarations as to who the aggressor would be.

Mr. MORSE. Mr. Bell and I have had considerable correspondence on that.

They are trying to make it clear that there is an improved atmosphere for private investment, that they are interested in private investment. 

I would like to have a chance to review the letter and perhaps suggest the lines along which one might reply. 

(The information follows:) INVESTMENT POLICY CHANGES BY INDIAN GOVERNMENT 

The answer which might be made to the letter would contain the following: 

In the past few months the Indian Government has become increasingly aware that the investment climate in India must be improved if foreign investors are to substantially increase their investments in India. Constructive steps have been taken. 

1. A private investment office has been established in New York City. 

2. The Indian Government has publicly announced that fertilizer projects affiliated or controlled by foreign parties will be exempt from price control and from restrictions on marketing area for a period of 7 years from the beginning of commercial production, subject to the Indian Government having an option to take up to 30 percent of the production at negotiated prices. This concession applies to projects licensed through March 31, 1967. 

3. The Indian Government has indicated that it would be prepared to permit foreign companies to be responsible for distribution in India of fertilizer produced by them. 

4. The Indian Government has undertaken to allocate foreign exchange to foreign-owned fertilizer companies to permit them to import fertilizer into India for the preparation of markets during the period of construction of their plants.

5. The Indian Government has established a committee of three senior secretaries to the Government to handle all negotiations involving the collaboration of foreign parties in setting up fertilizer projects, to expedite the necessary clearances to help remove bottlenecks which may develop during the construction and production stages. 

6. The Indian Government has given assurance that it will allocate the foreign exchange required by all fertilizer plants to permit operation at capacity levels, including raw materials, spares, and other maintenance items. 

7. The Indian Government has accepted the principle of the need for improvement of credit facilities to the cultivator and has initiated the studies required to bring about the necessary improvements. 

8. Despite strong domestic political opposition, the Indian Government is actively pursuing potential foreign investors and has eased some of the administrative and operating difficulties of the major foreign investor that is now constructing a fertilizer plant in India.

Mr. THOMSON. Mr. Secretary, what was the extent of our involvement in the removal of General Thi as the 1st Corps commander?

Was our CIA involved in that as deeply as we were in the overthrow of Diem? 

Secretary RUSK. No, sir, this was not an involvement of the United

States. The problem there was, as I understood it, that General 

Thi was an effective soldier but there were some problems about his readiness to apply the civilian programs in the north that the Saigon government was very anxious to have applied. 

There seemed to be some difficulty of view between General Thi and the overwhelming majority of the Council of Generals that run the country. And it was apparently agreed in the council, and by 

General Thi, that he ought to withdraw as 1st Corps commander.

We, ourselves, did not have a direct involvement in that issue. 

This was an issue within the group of some dozen generals who are now exercising the responsibility for the country. 

Mr. THOMSON. You are not suggesting that his retirement as 1st 

Corps commander was voluntary, that he agreed to it, are you?

Secretary RUSK. In the meeting of the council he apparently accepted the opinion of the council on this point. He has now gone to the 1st Corps and has apparently indicated to his friends that he would hope they would put aside questions of personality and everybody get together to get on with the main job.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. McDowell. 

Mr. McDOWELL. Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

Mr. Secretary, you have mentioned briefly the growing problem of food in many of the countries of the world. I know there are several pages devoted here in the presentation book to the approach to this problem within the economic aid program. However, I am somewhat concerned that the indication of change in direction to aiding many of these countries to produce more food is rather minimal. I don't see the same change in direction, and I am not referring now to South Vietnam or the adjacent countries there, as far as military assistance is concerned in other areas of the world, I don't see that change in military assistance.

In other words, it seems to me while we talk about this problem I am not sure there is sufficient attention being given in this program or the direction of it over the next 5 years if we have that kind of authorization, and I agree with the chairman that I am receptive, but not convinced yet although I certainly am appreciative of the strong support that you have given in that direction of the program, and Ï certainly am sympathetic to the long hours and tedious time spent up here in the duplicity of testimony before the four committees every year, both for the civilian personnel in the executive department and for the military personnel, but if we are now coming to realize this is a

real problem, the food question, and a situation which I think has become rather startling here at home; it has only been in the last 6 months that we have found out that almost with the exception of wheat, all of our food surpluses have been exhausted-wheat and cotton. I don't believe cotton is going to be any great answer to feeding starving people.

What further emphasis can we put upon this area? Can we not do more in developing a stronger program here with more money, because you are not going to build fertilizer plants, which is the greatest need they have in most of these countries, to increase their supply of food, unless we give that emphasis. 

Secretary RUSK. I would like to comment briefly on that myself, Mr. McDowell.

We are really in the beginnings of a major new period in our own approach to these food problems, for two reasons. On the one side, as you point out, our own surpluses are coming into balance with what might be called our own normal needs for domestic consumption, a reasonable reserve and commercial opportunity for sales abroad, plus our share of participation in food assistance abroad. 

We are moving out of the period of the psychology of what one of our advisers called "the garbage can psychology.' We had a vast surplus here that was going to spoil if we didn't get rid of it, and we felt we should move it out to those who could use it. 

We are moving into a period where we will have to consider what resources we ourselves, in 100-cent dollars, are going to invest in our agricultural productivity to permit us to play the part we ought to play in this emerging food situation we face in the next decade. That is one factor.

We are talking now not about a surplus which people took for granted outside some of them thought perhaps they were doing us a favor in receiving them because they were a burden to us. 

There has been a most intensive look at the prospect over the next decade in the food situation. Even if we were to bring back into production the 60 million acres that have been taken out of wheat production, for example, we could not meet the needs, we could not fill the gap between need and supply, at the end of, by say, 1975. 

This creates a very important problem that everybody has to work on. We do believe, that because of the food situation and also because of the necessity for building a solid platform for development, our AID program ought to shift its emphasis more toward agricultural production, health, and education.

It was the marriage of these three elements that made it possible for us to move with dramatic speed just after the turn of this century in many parts of our own country which were then genuinely underdeveloped. I think the situation is the same in other countries. 

I can assure you that there is no intention here to play down what can properly and usefully be done in helping other countries to expand their agricultural production. I can assure you we are prepared to be most insistent that, unless everyone goes almost flat out, the food problems of the world are not going to be met, and that they must forget about the possiblity of the United States standing alone at the end of day to come to their aid and solve that problem for them.

Unless they go flat out, there is no prospect that we can solve it by an increased effort on our side. We want to get into this an element of discipline, of help, a frank look at the emerging situation over the next decade, to let everyone understand that their and our resources have to start dealing with this situation or we won't find an answer, and the age-old answer of famine and disease and war will be on us.

Washington, D.C.

2172, Rayburn Building, the Honorable Thomas E. Morgan (chairman) presiding.

The committee meets this morning in executive session for a continuation of the hearings on the pending foreign aid legislation, H.R. 

12449 and H.R. 12450.
