STATEMENT OF HON. RAYMOND A. HARE, ASSISTANT SECRETARY OF STATE FOR NEAR EAST AND SOUTH ASIAN AFFAIRS 

Mr. HARE. Mr. Chairman, if it meets with your permission, I would like to submit the formal statement for the record and expand it informally. 

Chairman MORGAN. Without objection, it is so ordered. 

(The statement of Mr. Hare is as follows:) STATEMENT OF HON. RAYMOND A. HARE, ASSISTANT SECRETARY OF STATE FOR NEAR EASTERN AND SOUTH ASIAN AFFAIRS 

Mr. Chairman and members of the committee, (1) I am pleased to have this opportunity to discuss with you the proposals for the 1967 foreign economic assistance programs. It so happens that I have had a certain amount of firsthand experience in these matters-most recently as Ambassador in Turkey, and out of that experience has come a conviction that our assistance programs are an important and effective element for the achievement of our foreign policy objectives. (2) In compliance with the desires of the Congress and the President we have carefully reexamined our requirements in the area and the extent to which the individual nations are making genuine efforts on their behalf. The program proposals have been modified to meet new circumstances, to emphasize and accelerate self-help, and to focus attention on agriculture, health, and education. 91 w.www

As they now stand, they are designed as the minimum consistent with the achievement of our objectives in the Near East and south Asia. (3) There has been no basic change during the past year in the great significance of the area to us or in the nature of the problems with which we must grapple. Most of the states of the area are underdeveloped, subject to internal stresses and strains, threatened by domestic disruptive forces, and, in some cases by external power. They very much want for themselves true national independence and the modernization that will meet the reasonable expectations of their people. Their basic objectives are generally, but not necessarily always, consistent with ours.

The question of the bearing of recent events on the achievement of these objectives can be best introduced through a summary review of the three subregions of the area. I turn first to south Asia, since there have been significant and dramatic developments in that region, and since a large part of our assistance expenditures are made there.

SOUTH ASIA 

(4) South Asia is the heartland of non-Communist mainland Asia. The will and determination of the peoples of this region to withstand the pressures from Communist China will, in the long run, bear decisively on the question of whether Communist China can be contained and brought to respect the dictates of international law and society. We should not let this view of the importance of the region be obscured. (5) The war last fall between India and Pakistan was a tragic experience for us as well as for the belligerents, but like many tragedies, this one has been followed by constructive action. The participants seem now to have a new and sober appreciation of the fact that peace on the subcontinent is essential if their universally felt aspirations for national security and a better life are to be achieved. Even the Soviet Union has found it has served its interests to play a role of moderation through the initiative which it took at Tashkent. (6) On our side, we, too, have felt the necessity of carefully reviewing our policy with respect to the subcontinent. In so doing, we have concluded that future aid to India and Pakistan must be related rather directly to progress toward securing the peace between them, since without peace, economic development is not possible, and, without economic development, stability is uncertain. 

We have made this quite clear to both India and Pakistan. As you know, we suspended all military aid and sales deliveries to both countries when the fighting broke out last fall. Although we have recently relaxed our policies slightly on sales of limited and selected nonlethal military items, our embargo is otherwise still in effect. (7) Our recently indicated willingness to negotiate commodity loans of $50 million for Pakistan and $100 million for India reflects satisfaction with the progress made at the Tashkent Conference and thereafter. We trust that a continuing process of reconciliation will permit the flow of free world aid resources to build up once again to the level required to provide the critical margin between between stagnation and progress. (8) Our future economic aid to the subcontinent, as to other recipients, will continue to be clearly contingent on the readiness of the recipients to undertake those measures of self-help which experience has shown are necessary if our assistance is to achieve its agreed objectives. We have had encouraging signs that a large measure of underlying agreement has developed amongst us as to what these measures should include. The new-found determination of India's 

Government to overcome its food deficit problem and its dependence on U.S. 

Public Law 480 wheat shipments is an outstanding case in point. While drought and war set back the economy in 1965-66; the Indian Government appears determined to do the necessary trimming and to make the necessary policy adjustments to attract more foreign private capital and improve its general economic performance. With our help, coupled with that of others, India should be able to make substantial economic progress and maintain an influential stake for free world interests in the Asian struggle against Chinese communism. (9) Pakistan's good economic performance is a matter of record. In the 

5-year plan which ended last year, sound planning and increasing reliance on private initiative and market forces helped the economy to achieve impressive progress in all key sectors, and the GNP growth rate exceeded 5 percent. Like

India, unfortunately, Pakistan has suffered the effects of drought and war during the past year. (10) India and Pakistan have many essential assets, but they do not have the necessary capital. We would hope that our help, friendship, and encourage ment may serve to assist them in promoting their objectives of economic selfsufficiency and internal and external stability. We would hope that this would also contribute to a more stable power balance in Asia. That is our objective. 

I believe it is attainable. And I believe it is easily important enough, it terms of our interests in the 1970's and 1980's as we see them now to justify a substantial commitment of U.S. resources in support of the self-help efforts of India and 

Pakistan themselves.

GREECE, TURKEY, IRAN, AND CYPRUS 

(11) Greece, Turkey, and Iran, associated with the West in the NATO and CENTO alliances, continue to provide a stable political and military corridor that is significant for the entire area. With U.S. assistance and with increasing efforts on their part, each of these countries has stood firm against Communist attempts at direct or indirect penetration, and each is making renewed efforts to strengthen its political, social, and economic structure. (12) In Iran, the Shah and his government continue their wide ranging efforts to reform land holding, increase literacy, improve local government, and otherwise strengthen and modernize the social fabric. In addition, with increasing American private investment, the country is intensifying its efforts to modernize its economy and make it more productive. (13) In Greece, a lengthy political crisis accelerated the economic decline that had become evident early in 1965; thanks, however, to courageous self-help measures by the Government and to external assistance, especially food and feed grains from the United States, the downward economic trend has been arrested. (14) Free national elections in Turkey have brought a pragmatic, development-minded government into power with a substantial majority. Despite the many problems facing it, we are confident that Turkey should be able to continue its progress toward economic and social modernization in accord with its development plan and within a democratic framework. 

Although the Cyprus problem has had unfortunate reprecussions in Turkey's relations with Greece and also given rise to discussion of broader foreign policy implications, Turkey remains solidly within NATO and our own working relationship remains close and firm. (15) The Cyprus situation itself is fortunately more quiet but the basic problem remains unresolved. We continue to support efforts by the United Nations and by the parties themselves to work the way toward a lasting solution to this serious problem.

NEAR EAST 

(16) Most states of the Near East have continued to make economic and social progress despite the political tensions that surround and often embroil them.

It is a restless area, sensitive to the stirrings of Arab nationalism and still caught up politically, economically, and emotionally in the persistent Arab-Israel dispute. This tangled matter continues to defy settlement. The past year witnessed no serious increases in tension or outbreaks of violence between the parties, however, and most states of the area are increasingly preoccupied with developmental and other internal problems. At the same time much attention remains focused on the Yemen situation. While there has been no fighting there in recent months, the agreement of last summer between King Faisal and 

President Nasser to settle the problem has yet to be implemented. Another source of concern to which we continue to devote considerable attention is the arms rivalry throughout the Near East. (17) It remains our policy to support the independence and integrity of all states in the area. We continue to work with them in helping bring about the conditions of stability in which an enduring Middle East peace can be achieved and in aiding them to recognize Communist blandishments for what they are. Most of these nations have shown the will and the ability to resist Communist penetration. By supplying selective economic assistance we are making it possible for many Near Eastern countries to register real economic growth and thereby meet the aspirations of their people for greater opportunities and .a higher standard of living. 60-929-66-pt. 1-7

CONCLUSION 

(18) The news headlines of the year have impressed upon us the fact that in some countries the search for modernization, national strength, and international peace has been thwarted or diverted by continuing political instability, as in Yemen or Syria; by violent international antagonisms, as in the Indian subcontinent; or by food shortages, as in India and the United Arab Republic. Press and political leaders in some countries have ben critical of the United States, and several have taken steps to "normalize" their relations with the Communist world. In charting our future course in the Near East and south Asia, we should view these manifestations-irritating, burdensome, sometimes tragic-in conjunction with other underlying circumstances. The threat of direct Communist aggression has tended to recede somewhat; this, seen short-term, is reassuring. What is not reassuring, however, is that nothing has occurred to give reason to believe that there has been any real change in Communist objectives. The tragic India-Pakistan conflict brought the populous south Asian area to the edge of holocaust, but both countries drew back, and they now appear to be committed to a peaceful search for solutions to their differences. India, faced with a food crisis, is taking radical remedial steps. Though the headlines out of the area have emphasized war, coups, disorder, and poverty, it is significant that many of the states, embracing most of the region's population and resources, have recently effected peaceful transfers of political power. 

Throughout the area the long slow process of education and institution building goes on. The experiences of the year may have brought a new sense of realism: a disillusionment with simple solutions arrived at through war or ideological "movements," a new awareness of the need to respect facts, to exercise patience and restraint; a new willingness to turn inward to the hard tasks of self-help in development; and a new realization of the constancy of American purpose. 

19. When there is violence and instability in the Middle East and south Asia, the threat extends to ourselves as well as to our friends in the area; and our resources as well as theirs are diverted. With peace and increasing stability they, with our assistance, can concentrate upon the building of independent, self-sustaining democratic societies. At this juncture our assistancethough only a fraction of the total country investment-is frequently the crucial element in the maintenance of order, the building of infrastructure for production, or the development of basic human resources. 

Mr. HARE. Mr. Chairman, with the committee's permission, I hope,

I would like to make a few comments about the facts in the situation in our area at the present time. Since this is the first time I have had the occasion to appear before your committee, I naturally looked at some of the testimony of my predecessors and I found that they had very carefully brought out the strategic significance of our area and that type of subject.

With your permission this morning I would like to run briefly over the facts of the situation, the countries with which you are dealing, but before that I would like to say also a word or two about aid itself. 

Even in so doing, I may poach a little on the preserves of my good friend, Mr. Macomber.

Mr. Chairman, I began to be associated with aid when I was Ambassador in Saudi Arabia in 1951 when the Mutual Security Act was passed. Since that time I have had missions in Beirut, Cairo, and 

Turkey, where I just came from. I would like to say briefly that I believe I say it not only as an observer but also as one responsible as part of the country team-I have seen a great improvement in AID. 

It was an experimental organization. It has now become highly institutionalized.

Sometimes organizations become too highly institutionalized. Certain trends I have seen, Mr. Chairman, are these:

D. er I have seen AID develop away from any concept of being a giveaway organization-the giveaway concept has never worked-it is soporific, not a stimulant. It does not stimulate good will but ill will. 

It is wasteful. It is disillusioning. 

Fortunately I have been able to see change in the period I have been observing and working with the aid programs. There are certain changes I would like to mention. Aid is being presented clearly now as complementing and not supplementing local effort. 

In other words, the emphasis is put on self-help. A few years ago you talked about self-help and it was considered delicate--now this is done straightforwardly and without mincing any words. 

The second thing is that there is emphasis now, and important emphasis I believe, on the quality of aid programs. It is a very dangerous thing, I believe, surely unsatisfactory and surely unproductive, to have aid programs that have no terminal date. You get into charity then, rather than assistance.

Another thing is that aid is fitting into clearer planning. That is something we didn't think so much about at the beginning. Also aid is targeted more and more on specific objects, specific objectives. 

It is no longer a blunderbuss operation and, to use the terminology that they use in the nuclear sphere, AID is no longer a proliferating agency.

Also, there is less grant and more loan. The military and economic are related more carefully. We are also emphasizing the fact that this is not just an American responsibility but others should join in, particularly others we have helped.

Finally, we are directing more and more attention to human resources as well as material resources. These comments, Mr. Chairman, are not meant to be the comments of an expert on aid such as my friend Bill Macomber, but rather comments by somebody who has had to work with it and had to be responsible for heading country teams in coordinating aid programs.

If I might go back and say a few words about the area for which 

I am responsible. Greece, Turkey, and Iran are in the northern part of the Near East. They have certain things in common in the light of the fact that they are all what I call "loners" in the field of foreign affairs. They have no brothers and sisters, internationally speaking. 

Mrs. BOLTON. What did you call them?

Mr. HARE. "Loners." The fact that they are loners naturally makes them look for some form of support since all three of these countries have been historically menaced from the north. In my own time, since the war, I participated in these incidents. We have had the threats against Iran when the Soviet troops refused to leave after the 

Second World War. And then the threats against Turkey and Greece, which brought on the Truman doctrine. 

This has led these countries to be interested therefore in foreign support. They don't want to stand alone. It has led Greece and 

Turkey into NATO. It has led Turkey and Iran into CENTO, and it has led both them to feel they have important interests bilaterally with this country. [Security deletion.] 

Our relationship is firm. This was brought out recently in the case of NATO and as a consequence of Mr. de Gaulle's initiatives where both Greece and Turkey rallied around us very well.

I jump to the Near East. By that I mean the Arab countries to the south, and here I would speak in terms of generalization. Once again, here, you have several tides running. One is a tide of the so-called progressives. These progressives are the United Arab Republic, Syria, and Iraq with their policy of Arab socialism. Another group is composed of Lebanon, Jordan, Saudi Arabia, which tend to be following more traditional lines. There have been and there are differences, political and otherwise, between these two groups that come out from time to time.

They have held various Arab conferences recently in an attempt to resolve these difficulties. Now there is a certain surface agreement between them, but actually there is a basic difference. I would make this comment also that in these more conservative countries, so-called, the curious thing is that they tend to have done better than the progressives in their social and economic betterment. The others have made a great to do about social and economical betterment, but somehow those who are more traditional seem to have built more slowly, but built well.

At the present time the Near East is torn by the old problem of the Israel-Arab confrontation, but it is now being torn also, and I would invite your attention to this, by another problem, that of Yemen, which arose from the coup there several years ago. The people who promoted the coup felt they were in a rather weak position and called in the United Arab Republic. This developed into a confrontation between Saudi Arabia and the United Arab Republic which now goes on and which troubles us a great deal. We have exerted the best efforts we can to stimulate these two countries to talk this thing out, because there is no question but that a hostile confrontation between these two countries would be disastrous. 

Finally, and broad brushing very, very much, I would like to sketch the trend of what has happened in our relations with south Asia. 

Some years ago I took over the Division of South Asian Affairs-it was then called Middle East and India. That was in 1947. That was just prior to the catastrophe which took place prior to the separation of India and Pakistan. Up to that time there had been a sentimental attitude in this country toward India. She was called Mother India. 

The whole attitude was, I remember, quite a sentimental one. 

As a matter of fact, this was also translated into the political sphere in that we, as a nation, did support the concept of Indian independence. 

After the catastrophe of 1947 and the atrocities which were attendant to that, we had another problem. That was the emergence of the 

Kashmir problem, which is still with us. Here we moved in as a peacemaker. We were one of the members of the Five-Power Commission, and later on, a one-man affair, Dr. Graham took that over, I think he still, in a titular way, holds that position. 

Here we were still thinking of the Indian subcontinent in a restricted sense at that time. First it was India, per se, India with which we sympathized. Then India was partitioned. Our natural human instincts were to try to help people who had this problem, the India- Pakistan problem. But in 1949 there was a new dimension added to this. And I think the date of 1949 is important, because on that date

Gandhi died and Nehru inherited his mantle. And in that year, if I

am correct, the Chinese Communists took over in China. This ushered in a new period in our relations with the subcontinent, because for a period of almost 10 years Mr. Nehru, in his role of head of the Afro- 

Asian emerging countries, seemed to regard the emergence of the Chinese Communists as falling in that sphere. 

There was a period there, quite a long period, during which India and China were quite close [security deletion]. However, during this period certain difficulties began emerging. 

Apparently, there were certain maps that the Chinese had of this area which Mr. Nehru happened to see on his several trips to Peking. 

He said these maps included areas that India considered as belonging to India. This was made light of for a period of time. 

Then it became clear after a time that these so-called incorrectly. delineated maps were in fact an indication of a very serious problem. 

That problem was really the frontier, and eventually the relationship between India and China. That developed into trouble in 1958, and in 

1962 it developed into the invasion of India. 

The year 1962 marked another change in our relations. I think this is very fundamental, Mr. Chairman. It is sometimes a thing that is difficult to understand when we note the attitudes of India and Pakistan. The thing that happened in 1962, when China attacked 

India, the important change that took place there was that at that time the United States and the United Kingdom indicated support of India. This, to the Pakistanis, changed the power balance, because they were apprehensive primarily of India by this time. Earlier, I think they had been sincerely concerned about the threat from the north, but by 1962 they had become more concerned and increasingly concerned by the threat, as they saw it, from India. 

By the attack of China and by United States and United Kingdom coming to the assistance of India they saw the power balance changing. [Security deletion] unfortunately they looked to the Chinese as this counterbalancing factor. [Security deletion] this brings me to the question of the Indian side, because the Indians, feeling that they need support against the Chinese Communists, feel that they must have good relations with the Soviet Union. [Security deletion.] 

As a consequence, [security deletion] there tends to be a certain neutrality as between the United States and the Soviet Union, as far as 

India is concerned [security deletion]. 

That is a bit of broad-brushing of the general picture. I would stop there. 

Chairman MORGAN. Thank you, Mr. Secretary. 

Mr. Macomber.

STATEMENT OF HON. WILLIAM B. MACOMBER, JR., ASSISTANT ADMINISTRATOR FOR NEAR EAST AND SOUTH ASIA, AGENCY FOR INTERNATIONAL DEVELOPMENT 

Mr. MACOMBER. Mr. Chairman, I have a formal statement which I would like to submit for the record, and make a few informal remarks, if I may. 

Chairman MORGAN. Without objection, it is so ordered.

(The prepared statement of Mr. Macomber is as follows:) 

2 

STATEMENT OF HON. WILLIAM B. MACOMBER, JR., ASSISTANT ADMINISTRATOR, BUREAU FOR NEAR EAST AND SOUTH ASIA 

Mr. Chairman and members of the committee, I am very pleased to be here today and to have this opportunity to discuss the economic aid request for the Near East and south Asia region.

In this region there are over 775 million people-1 out of every 3 who live in the free world. Population is growing at an average rate of 22 percent a year, slowing the growth of per capita income which still averages less than $90 annually in the Asian subcontinent. Skills and literacy in most of the area remain inadequate for dynamic economic and social growth. While threequarters of the population are dependent on the soil, agricultural production has generally failed to advance sufficiently in the face of rising domestic demand.

The key to our aid effort in the Near East and south Asia region is "concentration." Our aid effort is concentrated in a relatively few of the 18 countries in the region, where the need is great, the environment fosters development, and the country's own self-help effort deserves support from the free world. 

Ninety percent of the region's funds are programed for India, Pakistan, and Turkey; 98 percent for these countries plus Afghanistan and Jordan. Small programs are planned for Yemen, Nepal, the United Arab Republic, Iran, and Cyprus. The proposed program is 90-percent loan repayable in dollars and tied to U.S. procurement. It is more than matched in this region by the economic aid programs of other donors and international organizations. 

I said a moment ago that an environment must exist which will permit development to occur. In the past year, as you know, hostilities erupted between 

India and Pakistan. The war, though brief, wasted resources needed for development and slowed economic progress in both countries. Pakistan lost much of the momentum of its import liberalization effort which had stimulated private sector growth the year before. Retrenchment was necessary to conserve scarce foreign exchange for the war effort. The scarcity of foreign materials slowed 

Indian growth and a severe drought compounded its economic problems. All this has served to underscore the urgent need for India and Pakistan to find ways to avoid further confrontations which so inevitably and tragically divert energies and resources from the essential tasks of feeding their people and developing their economics.

The steps taken by both countries since the Tashkent agreement to solidify and extend peaceful conditions between them have made it possible for the United States to make limited new loan commitments in both India and Pakistan in fiscal year 1966. For much of the year, new loan commitments were suspended. The proposed fiscal year 1967 programs for these countries will be viewed in the light of the continuing development of peaceful relations on the subcontinent, the knowledge that economic assistance will not result in an arms race, and the expectation of self-help measures, which reassert the priority of economic development and will stimulate and accelerate economic growth. 

We are hopeful that India will do better economically with the resources at its disposal. India has already launched a new attack on its agricultural problems and assigned agriculture first priority in its development program. We are prepared to give intensified assistance in financing fertilizer imports, extending investment guarantees and capital assistance for fertilizer plants, and expending technical assistance to support India's effort to increase agricultural production. 

The Indian Government is examining possible changes in other areas of economic policy, which could lead to more rapid growth. 

Pakistan's experience since 1962, in raising agricultural output, gives promise for other developing countries that important gains can be made in increasing food output. Assisted by U.S. programs to improve the use of water, fertilizer, and other factors, Pakistan's agricultural output increased 12 percent between 1962 and 1965, or about 4 percent a year. A rural works program, reducing unemployment during the slack season, financed by Public Law 480 local currency, has harnessed local initiative in the service of economic development and is helping create democratic local political institutions. Until the disruption caused by the war, Pakistan's overall economic performance was encouraging. The economy was growing at better than 5 percent a year, in part as a result of the

greater reliance placed on private initiative and market forces. Pakistan was financing more of its development program than was planned earlier. 

We are hopeful that conditions will permit us to give renewed support to an economic self-help effort which will reestablish these favorable trends. 

Turkey is also engaged in a major development effort. Gross national product has grown about 6 percent per annum; industrial production has recorded a steady increase of 10 percent a year. Our major economic effort will focus on helping Turkey achieve its own target of eliminating the need for concessional economic assistance by 1973.

In assisting countries in this region, we intend to give increased emphasis to improve their education systems and health programs. The countries must carry the major responsibility for financing the expansion of educational plant and services, but we can help countries achieve qualitative improvements, particularly in technical and vocational education and agricultural education. Our major effort in the field of health is in malaria eradication. Some 500 million people in the Near East and south Asia are now protected by programs AID helped support. Both India and Pakistan use development loans to finance the import of DDT and other equipment. India, after 10 years, is reaching the final stages of its effort. Pakistan is half way in a 10-year program to eradicate this disease. Programs in these countries, as well as others, will be pressed forward to completion.

THE FISCAL YEAR 1967 PROGRAM 

The fiscal year 1967 economic aid request of $799.4 million for the Near East and south Asia region includes funds for 11 countries as compared with 15 countries in fiscal year 1964. As I have noted, development loans account for 90 percent of the economic aid request for fiscal year 1967. 

Development loans. 

Technical assistance. 

Supporting assistance..

Program summary [In millions of dollars] 

Fiscal year Fiscal year 1965 actual 1966 estimate 

Fiscal year 1967 proposed 605. 5 430.0 719.0 49.9 46.0 48.0 38.5 34.9 32.4 693.9 510.9 799.4

The substantial increase over fiscal year 1966 estimated obligations is accounted for by the below normal development lending levels for India and Pakistan due to the suspension of aid during part of fiscal year 1966. 

DEVELOPMENT LOANS 

About 80 percent of AID's total development loan funds are concentrated in the Near East and south Asia countries. India, Pakistan, and Turkey will receive most of the loan assistance.

Program loans in India, Pakistan, and Turkey will finance increased imports of fertilizer, pesticides, and other components needed to raise agricultural productivity. These loans have also proved to be one of the most effective ways of stimulating private industrial growth, by financing essential equipment, raw materials, spare parts, and components needed to produce, for example, trucks, industrial machinery, pumps, and the like. 

Project loans will also be available in fiscal year 1967 to build powerplants, irrigation systems, transportation networks, and other facilities as will specific risk and extended risk investment guarantees for U.S. private investments in fertilizer and other production.

TECHNICAL COOPERATION 

As I have noted in earlier appearances before this committee, the technical cooperation program is an essential complement to our capital assistance, and continues to be most important way of making available to developing countries

U.S. experience, techniques, and ways of teaching, working, and organizing. The needs of developing countries for technical advice and training continue to be urgent. Our proposed program for fiscal year 1967 reflects an increased emphasis on agriculture, education, and health projects. At the same time, it continues the trend of recent years in sharpening and concentrating the focus of our program. While our level of expenditure remains approximately the same, in the 

3-year period, fiscal year 1965-67, we expect to terminate 103 technical assistance projects, while starting 22 new ones. Technical assistance is planned for nine countries, CENTO and under a regional program. Almost 70 percent is concentrated in five nations: Afghanistan, India, Jordan, Pakistan, and Turkey. 

As examples of some of our activities in agriculture, we will support the campaigns of India and Pakistan to produce enough food for growing populations. 

Programs to increase wheat production in Afghanistan will be intensified. Jordan will continue to receive assistance in developing cooperatives, extension services, credit and marketing, and rural development in general. 

In education, India's summer institute program for teaching science and mathematics will be expanded. Vocational-engineering efforts, teacher training, and agricultural and educational program improvements will continue in Afghanistan. 

We will also be looking for ways of improving nutrition, particularly for the young of this region.

More than 2,000 present and potential leaders from the Near East and south Asia countries are being trained in the United States and third countries. These professionals, administrators and technicians, will be returning to positions in government or private enterprise in which they can contribute effectively to national development.

Our technical cooperation program will draw on the experience and competence of both private and public U.S. institutions, including many universities and colleges.

SUPPORTING ASSISTANCE 

The request for supporting assistance for the region continues the downward trend of previous years. At $32.4 million, it now comprises only 4 percent of the total regional request and will be for only two countries: Jordan and Yemen. 

It may be compared with over $300 million of supporting assistance made available in 1960.

Support of Jordan as an independent country continues to be an important element in the U.S. effort to maintain peace in the Near East. Though on a diminishing scale, U.S. budget support continues to be needed to help Jordan meet its ordinary budget expenditures. In Yemen, our program is increasingly oriented toward small-scale urban and rural self-help projects and educational training programs.

The foregoing brief outline is the fiscal year 1967 proposal for assistance in the NESA region for which we are asking your support. I believe it is properly designed to support U.S. security interests in this potentially volatile and critically important portion of the developing world. I will be pleased, of course, to answer any questions you may have on any aspects of the program. 

Mr. MACOMBER. I would like to begin by saying how pleased I am to have this opportunity to renew my association with this committee, Mr. Chairman. It goes back now over a number of years. It is an association I value very much. 

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Macomber, I want to say I know that every member of this committee who had the opportunity to work with you when you were the Assistant Secretary for Congressional Relations welcomes you back. You are very popular on both sides, and I want to say that you have done a good job. 

Mr. MACOMBER. Thank you very much.

I welcome, too, the opportunity to appear before you once again to discuss the economic aid request for the Near East and south Asia region for the coming fiscal year. 

This region, as you know, Mr. Chairman, is of great strategic importance, partly because of its location and its resources, but more

importantly because stretching as it does from Greece to India and the United Arab Republic to Ceylon, its people represent a third of the world's non-Communist population. It is a volatile and impatient, often turbulent, region, split by deep antagonisms, but feeling from one end to the other one deep, common overriding pressure. That pressure, after centuries of suffering and of subsistence existence, is to do something and do something right away about elevating the human condition.

In this struggle for the elevation of the human condition, it is a good measure of the job that is left to be done, and it in fact perhaps gives us a key to understanding some of the frustrations in this area, when we consider that this one-third of the population of the free world has an average annual income of less than $100 a year. In this same region, three-quarters of the people can neither read nor write. 

It is not, of course, the job of the AID program to hand over fully developed economies to these countries. That could not be done. The only way that full economic development can come is over a period of time, through the struggle and self-help of the countries themselves. 

It is, however, essential if these countries are to avoid collapse, chaos, and set themselves up for subversion and Communist penetration, that they have a sense of moving ahead. Some of these countries, to achieve this sense of moving ahead, do require outside assistance, adding to their own efforts technical skills and capital not otherwise available to them. Without this help the countries will not develop this sense of momentum and forward movement. 

This is the job of the United States and other free world aid programs. We must design these programs with this target in mind. 

You have to fight the tendency to try to tidy up the whole world and get all the job done overnight. That is not the objective. The effort is to get a sense of momentum, of forward movement, a sense of progress, and to move countries up to the point where they can take off on their own.

It would be folly, Mr. Chairman and members of this committe, as you well know, to assert that we have learned all the answers in this aid business. We haven't.

As Ambassador Hare has indicated, I think we have learned a good deal over the years, but we have more to learn. It is much too big a task to have learned everything about it. 

Historically speaking we are engaged in an undertaking the likes of which the world has never seen before. Obviously it is still a pioneering effort.

Having said that, I think we can say that we have learned a very great deal as we have gone along. Each year I sincerely believe we have improved these programs. They are a long way from perfect; they are better. They are getting better because we are using the lessons we learned as we struggle with key problems. Certainly we have learned, as Ambassador Hare suggested, that self-help is the key to this operation.

We have learned, Mr. Chairman, to speak up more vigorously on the subject. We are not as diffident as we used to be. 

If I was to name one particular characteristic of last year's aid program I would say it had, more than in any year before, stressed

self-help. We have bargained pretty hard for self-help performance in return for our assistance.

I am glad to say the countries that we deal with do not take great offense at this. They might have in the past. They realize that it is not an effort to be a bully or throw our weight around, or a do-it-ourway-or-else attitude, but it derives from a sincere belief that it is the only way to get the job done. 

Certainly the job will not get done if there is not a matching selfhelp effort on their part.

Another thing we have learned there, Mr. Chairman and members of the committee, is that generally speaking concentrated programs, focused programs, do a much better job than ones that are spread around and get too thin. Thus, we have tried very hard to have "concentration" be the key to the programs in the Near East and south Asia region.

There are 18 countries in this region. If you look at the program we are presenting today, sir, 90 percent of the funds we are requesting will be spent in three countries-India, Pakistan, and Turkey. If you add one country Jordan, it is 95 percent spent in four countries. If you add Afghanistan, you get up to 98 percent.

So we have made a real effort, I think, to concentrate our funds and not get spread too much.

We are requesting $799.4 million for the coming fiscal year. This compares with last year's request which was a range of $771 to $886 million.

Ninety percent of the money we receive will go into loan funds. 

These loans, of course, are to supply the critically needed capital which is not otherwise available. These loans will be tied to U.S. procurement and repayable in dollars.

There are two other important categories, as you all know, of funds which we use in this region. 

The first of these are technical assistance funds. The technical assistance program helps develop technical skills which are essential to a country's being able to move ahead.

The technical assistance program continues to be one of the most important ways of making available to the developing countries U.S. techniques, experience, ways of thinking, working, and organizing. 

Looking at a 3-year period, starting with fiscal year 1965, you will see we spent on technical assistance in that first year $50 million. In fiscal year 1966 we expect to spend $46 million. In fiscal year 1967 we are requesting $48 million.

In other words, the level of technical assistance expenditures is staying about the same over this 3-year span.

As I indicated, we believe very strongly that it is important to concentrate, to focus our program. In no area is it more important than in technical assistance.

I am sorry to say that this may mean often stopping commendable programs. But I have found in my aid work both here and abroad that if you get spread around, you get bogged down, and you don't do as good a job, you are not as effective as if you narrow down what you want to do and mobilize your efforts behind a narrower range of activities.

In this same 3-year period, during which we are basically spending the same amount of money each year, we are terminating 103 technical assistance projects and starting 22. You can see that we are narrowing the number of projects down considerably while still mobilizing the same amount of money and approximately the same numbers of people behind them. 

Mrs. BOLTON. Would you be good enough-discontinuing how many?

Mr. MACOMBER, We have discontinued, are discontinuing, or will discontinue in the coming year 103 projects. In this same period we will have started up 22 new projects. 

I should warn you that these figures are a little misleading. You will find every now and then that some or all of a terminated project will be tucked into a new project. These figures are not absolutely accurate, but basically they are accurate and convey the trend. 

We are not reducing our effort a bit, but are trying to pick out the most important area to work and then mobilize the necessary effort behind those areas. 

The third category is "Supporting assistance." Ambassador Hare has talked about the problem of grant assistance, and has spoken quite accurately of our satisfaction in the way we have been able to move away from that category of aid. We have not entirely completed the job.

It is interesting, however, to note that in 1960 we spent over $300 million in 10 countries in supporting assistance. This coming year we will spend $32.4 million in two countries. One is Yemen and we will spend $2.4 million there. The big user still is Jordan. 

Even here we have some progress to show. When I first went to 

Jordan in 1961 our budget suppport figure was $41 million, and it is coming down to $30 million this coming year. We are encouraged by that situation.

Before closing these remarks, I would like to make a comment on two special situations that I know are of particular interest to this committee.

First of all, the United Arab Republic. Mr. Chairman and members of the committee, the aid situation with respect to the United Arab Republic remains essentially as I described it to you a year ago. You will recall at that time we had had a program of development loans of $40 million in fiscal year 1962, and $44 million in fiscal year 1963. The loans were made in connection with a major effort to improve the diplomatic climate and relations between our countries.

Then for reasons with which you are all familiar, that loan level dropped to zero in 1964, and in 1965. It will be zero again in 1966, and we are not planning at this time any development loan funds for the United Arab Republic for the coming fiscal year. 

We do have a small pipeline that continues on from those earlier loans made back in 1962 and 1963. It is a relatively small pipeline. 

We have a small technical assistance program valued at about $2.2 million.

The major change from last year is a new Public Law 480 agreement that is substantially different than the agreement we had before.

You will recall when we were here a year ago, we were finishing the third year of a 3-year agreement running around $145 million a year. In January we concluded a 6-month agreement valued at about $55 million, with a quarter of it under title IV repayable in dollars. It is quite a different agreement than we had 3 years ago. 

Finally Mr. Chairman, I would like to make a comment about the subcontinent. What happened there last summer is such great tragedy in so many ways. From an aid point of view, our subsequent policy has had to be a very guarded one, and it has been a guarded In fact, all new starts in economic aid in both India and Pakistan were suspended for most of the year, and only recently and only to a limited extent, has this policy been relaxed. one. 

To employ a banker's phrase, the subcontinent, after the explosion of September, has not had what, one might call a "suitable investment climate." 

Recently, however, signs have been more encouraging. We hope very much that as the current trends toward improved Pakistan-India relations continue and as these two countries increasingly shift their thoughts and energies toward decisive and resourceful self-help economic measures, we will be able to renew our development partnership with each. 

Thank you very much, Mr. Chairman. 

Chairman MORGAN. Thank you, Mr. Macomber.

Mr. Macomber, about 24 percent of the total foreign aid this year is contained in your area-$779 million. It is encouraging to see that most of this is to be development loans-$719 million. 

You say in your statement 80 percent of this development loan request goes to three countries, India, Pakistan, and Turkey? 

Mr. MACOMBER. Ninety percent, sir.

Chairman MORGAN. We have often wondered ever since we assumed from the British the burden we have had to provide grant assistance to Jordan. How long is this going to continue? You said that when you went there in 1961 it was $40 million; it was $40 million for several years. It has gradually come down over a period of years. Last year I think it was $32 million, and this year it comes down to $30 million.

Are we going to have to carry this burden in Jordan as long as we have a foreign aid program, or can we get out from under? 

Mr. MACOMBER. We are going to have to carry it for a time. I try to refrain from giving too optimistic reports on Jordan, although I am optimistic about its prospects. The indices of Jordan's economic performance and growth suggest that whereas up to now we have been reducing budget support at a rate of $2 million, $1 million, $3 million a year. We think it can begin to come down at a more rapid rate within a few years. It should take a much shorter time to get down from $30 million to $20 million than from $40 million to $30 million. 

I hope this is going to happen, but these predictions have a way of going askew.

People are beginning to talk about $5 million, $6 million cuts a year, but these things may not go that quickly.

In answer to your question I think we will get out from under the 

Jordan budget support burden. The question is, "How long?"

The Jordanians are driving to be independent of budget support in 11 years. Whether they can or not I don't know. With any luck at all, and good management, they should at least be close enough to being out, so that the burden on us will cease to be anywhere near as heavy as it is now.

One of Jordan's greatest assets is its tourism potential. The Holy 

Land is unique. There are other fascinating antiquity sites which when combined with Jerusalem and Bethlehem will pull in tourist dollars. That is what is going to help get them even. 

Chairman MORGAN. You say in 1964 we supported 15 countries in the area and this year we are only supporting 11 countries in the area. 

What are the four countries that have been eliminated? 

Mr. MACOMBER. Greece and Lebanon, because they have done pretty well; Syria and Iraq for quite different reasons. Those are the four countries that received aid 3 years ago.

Chairman MORGAN. Just two of them 

Mr. MACOMBER. Two graduated and two were dropouts.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Hare, according to the newspapers when 

Vice President Humphrey left Pakistan he indicated the Government of Parkistan recognized the danger of Red China. Right after he left, the Foreign Minister of Pakistan made a public statement to the effect that Pakistan had never regarded Red China as a threat to its own security. Where does Pakistan stand with respect to Red China today, and is our aid policy in any way tied in or dependent upon the attitude of Pakistan as to Red China?

Mr. HARE. As I indicated, perhaps too briefly, when I was talking before, this is a question, you might say, of power as it is exerted in a political sense. 

Originally the Pakistanis saw their main threat from the north, from the Soviets and a secondary threat from India. As time went on and the threat from the north here became less apparent due to different Soviet tactics, the threat from India became to them increasing. 

In 1962 when the Chinese attacked India and we supported India, then the Pakistanis became very perturbed [security deletion]. They moved to establish better relations with China. 

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Hare, last fall when a Foreign Affairs subcommittee visited Pakistan, it was found that our U.S. Embassy personnel were not permitted to travel except between the two capitals of Pakistan unless they had written permission.

Mr. HARE. They were still in effect.

(The information follows:) MOVEMENT OF PALESTINIAN REFUGEE LABOR TO OTHER ARAB COUNTRIES 

As far as we are aware, there are no restrictions placed on Palestinian refugees in the host countries to prevent their acceptance of employment in other Arab countries. The UNRWA educational program, particularly its vocational training, encourages this process. UNRWA also helps the graduates of its training centers to find jobs, in many cases outside of the host country. Although overall figures for refugees who are employed in other Arab countries are not available, a recent report from our Embassy in Cairo states that in the last 4 years over 4,000 refugees from Gaza have come to Egypt alone to work. 

[Security deletion.] 

In our view the best way to encourage the movement of refugees out of the camps and into jobs is for UNRWA to give practical training to as many refugees as possible. A well-trained man is not likely to vegetate in a refugee camp when he knows he can find good employment elsewhere. For this reason the United 

States has considered it important to support UNRWA's educational program. 

In December 1963, for example, we asked that 5 percent of our contribution be shifted from UNRWA's relief budget to its educational program. In December 

1965, we asked that any reductions in UNRWA's programs which might be necessitated by the Agency's financial difficulties not be at the expense of its education program. 

Mrs. BOLTON. I recognize it isn't your area. I wish it were.

Mr. MACOMBER. Four.

Iran is the next graduate.

not. There are many things wrong with the aid program that ought to be discussed, but much time is taken up discussing these things that are not true.

Mr. ADAIR. Mr. Chairman. 

Although others have said it, I want to add my word to you gentlemen and say among the many witnesses that appear before us, you are certainly amongst the most esteemed and respected. 

Mrs. Bolton, Ambassador Hare, spoke about the value of your appearances before the subcommittee. I certainly want to subscribe to what she has said. Your unhurried, objective presentation was most valuable. I only regret that the entire committee wasn't there to hear what you had to say on those occasions. Then, of course, anyone who could take three and a half years as liaison man with the Congress deserves some sort of special accolade, I think, Mr. Macomber. 

Moving on to the questions, several years ago we were quite interested in the Helmand River project and all its ramifications up in Afghanistan. Is there anything included for this fiscal year and any scheduled for next fiscal year?

Mr. MACOMBER. Yes, sir; there is. It is an on-going program. We are providing technical assistance and development loans. 

Mr. ADAIR. To improve agriculture? 

Mr. MACOMBER. Yes, basically for supervision of reclamation work and for extension, plus occasional capital construction. 

Mr. ADAIR. For power or irrigation canals?

Mr. MACOMBER. Both. A lot of money can be spent on power and this raises an important and interesting issue when you are struggling to make a better aid program. We are heavily involved in the 

Helmand Valley, yet the portion we work on represents only 1 percent of the arable land of Afghanistan.

Mr. ADAIR. Several years ago it was the attitude that we sort of got dragged into that thing by the heels, and were getting a lot of blame in Afghanistan for some of the shortcomings of the program while at the same time we were told it was going to yield more electric power than would be needed. 

Is that latter fact still true?

Mr. MACOMBER. No. It ultimately will be a great power asset to the country. Now power consumption is very low. 

We are worried about the amount of power Afghanistan wants to add in the valley. We think the plan is to ambitious at this point of time that the power authority may have only limited capacity to manage it. So we are in a little bit of a tug of war here. We are

not going ahead with financing additional power until we are now persuaded the need exists and we think they will develop the human skills to operate it.

The technical assistance program goes on. It is quite true the Afghanistan Government has gotten its prestige committed to the success in the valley. They argue so have we. 

We feel we should keep working there, but we don't want to slight the rest of Afghanistan. We want to work in such things as seed and irrigation elsewhere in the country, too.

Mr. ADAIR. How much are we putting in this fiscal year in that project?

Mr. MACOMBER. $1.6 million this year, and we will consider a loan for additional power at Kajakai Dam. That is a $15 million project. 

We also have a loan under consideration for some land reclamation work. The first figure I gave you is a grant. 

Mr. ADAIR. For technical assistance? 

Mr. MACOMBER. Yes.

Mr. ADAIR. Turning to Egypt, would you enlighten us a little further as to what you know about Nasser's plans with respect to Yemen? 

Mr. HARE. You will recall that Nasser went in there at the invitation of a Yemeni Government that arose from the coup several years ago. He put more and more troops in there and he found, as many people had found before him, that Yemen was a sort of military quicksand. In the process, he became confronted with Saudi Arabia which was supporting the monarchists. At one time it appeared that there was a danger of actual hostilities between the United Arab 

Republic and Saudi Arabia. 

But last year in August Nasser went to Jidda to meet King Faisal and they drew up the so-called Jidda Agreement which called for a meeting of both Yemeni groups in November.

Following the meeting there would be set up a provisional government and the United Arab Republic would withdraw. The conference met. It did not succeed in setting up a provisional government. 

Nothing happened whatsoever.

Then the question arose what would happen after that. Saudi 

Arabia maintained that the United Arab Republic should withdraw. 

Mr. ADAIR. Are they withdrawing?

Mr. HARE. Not at the present time. They are regrouping but they are not withdrawing. 

Mr. ADAIR. How many troops do they have? 

Mr. HARE. Around 70,000.

Mr. ADAIR. Could it be said, Mr. Ambassador, that any of the aid we are giving either through this bill or Public Law 480 is contributing to the support of those troops in Yemen, Nasser's troops in Yemen, do you think?

Mr. HARE. It would be very difficult for me to envisage a contingency where that would be true. Do you have any comment, Bill?

Mr. MACOMBER. [Security deletion.] 

Mr. ADAIR. Specifically, and again relating not only to this bill but to Public Law 480, what do you see for Egypt in the coming fiscal year? 

Mr. HARE. In Public Law 480, sir-

Mr. ADAIR. Both Public Law 480 and under the terms of these bills.. 

Mr. MACOMBER. In this bill we have $2.2 million for a technical assistance program. That is all the aid program we now plan for the 

United Arab Republic next year, except the pipeline which continues on. We have a 6 months' Public Law 480 program which ends June 

30, this year.

Mr. ADAIR. What then? 

Mr. MACOMBER. No one can tell you what then. 

Mr. ADAIR. There are no definite plans?

Mr. MACOMBER. If our relations are improving and better than they were sometime in the past, we presumably will go ahead with Public 

Law 480, but we will see.

Mr. ADAIR. I address this question to either of you: Has the way in which we have used our aid in recent months, or within the last year or two in Egypt-and again in aid, I am including Public Law 480 operations tended to weaken the ties between the Communists and 

Nasser to any degree?

Mr. HARE. I would be inclined to put it a little differently. I think that what it has done, what you can demonstrate is that it has given us an entree. [Security deletion.] We can maintain a dialog. That we know.

To what extent our being there detracts from the effectiveness of the others being there is a question. But what we are thinking about primarily is a specific objective, and that is maintaining a viable contact with the United Arab Republic for our own special reasons. 

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Selden. 

Mr. SELDEN. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. I want to join with the other members in welcoming Secretary Hare and also in welcoming back Mr. Macomber. In line with Congressman Adair's questioning, has there been any appreciable change, in recent months, in the antagonistic attitude of Mr. Nasser?

Mr. HARE. [Security deletion.] In this connection I was Ambassador to the United Arab Republic, for Egypt, it was both while I was there- Mr. SELDEN. I had a pleasant visit to Egypt when you were the 

Ambassador there.

Mr. HARE. [Security deletion.] 

You have to distinguish between basic actions in private discussions and what is said publicly. Unfortunately however, you can't just completely ignore what is said publicly. For instance, just recently President Nasser made a speech which was quite unpleasant, and almost exactly at the same time it coincided with the visit here of one of his closest confidants who came in a friendly way with a warm letter from President Nasser. This was within a question of a day or two. [Security deletion.]

Mr. SELDEN. I have been concerned with the fact that the tricontinent conference that met in Havana last January, which by its resolutions openly advocated subversive aggression here in our own hemisphere, received a special message from Mr. Nasser with an invitation to hold its conference next year in Cairo. 

I would hope that in connection with our aid program we would let Mr. Nasser know that support of this conference doesn't set well with the American people and, in my opinion, there will be real

pressure in this country to cut off any aid to the United Arab Republic if the conference is held there. 

Do you have any comment on that? 

Mr. HARE. Yes, sir. This conference grew out, apparently, as some appendage of the old Afro-Asian Solidarity Organization which has its headquarters in Cairo. The representation in Havana was not official, but the intent of the conference, I agree with you entirely, was completely unacceptable. 

Mr. SELDEN. There were some official members of Nasser's government there.

Mr. HARE. That is correct. Official members were there, but not as official representatives. 

Mr. SELDEN. They were there with his knowledge and consent, however.

Mr. HARE. Certainly. It is our understanding that the next conference is to be in 1968 and I would like to assure you that we have ways to indicate that we don't think this is a good idea. [Security deletion.]

Mr. SELDEN. Thank you. 

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Frelinghuysen. 

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

I would like to join in welcoming these two gentlemen to the committee today. As might be expected, they are interesting witnesses. 

I would like to start off by asking about the proposed aid to India and Pakistan. As I understand it from reading the books, about $600 million is to be made available to those two countries under the proposal which you are submitting.

This is roughly the same amount which you had planned to make available during the current year, but which you did not, because of the unfortunate developments in the area? 

Mr. MACOMBER. That is correct.

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. If self-help is so important, and if as Mr. 

Macomber pointed out we can expect some decisive and resourceful self-help in this area, I wonder if you could spell out why we are so optimistic about a change of position with respect to those two countries? Or have you been satisfied with the degree of self-help which they have been providing in previous years?

Mr. MACOMBER. In the case of Pakistan, we have been very gratified by their self-help performance.

They have liberalized imports. They are relying more on the marketplace to regulate the use of their resources. They have freed up the private sector. They have gone after the agricultural sector in a much more aggressive and effective way. 

They have a creditable record in increasing their exports. They have done some tough belt tightening. Next door, India has done a remarkable job in many ways and led the developing world for quite a time, but in the last few years its economy has slowed down. 

Its own people recognize this. India is plagued with a problem, for instance, of underutilization of its existing industrial capacity. Their controls and plans tend to work rather in the direction of building more capacity.

Pakistan had the same problem 2 years ago, but through liberalization of imports, use of some capacity went up to 95 percent. India has

not taken this step yet. India also has not taken the kind of steps we feel they should to attract foreign investment and stimulate its own private investment.

They haven't done the kind of job that needs to be done on the export side. At least of equal importance they have not really borne down on their agriculture problem. If you look at the record over the last 15 years it is not so bad considering the massiveness of the problem. 

It is very easy for me to sit here and say they should be doing better in agriculture.

They opened new lands but they have run out of land and that way of increasing production is ended. Yields per acre are low. Credit must be gotten to the farmer. Prices of food grains have not always been high enough to give incentives to increase production. There hasn't been enough fertilizer to buy. Water is spread thinly, rather than mobilized in the more fertile areas. All these things have slowed 

India down.

We have reason to be quite hopeful that Indians will act on these problem areas. On self-help, we can't stand off and say you must do this or that. The only real way you can succeed is when there are many officials within the government who agree our proposals make sense and should be adopted.

I think it is quite clear that if India does not make at least some progress, then the interest in helping it is going to drop off a good deal, not only on our part, but on the part of other consortium members. 

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. These differences between the two countries would seem to be a reason to be very cautious about any large-scale program such as is presently contemplated for India. 

As you pointed out, these are very long standing problems. Of course, India has problems. That is why, as you say, it isn't easy to tell them "Improve your agriculture," and get them to do it. 

It isn't clear to me what makes you think there is more reason to hope this year for a dramatic change than there was last year. Surely the confrontation with Pakistan, or the new look that the administration is taking with respect to criteria for giving aid, hasn't developed in India an awareness of the necessity of taking the same belt-tightening practices that Pakistan has pursued, has it?

I think many people in India believe that they should undertake economic reform. The Government faces election next February. 

Some of the measures would be unpopular, and time is squeezing in on them.

Mr. MACOMBER. [Security deletion.] 

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. I am sure my time has run out. It isn't that

I am trying to shut you off; I would like more time to discuss matters of this consequence. 

Ambassador Hare, in your prepared statement you have two references to India and Pakistan which I wonder if you would clarify for me. 

On page 3, you say:

We have concluded that future aid to India and Pakistan must be related rather directly to progress toward securing peace between them, since without peace economic development is not possible. Without economic development security is unsure.

I would think this would be the kind of test that would be impossible for them to meet. Or do you simply mean a cessation of hostilities? You are not making a settlement of the Kashmir situation a condition for aid, are you? Maybe that would be a good idea, but 

I don't suppose you mean that by this statement?

Mr. HARE. [Security deletion.] The thought here is that you would have to have a reasonable assurance that there would not be hostilities over Kashmir again.

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. How could you get a reasonable assurance on that point? That is what I am interested in. It would be great if you could. How could we get any reasonable assurance that they will sign a piece of paper saying "we hope we don't"

Mr. HARE. I don't believe that is a prospect right now. I do believe that both sides were sobered at what happened. They both engaged in war. It stopped at a certain point. I think both looked over the abyss and saw the very hazardous and dangerous future.

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. That is a different argument than to say that "we are going to secure peace," and to require that there must be evidence of progress toward securing peace. 

You now say they recognize the danger of a real change in the status quo, but this isn't at all progress toward peace. 

Mr. HARE. That is the first point I intended to make.

The second one is that this is just not fear. Since the subject of 

Tashkent, a number of steps have been taken, and particularly on the military side. The military really has completed almost entirely [security deletion] everything that had to do with withdrawal.

They have taken a number of other steps. On the military side it has been interesting. There has been fairly close cooperation between the Pakistanis and the Indians. 

This type of thing is real, and I hope we can go further.

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. I would like to ask about your statement on page 2. I quote:

The will and determination of the peoples of this region to withstand the pressures from Communist China will, in the long run, bear decisively on the question of whether Communist China can be contained. 

How useful an argument is this? How useful a guideline is this in whether or not to give aid? Are we actively attempting to generate anti-Communist feeling on the part of our recipients? 

Mr. HARE. In general, yes. Specifically with reference to the Chinese development; yes, twice.

(The information follows:) U.S. CONTRIBUTION TO UNRWA 

The U.S. contribution to UNRWA in fiscal year 1965 was $24.7 million. Last year the executive branch proposed that our contribution be reduced by $1 million from this figure. This cut was to be in the Public Law 480 commodity por-tion of our contribution with the cash portion remaining the same. In addition, however, Congress reduced the cash portion by $800,000, or 5 percent of the proposed cash contribution, resulting in a total cut of $1.8 million. Our contribution in fiscal year 1966, therefore, comes to $22.9 million. We are proposing a contribution of the same amount for fiscal year 1967. progress

Mr. MACOMBER. I think it is a significant move. I saw the on the assimilation of the refugees, but not on the financial side..

What you have is 700,000 refugees living there in Jordan, of whom about 425,000 get rations, and of this figure something like 200,000 live in the camps. 

What you see happening is that these camps become suburbs of the cities. If you go to Amman, as you have, Mr. Farbstein, you know that the camps that surround the capital city have become suburbs of the city. If you ask the people there if they are refugees they say they are. Many have part-time work. 

If you go down to Aqaba they are all refugees. They wouldn't go back. You see, the economy is picking up these people, giving them work and a new home. Although they technically regard themselves as refugees, their lives become more oriented to their new home. 

There has been some progress in reducing our financial burden. 

As I suggested, this problem would take care of itself if you had enough time. But what concerns you was, Could we keep on paying for this? 

Mr. FARBSTEIN. I recommend you read an article yesterday in the

New York Times. I think it is stated there that one merchant has 

1,400 ration cards.

Mr. MACOMBER. That is a very serious problem. As a result of that, the UNRWA will not issue new cards no matter how many refugee babies are born. There are no new ration cards issued unless people turn in these others. Merchants do buy them up.

Egyptian cotton.

Mr. HARE. There is one basic factor here. As far as the normal pay and food and upkeep for these soldiers is concerned, they would be paying that even if the troops were in Egypt. The expense would be the difference in maintaining them in another country. 

Mr. FARBSTEIN. I suppose you could look at it that way, except when you are in a foreign country I think you have arms that are being used together with living costs, which may not reach the same degree that it would in the foreign country, because by the same token I suppose you could say it costs us just as much money to keep our soldiers here as to keep them in Vietnam. Mr. MACOMBER. That is right.

Mr. FARBSTEIN. You talk of military aid also when you say this?

Mr. FARBSTEIN. You mean Jordan will be sufficiently self-sustaining to be able to purchase its own arms?

Mr. FARBSTEIN. It is going to be a long time.

Mr. MACOMBER. It always takes longer than you think. But they appear to be on their way. If they can keep building up their income, as they say, in 12 years, 15 years they will be on their own as far as budget support is concerned.

(The information follows:) FREE WORLD AID TO YEMEN KUWAIT 

(a) In 1964: A one-time grant from the Gulf Permanent Assistance Committee totaling $4,200,000 for a 3-stage program for construction of 22 schools accommodating 5,520 students, 20 clinics, and 3 hospitals.

(b) In 1964: A loan of $28 million from the Kuwait Fund for Arab Development, for capital development projects in Yemen. Approval of individual projects is dependent upon the results of a general economic survey which, so far, has not taken place.

WEST GERMANY 

Technical assistance grants total $3.35 million of which $1.1 million was disbursed as of December 31, 1964. In addition West Germany has agreed to provide $2.5 million in development loans, none of which have been made to date. 

Yemen broke diplomatic relations with West Germany early in 1965 and, except for telephone assistance in Taiz, West Germany's assistance to Yemen has been suspended since that date.

The following projects are financed from technical assistance grants: (a) A sisal farm near Taiz of 1,200 hectares. (b) Ground water survey and demonstration agricultural farm at Mirawa, in the Tihama region near Hodeida. (c) An agricultural vocational training school, tree nursery, and experimental farm in Sanaa. (d) A tree nursery and plant protection in Taiz. (e) Well-drilling projects in the Tihama and the high Yemen. (f) Assistance to the telephone system in Taiz. 

ITALY 

A number of Italian doctors are working in Yemeni hospitals in Taiz and Sanaa as salaried employees of the Italian Government. SWEDEN The Swedish Save the Children Foundation operates a maternal and child welfare clinic and hospital in Taiz. A Swedish physician, assisted by Swedish nurses, is in charge.

UNITED NATIONS 

(a) The FAO operates a practical agricultural school in Sanaa. (b) The WHO maintains a child health center and a school to train nurses, laboratory technicians, and sanitarians in Sanaa. It also provides fellowships for the study of medicine and laboratory technology in Cairo. (c) UNESCO supports an inservice teacher training program for 300 teachers annually in Taiz and Sanaa and Hodeida. In addition it is planned to open 2to 

3-year teacher training schools in Taiz, Sanaa, and Hodeida with an initial enrollment of 120. (d) UNICEF sponsors an American Public Law 480 title III feeding program at the WHO center in Sanaa, and in its own building in Hodeida. (e) UNTAB awards fellowships for training Yemenis at the Institute of Public Administration in Cairo. In March 1965 UNTAB inaugurated a topographical school in Sanaa. It has a French director, employed by UNTAB, and currently has 12 Yemeni students enrolled.
