ANTI-AMERICAN STUDENT DEMONSTRATIONS IN THE NEAR EAST AND SOUTH ASIA AREA SINCE JANUARY 1965 INDIA 

February 8, 1965, Calcutta: 40 students held demonstration before American consulate general in protest against U.S. involvement in Vietnam-demonstration organized by Democratic Student Organization-no damage. 

February 9, 1965, Calcutta: 300 persons, including sprinkling of students, demonstrated before the American consulate general in followup protest against U.S. involvement in Vietnam-demonstration under guidance of leftist organizations, including both wings of the Communist Party-no damage. 

June 5, 1965, Bangalore: 50 to 60 youths belonging to the Karnatak Youth

League demonstrated before the USIS cultural center condemning U.S. action in Vietnam and Dominican Republic-no damage. 60-929—66—pt. 1- -9

July 1, 1965: Communist-dominated youth organizations demonstrated on Vietnam before a number of U.S. offices, as specified below: (a) Bombay: About 150 persons staged a 12-hour hunger strike before the

American consulate general, a similar number of persons conducted the same type of protest operation in front of the USIS; both demonstrations spearheaded by the All-India Students Federation-no damage. (b) Calcutta: 150 to 200 students squatted for 7 hours in front of the

American consulate general, and later staged a 4-hour demonstration before the USIS; operation conducted by the Bengal Provincial Students Federation, the Democratic Students Organization, and the student unions of 

Calcutta University and Asutosh College—no damage. (c) Guntur: About 100 youths fasted for 12 hours before the U.S. cultural center; demonstration sponsored by the All-India Students Federation-no damage. (d) Hyderabad: 40 youths conducted a 12-hour fast in front of the

U.S. cultural center; operation sponsored by All-India Youth Federationno damage. (e) Trivandrum: 75 to 100 persons, including students, demonstrated before the U.S. information library; demonstration under the stewardship of the All-India Youth Federation-no damage. 

July 10, 1965, Madras: 25 persons conducted a 12-hour hunger strike in front of the USIS to protest against United States-Vietnam policy; United States demonstration held under aegis of the All-India Youth Federation—no damage. 

July 14, 1965, Calcutta: Approximately 1,000 students marched on the American consulate general to voice protest against U.S. involvement in Vietnam; later, 200 to 300 demonstrators picketed the USIS for 4 hours; participants reportedly members of the Communist-dominated Bengal Provincial Students 

Federation and Democratic Students Organization—no damage.

December 4, 1965, New Delhi: 15 American students (most of them enrolled at Delhi University) demonstrated before the U.S. Embassy in protest against 

U.S. policy in Vietnam; these students called themselves the Overseas Americans for Peace, engaged in heated verbal exchange with Members of U.S. 

Congress-no physical damage.

PAKISTAN 

May 13, 1965, Dacca: Unspecified number of students, protesting against U.S. foreign policy, threw bricks at USIS library-slight property damage.

July 19, 1965, Dacca: Unspecified number of students, mostly from Dacca University, conducted anti-U.S. demonstration on theme of "dollar imperialism" at American consulate general and USIS—no damage.

July 20, 1965, Dacca: 50 persons, mostly students, demonstrated before USIS for 20 minutes in protest against U.S. postponement of Pakistan consortium meeting-no damage.

July 22, 1965, Dacca: 150 persons, mostly students, demonstrated in front of 

USIS protesting U.S. postponement of Pakistan consortium meeting-two po licemen and bystander injured by bricks, no damage to U.S. property. 

July 31, 1965, Karachi: Unknown number of vandals, presumed to be students, painted anti-U.S. slogans on wall of U.S. Ambassador's residence. 

August 7, 1965, Quetta: Crowd of unspecified numbers, composed mainly of students under the guidance of student union of the Government college at Quetta, smashed four windows at U.S. information center in course of anti-U.S. demonstration-damage limited to broken windows. 

September 8, 1965, Lahore: Unspecified numbers of youths, large enough to be described in press as "crowds," demonstrated in front of the American consulate general in protest gainst the alleged use of American arms by Indians against Pakistanis damage: burned U.S. flag.

September 21, 1965, Karachi: 10,000 persons, including many students, attacked the U.S. Embassy and USIS library in protest against U.N. resolution (supported by United States) ordering India-Pakistan cease-fire-minor property damage; USIS library set afire and badly damaged, no injuries to U.S. personnel; Pan American office also damaged by mob, U.S. government property damage set at approximately $280,000. The Government of Pakistan apoligized and offered restitution for damages incurred. 

September 21, 1965, Karachi: 100 Arab students marched on U.S. Embassy to express resentment over U.S. policy on Pakistan and Kashmir-no damage.

October 5-6, 1965, Dacca: Unspecified number of students conducted anti-U.S. demonstrations at privately American-financed and operated Notre Dame College damage unspecified but limited. 

December 7, 1965, Mirpur ("Azad Kashmir"): Upward of 100 students surrounded group of visiting U.S. Congressmen, yelled anti-U.S. slogans, and defaced windshields and windows of Congressmen's cars (property of the Pakistan 

Government) with paint and ink; demonstration lasted half-hour-damage limited to defacement of cars.

YEMEN 

On October 27, 1965, a Yemeni mob which included some youths who may have been students inflicted some minor damage on the American Embassy branch office in Sanaa. The demonstration was originally directed against the headquarters of the Saudi contingent of the United Arab Republic-Saudi Peace Commission but from there moved to the U.S. Embassy. The Yemeni Government subsequently intervened to disperse the demonstrators, made an immediate apology, and offered reimbursement for all damages. 

FOOD SITUATION IN INDIA 

The food situation in India will continue to be tight for some months. Assuming no overall worsening of the drought situation, India will have a total food grains crop shortfall of an estimated 12 million tons. There will be maldistribution of supplies in some areas due to organizational problems or transport difficulties. Rationing of food will be tight in most large cities. There should not be any widespread famine or starvation if the imports of food from all sources continue at high levels.

The Indian Government has been greatly concerned over the seriousness of the problem. A worldwide appeal has been made for food assistance, and about $90 million has been pledged by nations other than the United States. Port facilities, unloading of ships, and transportation have been organized and improved to assure efficient and rapid movement of incoming food supplies. In 

April the landed tonnage of incoming food grains will rise to about 1 million tons as compared with 500,000 to 600,000 per month in the fall of 1965. 

The Government has expanded to nearly 120,000 the fair price food grains shops to assure local distribution at fair prices of available food grains. 

As per capita consumption in India has improved somewhat over the past several years, it is probable that some decline in per capita consumption may be tolerable.

If food shipments to India continue at high levels, it should be possible to prevent any widespread famine and starvation. There will be smaller problems but we are hopeful that these can be quickly handled with the means available to the Indian Government.

If the summer monsoons rains beginning in May are normal, the seriousness of the Indian food problem will decline significantly in late 1966. If the drought again becomes evident in a widespread area of the nation in this period, then conditions in India next fall and winter will be very critical. In any event, India will need U.S. food assistance through this year and probably next year.

(The information follows:) PALESTINE REFUGEE SITUATION UNRWA'S BUDGET DEFICIT 

UNRWA's budget deficit for the current year was originally projected at $4.2 million, but recent reports indicate that Commissioner General Michelmore may have some success in raising additional funds to reduce the gap. 

PROGRESS TOWARD REFUGEE RESETTLEMENT 

There has been no major breakthrough on the question of resettling the refugees. The Arab governments remain adamantly opposed to resettlement on the grounds that paragraph 11 of U.N. Resolution 194 (III) of 1948 gives each refugee the right to be repatriated if he so chooses. [Security deletion.]

REMOVAL OF PALESTINE LIBERATION ARMY RECRUITS FROM RATION ROLLS 

The U.S. Government has repeatedly called the attention of UNRWA to the problem of removing Palestine liberation army recruits from the ration rolls. 

UNRMA has made representations on this subject to the host governments, but we are not satisfied with the results achieved to date. Unfortunately, UNRWA cannot deal successfully with this aspect of rectification without some cooperation from the host governments, since UNRWA alone would have great difficulty in accurately identifying ration recipients who are P.L.A. recruits. We intend to continue to stress to UNRWA the seriousness with which we view this problem and to press for action that will lead to concrete results.

It involves bringing high school and university teachers together for a 6-week program in the summer to expose them to the most modern methods of teaching mathematics and the basic sciences. (The information follows:) KARNAPHULI DAM, EAST PAKISTAN 

The Karnaphuli Dam with an 80-megawatt generating capacity began delivering power in January 1962. The total cost of the project was the equivalent of $102.5 million of which the net U.S. dollar contribution was $37.5 million. The 

United States also obligated $30 million equivalent in local currency.

The problems experienced as a result of damage to the main dam spillway structure, which had delayed completion of the project, were solved and the plant is operating at its rated generating capacity.

Mr. MACOMBER. The new Government of Ceylon, as one of its early acts, agreed to pay compensation and negotiated an agreement with the companies on what that compensation would be. They have already paid over approximately half. 

Mr. ADAIR. It is moving forward satisfactorily? 

Mr. MACOMBER. Yes, sir.

Jordan is the only one that is going ahead fairly rapidly. That again, as I understand it, will not basically affect the plan. 

Mr. MACOMBER. The only serious bit of construction is the one on 

Yarmouk River. That one is going up. That can still be built and keep the allocations within the Johnston plan formula. Where it will go wrong is if the Arabs build diversions up much farther to the north, and then run canals and run the water down into that Murheibeh Dam.

The outlook for both increased exports to Turkey and increased investmentshigh tariff rates ranging from 10 to 50 percent, covering both needed raw materials and capital. Turkey's problems have stemmed from that. We have been very anxious to see the private sector be given more of a role there. 

The new Government that has been elected has come to power arguing the same thing that these great state enterprises are often inefficient and need to be made to pay their way. 

They express this position, but it is very hard to go against the established custom of Turkey. [Security deletion.]
