WEDNESDAY, MARCH 23, 1966 HOUSE OF REPRESENTATIVES, COMMITTEE ON FOREIGN AFFAIRS, 

Washington, D.C.

The committee met, pursuant to adjournment, at 10:10 a.m., in room 2172, Rayburn House Office Building, Hon. Thomas E. Morgan (chairman) presiding.

The committee meets this morning in executive session for a continuation of hearings on H.R. 12449 and H.R. 12450, the pending foreign aid legislation for the fiscal year 1967. 

Our witnesses this morning are the Honorable G. Mennen Williams, Assistant Secretary for African Affairs, Department of State; and the Honorable Edmond C. Hutchinson, Assistant Administrator for 

Africa, Agency for International Development. 

Both of you have prepared statements. 

Mr. Secretary, you may proceed first.

STATEMENT OF HON. G. MENNEN WILLIAMS, ASSISTANT SECRE- TARY OF STATE FOR AFRICAN AFFAIRS 

Mr. WILLIAMS. Mr. Chairman, I am glad to appear before this committee. This is my last appearance in my present capacity.

I first of all want to thank this committee for its personal support in the past and also for the support that you have given the program, which I think has been essential to its success. 

Sir, I am perfectly willing to summarize the statement and put it in the record, if that would meet the convenience of the committee. 

Chairman MORGAN. Without objection, it is so ordered. 

(The prepared testimony of Mr. Williams is as follows:) STATEMENT OF HON. G. MENNEN WILLIAMS, ASSISTANT SECRETARY OF STATE FOR AFRICAN AFFAIRS 

Mr. Chairman and members of the committee, this will be my last appearance in my present capacity before this committee, and I want to extend my personal thanks for the support you have given to American programs in Africa over the past 5 years. This support has been essential for the effectiveness of U.S. foreign policy in Africa. The United States now enjoys cordial and constructive relationships with almost all the African states, in significant measure due to the mutual understanding engendered by our aid program. 

A marked improvement in relations between the United States and a number of African countries occurred in the past year, an improvement fostered by past U.S. assistance. For example, U.S. aid has proved to the people of Ghana that the United States has a continuing interest in the welfare of the country. As a result, the government in Ghana is now entering a period of closer cooperative 133

association with us and other Western nations. To cite another example, American corn shipped to Kenya to relieve a difficult food situation has given that country freedom of action in dealing with its internal problems and with extremist elements. Algeria is another country where U.S. assistance has maintained a link with the welfare of the people during a period of political upheaval. 

Our efforts in Africa will require your continuing support for some time to come. The great need for help and for development continues in a region where incomes average only 30 to 50 cents per day. The instability of the past year in some parts of the continent emphasizes the need for continued foreign assistance to help the young and developing African nations build the institutions of their new societies and to raise living standards. Without American and other Western help, Africa's internal stability and association with the free world would be endangered. 

POLITICAL DEVELOPMENTS 

In the past year the majority of African countries just passed their fifth year of independence and were still experiencing the problems of new nationhood. 

They continued to strive for consolidation of political independence and to make that independence more meaningful through economic growth. Most governments maintained a nonalined position while continuing and enhacing friendly relations with the United States. They permitted the United States to operate space-tracking and communications facilities, cooperated in civil air matters and provided diplomatic cooperation in a number of matters. American initiatives for peace in Vietnam won the promise of support from many of the 14 African nations I visited in January as President Johnson's special envoy. In Tunisia, for example, President Bourguiba has given support to U.S. Vietnamese policy and has warned other African leaders against Chinese Communist imperialism. 

Serious problems and instability existed last year in some countries. For example, the Sudanese Government continued to be confronted by dissident elements in the south. Refugee movements across borders continued, such as the incursions of Tutsi refugees from Rwanda. Mozambique was the scene of armed insurrection, but there was less activity of this kind in Angola than in the past. 

The problems arising from Southern Rhodesia's illegal break with Britain exacerbated the already-explosive situation in southern Africa that has arisen from the failure of white minorities to respond to black African demands for self-determination and racial equality as inalienable rights. The white minority of 220,000 in Southern Rhodesia seized political power over the 4 million black African majority in defiance of the wishes of the United Kingdom, the governing power. The rebel regime has not been recognized by any country and most nations are supporting both Britain's economic measures designed to end the rebellion and the UN Security Council resolution urging states to "do their utmost to break all economic relations with Southern Rhodesia." The stability of southern Africa is deeply affected by this rebellion, as is the credibility of the western position in independent Africa. Free African states expect resolute action by 

Britain and th West generally to quall the rebellion. The United States supports British efforts to end the rebellion. We believe strongly that American and 

Western interests are best served by containing the Southern Rhodesian problem and avoiding having its effects spread to the rest of Africa. 

In the Congo, much of the Central Government's energy and resources over the past year were funneled into a military effort to bring the rebellion in the eastern part of the country to a close and to rehabilitate liberated areas. This effort was largely successful in reducing any single coordinated rebel movement, but pockets of resistance still exist. Continued diligence will be required to achieve the level of security and stability necessary for political, economic, and social rehabilitation. During the summer and early fall of last year, the combined effects of the military effort against the rebels and in fighting among civilian politicians hindered effective government throughout the Congo. This led to a bloodless military takeover in November 1965. That coup probably has ended, for the time being at least, the immediate danger of political chaos which could have led to heightened disorder and instability. The new government has indicated its determination to provide the stability needed to finish the task of pacification and to proceed on a program of reconstruction and development, but the task is difficult and the magnitude of the problems does not permit rapid results. 

African dissatisfaction with economic and social progress to date is readily apparent in the series of recent events which have seen other military groups

1. 

Der assume power in a number of African countries such as Algeria, Dahomey, Cen tral African Republic, Upper Volta, Nigeria and Ghana. Most of these military takeovers were not initiated by military men thirsting for power. Rather, in some instances, the Army stepped in at civilian request to restore order in a chaotic situation or because an impassé existed among contending political forces that were tearing apart the fragile fabric of the new society. We should also note that in a few important cases constitutional means to achieve political change virtually had been denied to the people. In Algeria and Ghana, for example, these coups were a considered reaction to the arbitrary rule of the old governments which made change almost impossible except by a direct seizure of power The reasons behind the governmental overthrows involve consideration of other factors to place these events in perspective. The institutions of the new 

African societies have not yet been developed with the benefit of much experience and tradition. Educational levels are thin. Sheer poverty is prevalent. The existence of various tribal and ethnic groups provide fuel for internal rivalry. 

Communist machinations are ever present, and some states have interfered in the affairs of others.

Viewed in this perspective, instability is not surprising, especially if evaluated in light of the history of nation building in other parts of the world where great trials accompanied the making of states. Africa, which has just emerged from colonialism cannot be expected to vault into 20th-century democracy and stability without similar problems.

Leadership in Africa today requires a rare combination of skills to guide a nation along a steady and progressive course. The African populations saw independence as the key to national identity, personal dignity, and progress. 

After independence they expected visible signs of rapid improvement. When 

African governments fail to make progress rapidly enough for the people-or become corrupt, venal, or abusive of democratic processes-they lose support, just as governments do everywhere. In Africa, the traditions of political democracy are young and, as we have seen, there is a tendency to take more direct action against an unpopular government. 

Many people have asked if the Communists have had a role in the recent takeovers. The answer is "No." In several instances, in fact, they were in large measure a nationalistic reaction against Communist interference. In 

Dahomey and the Central African Republic, the Chinese Communist Embassy staffs were expelled shortly after the armies assumed power. In Ghana, many 

Soviet and Chinese technicians have been sent home. As a result, some segments of the Communist press have accused the United States of being the power behind the overthrows. This, of course, is without any foundation in fact. 

Why have the Communists failed to make greater headway despite their constant probes? I believe there are three basic reasons:

First, there is the good sense of the Africans themselves, who refuse to accept a new form of domination. 

Second, the Communists arrogantly overplayed their hands and gave Africans firsthand evidence of the danger to their liberties from Communist control. 

Finally, American policy toward Africa has proved to be a flexible tool in providing Africans with a friendly non-European and non-Communist association.

THE NEED FOR AID 

In assessing United States aid to Africa, we believe that a chief objective should be to help build institutions and conditions that will lead to stability and progress. In this connection, I should point out that we have reason to believe that most of the new military governments show indications that they will employ aid wisely by insisting on greater self-help measures, honesty and competence. The help obtained from the free world will be essential in assisting these countries become a part of the family of responsible nations. If hope for the future does not exist in an association with the West, these nations could well take a course that would be damaging to our national interest. 

Obviously, we would prefer to see the growth of stability and constitutional government which allows citizens to judge and, if necessary, dismiss governments by peaceful, legal means. 

African political developments no doubt will continue to reflect the newness and inexperience of these independent nationals, as well as the jealous guarding of African rights and a fierce determination to preserve African independence.

In this historical process of consolidating nationhood, expectations of stability similar to that of established societies would be unrealistic. Experimentation, power conflicts, and change will continue, and in this cauldron of forces the Communists will press for an opportunity to penetrate. 

Even though unrest in Africa has occupied most of our attention in recent months, there nevertheless has been growth and progress in many parts of the continent-much of which has been stimulated by U.S.-aid program. I would like to mention an example of growth in a country where we have one of our largest aid programs-Tunisia.

Tunisia is a small but important country in which an honest and determined government is working hard to bring stability and progress to a key area of the continent. Tunisia has shown an average annual growth of GNP of 5.6 percent. With 40 percent of its land devoted to agriculture and 55-60 percent of its people engaged in agricultural pursuits, much of our aid effort is concerned with helping increase agricultural output, including agricultural extension and rural development. As one example, insufficient water is one of Tunisia's most serious continuing problems. U.S. aid is helping with the development of storage dams, wells, and irrigation systems. These projects are expected to increase food output substantially and will lead to further progress and enhance political stability. The progress already shown in Tunisia has been a prime factor supporting stability in this country. 

Tunisia is also taking active steps to promote private business. The Government is withdrawing from hotel construction and management, and private investors have shown increased interest in participating in these activities. The 

Government has decided to sell a million acres of state-owned farmland to private farmers. American business is also being supported in a new program in which small- and middle-size businessmen from both the United States and Tunisia are visiting each other's countries to find opportunities to establish joint ventures.

In recent months we have seen how important has been our policy of maintaining contacts with people in all walks of life through our aid program. In every instance where a military takeover occurred, there was a spontaneous turning to the United States for assistance because of past associations and our aid and other programs. And, although the Communists keep up a propaganda drumfire against us, they have been expelled in a number of cases. 

In this context, our aid program is a chief component of American policy toward Africa. As President Johnson said in his aid message to Congress last month: "The incessant cycle of hunger, ignorance, and disease is the common blight of the developing world. This vicious pattern can be broken. It must be broken if democracy is to survive."

In Africa, virtually every country needs economic assistance and it is in our national interest, as well as right, that we as well as other developed nations provide help. The goal of peace and stability throughout the world is supported by an aid program that can help build such a world. 

Therefore, America shares with Africa the desire for peace, freedom and growth-for a community of nations cooperating and sharing the harvest of political and technological revolutions and progress. We also have specific interests that call for a mutual relationship with Africa. The African continent is rich in a number of strategic materials needed by the free world. We have important facilities there such as space-tracking stations. American direct investment in Africa, although small compared to total American investment abroad, amounts to more than $1.6 billion and has been increasing rapidly. 

It is thus in our own interest to improve the chances for the emergence in Africa of free and independent states with a commitment to political, social and economic growth. Each such country which emerges in Africa strengthens the free world and enhances our own prospects for peace and freedom. 

Although the exchange of persons program is not part of the aid program, I would like to take this last opportunity before this committee to comment on this highly valuable program which promotes African-American understanding. 

The exchange program finances visits to the United States by African leaders and specialists, the education of African refugee students, and visits to Africa of American specialists. It complements the training of Africans for economic development tasks under the economic aid program. Nothing else that we do in

Africa is pointed more directly at increasing the mutual understanding so necessary for good relations between Africans and ourselves. We should strengthen this program.

May I say in closing that if we continue and even increase our effective participation in assistance programs to help in the development and preservation of free and independent African states, we will have contributed greatly to the security of our generation of Americans, and to the welfare of our children, who will be even more intimately involved tnan we are with the affairs and future of the nations of the developing world. 

In sum, then, what we want to see in Africa is the opportunity for Africans to develop in freedom and progress. We want our aid to be put to constructive development to help build stability, to allow Africans to meet their problems by peaceful means, and to continue their cooperative friendship with the United States.

Mr. WILLIAMS. Mr. Chairman, I have analyzed the coups that have occurred recently in Africa. I have indicated that the aid programs have been useful in part in helping establish stability in those areas where there nonetheless have been disturbances, that our aid programs have assisted us in maintaining a relation of friendship so that after the coups these people have tended to turn to the West, rather than to the East, and in a couple of instances have actually expelled the Chinse Communists.

I have indicated, also, Mr. Chairman and members of the committee, that the aid program in a country such as Tunisia, has been successful in helping that country move toward the economic takeoff, and has also helped that country maintain its independent international position. They have supported us against the Communists in Vietnam. 

I think, sir, with that, since this committee is very familiar with the arguments for aid and for our position in Africa, I will close the statement and be ready to answer questions. 

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Hutchinson, you may proceed, and then we will direct our questions to you and Secretary Williams at the same time.

STATEMENT OF HON. EDMOND C. HUTCHINSON, ASSISTANT ADMINISTRATOR FOR AFRICA, AGENCY FOR INTERNATIONAL DEVELOPMENT 

Mr. HUTCHINSON. Mr. Chairman, I will also submit my statement for the record and outline a few points in it. 

Chairman MORGAN. Without objection, it is so ordered. (The prepared testimony of Mr. Hutchinson is as follows:)

STATEMENT OF HON. EDMOND C. HUTCHINSON, ASSISTANT ADMINISTRATOR, BUREAU FOR AFRICA, AGENCY FOR INTERNATIONAL DEVELOPMENT 

Mr. Chairman and members of the committee, it is a pleasure to be here again to explain our proposals for U.S. aid to Africa during the fiscal year 1967, and especially to point out how the main segments and emphasis in the aid program tie into the points the President stressed so emphatically in his foreign aid message. In the printed materials which have been given you, we have tried not only to describe the specific components of the program by country and area and field of concentration, but also to show how the aid program as projected for fiscal 1967 builds on the lessons and experience of the 5 years during which we have had U.S. economic aid to the newly independent nations of Africa.

As indicated in the section of the printed program book entitled "Africa Regional Summary," there are some significant conclusions to be drawn from this past experience:

(1) The continuing large-scale interest of other Western countries in providing aid to Africa, resulting in the situation where the United States is an active partner but does not play the dominant role in the provision of aid to the region as a whole; (2) The fact that the realities of country prospects and performance and U.S. interests have caused our aid to be concentrated in comparatively few of the African countries-on the average in about eight; (3) The fact that technical assistance, for the training of manpower and the creation and strengthening of institutions, has been and remains the most important and urgent requirement for African development; (4) The fact that meeting these manpower and institutional needs is to a considerable extent a precondition for major capital investment in the private as well as the public sector, so that U.S. capital assistance, although needed, especially in connection with the training and institution-building activities themselves and in transportation and other fields necessary to tap unused produc tion potential, has been relatively smaller for Africa at this early stage in its development than for other parts of the world, and (5) The fact, that in spite of political upheaval and general restlessness, there have been relatively few crisis situations resulting in the need for substantial U.S. emergency aid and the proportion of our aid going for this kind of purpose has steadily diminished-meaning that our aid resources are concentrated more and more on economic development purposes. 

This experience provides the essential themes around which the proposed program for fiscal year 1967 is built. It is a program amounting in total to $221 million of AID funds, which will be supplemented by another $5 million of support for trained civilian manpower requirements in the Congo (Léopoldville) provided through the U.N. from U.S. funds, and by about $150 million of commodity assistance under the Food for Freedom program. Although we don't know exactly what other countries and international organizations may do in the fiscal year 1967, it is probable that their input will be at a level generally comparable with recent years, so that U.S. assistance in all these forms will account for somewhere between 25 and 30 percent of all external aid to Africa.

The proposed AID program has three main elements: The first is $94 million for technical assistance-reflecting both a continued priority to training and the basic institutional framework and additional emphasis stemming from President's "new initiatives" in food production, education and health, especially the latter. The second main element is development lending in the amount of $100 million, reflecting primarily the results of the active program of feasibility surveys and economic and sectoral studies. AID has undertaken as experience showed that the African countries themselves frequently lacked the technical and engineering staffs needed to produce fully developed projects acceptable for U.S. financing. The third main element is slightly under $27 million for supporting assistance, to be used for economic stabilization support in a few countries, primarily Congo (Léopoldville), and for a few small politically significant projects which meet the test of usefulness and operational feasibility but do not have developmental priority in our thinking. This supporting assistance amount is about 12 percent of the fiscal year 1967 total African program financed by AID.

There are also of course possible contingency requirements which we cannot now predict, except to predict that there are apt to be some. The most specific one is the possibility of additional requirements in the southeastern African area, growing out of the Rhodesia situation and its implications for Zambia and other neighboring countries. It is conceivable that the recent wave of coups and governmental changes in other African countries may create other requirements-depending on the evolution of the situation. This is not a forecast of any particular requirements of this kind; the fact is that we just don't know at this time. For example, we are providing emergency funds in the current 

1966 fiscal year for emergency transportation needs for Zambia, with specific objectives which should be met from this year's funding, but we cannot predict with any certainty what new requirement in this area might come up in fiscal 1967. As another example, we are responding initially to the new situation in 

Ghana principally through Public Law 480 commodities to meet urgent needs, but in fiscal 1967 Ghana may well need other forms of support for its stabilization efforts. We cannot yet foresee accurately the eventual repercussions of

some of the other recent events in Africa, but I would be far less than candid if I were to ignore the possibility that U.S. aid activity of some sort may arise from some of these events. 

With this general summary of the principal emphasis, amounts and uncertainties in the aid program, I would like to turn now to a few points which may be of special interest to this committee. 

One of these is the emphasis on self-help and performance which bulked so large in the President's aid message. It is clear from our experience that development progress is not something that can literally be imported from abroad. The will and the capacity for self-help are as necessary ingredients in Africa as they are anywhere else. The problem is to decide first what constitutes adequate self-help policies and measures in the African setting, and secondly, what the United States, contributing only a share to African development, can realistically require. 

We have listed in our printed material some of the self-help and performance criteria which should be common to all countries, but of course the refinement of these must be done on a country-by-country basis in the light of each country's actual policies, resources, and known problems. It would be unrealistic to advocate or to demand perfectionism on this score. If all African countries had smoothly functioning, stable democratic societies, economic policies designed to get the greatest and most lasting return from their available resources, efficient government and economic administration, and were making the optimum allocation of their resources between development and current needs, and between development sectors, they would not need our aid or aid from others, at least as much as they now need it. If all these things were true they would not be less-developed countries. So to me, self-help and performance standards in Africa do not mean any standards of ideal performance, but what can realistically be expected of governments and societies in the particular circumstances in which they now are. Essentially, I think this boils down to four main things: 

One is that they be genuinely serious about development-serious to the point of giving it priority over other interests and temptations, serious in recognizing that the pace of development will be basically determined by their own resources and capacities and not by the amount of outside help, no matter how abundant or well-intentioned.

A second benchmark is the need for pragmatism and flexibility-the ability to realize that plans and intentions, no matter how well drawn to start with, may stumble or go awry because of inadequacies either in a country's own administration or in the performance of foreign donors, or for reasons wholly beyond their control such as drastic external market changes or bad weather. The test here, it seems to me, is the willingness and ability of the country's leaders to recognize and adjust to these changes and not to cleave to a predetermined line regardless of what might happen.

A third performance measure which can reasonably be asked is the determination to take measures which are clearly needed but which may be temporarily unpopular or unpalatable. These may range all the way from the frequent need for budgetary or foreign exchange restrictions on tax and revenue measures to restore fiscal stability as an essential precondition of orderly development, to the ever-present necessity to defer progress toward some attractive goals such as universal primary education or industrialization in favor of sectors which have less emotional appeal but can actually do more to improve basic living standards faster. 

A fourth important measure is of course the country's willingness to give material support--money and manpower to those development projects for which it asks U.S. assistance, and to cooperate fully in their implementation. Lack of such cooperation puts a very severe limit on our ability to help, and in a few cases has made it virtually impossible.

The problem of how the United States can best work with African countries to insure their adherence to adequate self-help and performance standards is not an easy one. One way we can and do approach it is through our direct relations on individual aid activities. Where we are the major source of external resources as in Liberia and Tunisia, or supply a substantial even though not major part of total aid as in a number of other countries, it has proved possible to have serious and moderately successful discussion and acceptance of the need for responsible self-help policies for the economy as a whole. In other cases, the consideration of individual projects or aid to particular sectors has provided an op-

portunity to define and secure agreement on self-help measures applicable to that sector.

But the value of this kind of understanding is limited unless other donors agree and follow a similar path in their own discussions and unless the country itself adheres to similar standards in the use of its own resources. The most promising technique in the African situation, therefore, has proved to be concerted discussion and agreement between several major donors and with the country on the nature and extent of self-help and performance measures needed in the country's particular circumstances. Methods for this may be formal or informal, but consultative groups organized and sponsored by the IBRD are an example of one device which has already proved useful and from recent experience, promises even greater utility in the future. Related to this device is reliance on the International Monetary Fund to analyze and offer a prescription for monetary stabilization needs. Recent experience with the combination of these two approaches in the consultative groups for Tunisia and Nigeria was most heartening.

One final point on self-help is the fact that very frequently in Africa the failure to take what we would regard as necessary self-help measures is not due to unwillingness, but simply to lack of knowledge of how to carry out the required measures or lack of the people with the skills and expertness needed to do it. 

Clearly in this situation, the need is not so much one of laying down conditions as of helping the countries become equipped to meet them. This is in fact the purpose to which several aspects of our technical assistance program are addressed, especially the provision of U.S. operational people for key governmental spots concerned with central economic or fiscal administration or in major ministries.

NEW INITIATIVES 

The President's aid message also lays great stress on aid in three specific fields: agriculture and food production; education and manpower needs; and health and sanitation (including attention to the population problem). While we lay no claim to clairvoyance or precognition, the realities of the African situation-the urgent need for manpower training and institutional development and the fact that most of Africa is rural and the greatest known potential for increased production of wealth is in agriculture—have already compelled us to give primacy to the first two of these fields. That has, in fact, been true through the history of U.S. aid to Africa, particularly in the technical assistance category where these two areas together have accounted for perhaps threefourths of our total technical assistance activities in the last 5 years. For fiscal 1967 this proportionate emphasis is continued and in fact strengthened, but this does not mean any drastic shift in the composition of the aid program for Africa. It is rather a heightening and strengthening of the priorities already required by the nature of the problems encountered. 

The greatest share of aid for education will center on two aspects most urgently needed for the manpower and production priorities of the region-higher education, and secondary and vocational training. The program anticipates more than 125 projects in these 2 fields in 25 countries and in our regional program, requiring some $36 million of fiscal year 1967 funds. In addition, for two countries Nigeria and Ethiopa-we are undertaking comprehensive surveys to assess the adequacy and appropriateness of the extensive aid to education already provided and to provide guidance for the future. In another move to sharpen and make more cohesive U.S. aid to African higher education, AID 

and State Department's Cultural Affairs Office (CU) have agreed that AID 

should take on responsibility for undergraduates and graduate training in the United States of African students previously financed by CU in fields of study related to economic development. Among other advantages, this shift, which will become effective in fiscal year 1967, should result in the selection and training of African students in these fields under one set of criteria. It should also permit us to apply to this additional new group of students the concepts we are trying to work out for those already under AID financing, of relating their training and its timing to specific known manpower requirements in their home countries. We are also exploring with U.S. private foundations and other interested parties ways to apply these same standards and achieve these same results for the considerable numbers of African students now in the United States but not sponsored by any U.S. Government program. As you know these are

among the points stressed by this committee's Subcommittee on Africa in its report of August 1965, on African students and study programs in the United States. 

Emphasis in agriculture will be on training, on assistance to agricultural institutions, both educational and research; on extension services; and on irrigation and other projects to make possible fuller use of agricultural land and resources-including forestry and fisheries as well as crop production. Comprehensive surveys of agricultural needs and prospects and related investment opportunities are being undertaken in Tunisia, Nigeria, Ethiopia, and Morocco. 

The third of the new initiative fields, that of health, is the one which shows the greatest proprotionate increase in the program proposed for fiscal year 1967. 

The two major aspects of this emphasis are: (1) The large regional program of measles control and smallpox eradication being undertaken in west African countries with a population of over 100 million people. This project will extend over 5 to 6 years at a total cost in excess of $30 million, with some $7 million to be used in fiscal year 1967; (2) capital project loans and technical assistance for the supply of safe water in several major cities and rural areas, thus attacking one of the principal causes of endemic diseases. These activities may total as much as $40 million in fiscal year 1967. 

In the matter of population growth, we expect to give close attention to an assessment of the problem as a prerequisite to consideration of any action measures. This is among the purposes served by our technical assistance in several countries to improve statistical services. As a generalization, population pressure has not reached the serious proportion in most part of Africa that it has elsewhere, through there are areas of pressure. Several African countries have, however, undertaken active programs in this field, in some cases with the assistance of U.S. nongovernmental agencies, such as the Ford Foundation. 

Some AID support of these activities is being provided through means such as the use of our document facilities to reproduce printed material, but no specific provision for other work in the population field is made in our fiscal year 1967 program.

STRENGTHENED REGIONAL EMPHASIS 

Another point mentioned in the President's aid message was the intention to give greater emphasis to administration of aid through regional organizations and regional methods or channels.

This concept stems from the conclusion, which seems to be widely held both within Africa and among countries interested in helping Africa, that many African countries are so small in population and so limited in their own resources that their development prospects will remain very meager if they are confined to activities undertaken on a purely national basis within their own borders. 

On the other hand, if they coordinate their development planning and activities, they could avoid the pitfalls of wasteful duplication of national investment in small size uneconomic producing units and could concentrate on realizing the benefits of economic and industrial specialization through multistate or regional marketing arrangements, to the mutual advantage of the members. 

In Africa, the economic advantages of such a regional approach to development have particular relevance for the smaller countries, which are in most cases also those where the United States now has the smallest direct bilateral aid programs. However, it applies as well to east Africa where regional organizations linking Kenya, Uganda, and Tanzania have existed for some time and offer promise for increased scope of activities. The proposed Economic Community of Eastern Africa now under consideration offers the possibility of regional cooperation among a larger number of countries in that area, including Zambia and Malawi. As a practical matter, this concept is probably best applied within the bounds of the several subregional areas into which the continent is divided by geography and similarity of production patterns-east Africa, Central Africa, West Africa, and north Africa, to identify them very roughly. 

The question of how U.S. aid to Africa could be adapted to provide a greater regional emphasis has had extensive consideration within the executive branch and the Congress and was one of the points which received particular attention in the comprehensive review of the foreign assistance program made last fall. 

This consideration centered on two problems which affect not so much the validity of the regional aid concept as the timing and methods to be employed for it: 

First, the fact that most of the African regional organizations are not yet fully organized, staffed, and equipped to take on a sharply increased aid load and 

---

will require considerable time and help to reach that state; and second, the risk of advrese effects on U.S. foreign policy interests if the shift were to be made too abruptly. The basic conclusion was that our interests would be best served by an approach along the following lines: (1) The United States will give active support on request to the building up and strengthening of African regional organizations which can contribute on a regional and multinational front to carry out those development opportunities which offer a greater advantage if done on a multicountry basis. Such organizations include the new African Development Bank; the East African 

Common Services Organization and other east African regional institutions; subregional organizations which are starting to grow up in other parts of Africa; the OAU's scientific and technical branch and other organizations which can enlist country cooperation in special fields such as health and animal disease control; and the U.N's Economic Commission for Africa in its role of trying to stimulate and organize regional cooperation in Africa. (2) Concurrently, AID will move to put more of our aid on a regional basis under our own direct administration. This can be done to the extent that we can ourselves define and secure agreement that the scope of projects will be broader than single countries and will provide benefits to several countries. 

This is true now for some of our activities in the Africa regional part of our total program, such as the measles-smallpox program and the training of African students in the United States. This way of putting aid on a regional basis can be expanded, but this too requires time to give aid projects the broader scope required. In general, this approach is one which can be applied effectively only to new aid activities, not to those that are currently underway in bilateral programs. (3) The United States will continue to provide aid primarily on a bilateral basis to some 8 or 10 African countries which are for the most part the larger ones where we already have a substantial involvement for a number of reasons, including their basic resources for development. These are the countriessuch as Nigeria, Tunisia, Liberia, Ethiopia, etc.-where most of our aid has been and will continue to be concentrated. They account for close to threefourths of total U.S. aid to Africa. These are also the countries where we are putting the greatest emphasis on working out the techniques of cooperation with other donor countries and with international aid agencies, which I mentioned earlier. (4) For the remaining countries, we will work toward a pattern of providing aid through regional organizations and methods to the extent and at the pace which proves to be feasible and consonant with total U.S. interests. This will still result in a mix of regional aid with a substantial proportion of bilateral aid, at least for some time to come. For east Africa, the intent is to work toward this goal by building on and encouraging the existing and planned regional mechanisms so far as practicable.

The net result of this course of action in the fiscal year 1967, as shown in the program estimates which you have before you, is that most of the funds for that year will be used in bilateral programs even for those countries where the shift to regionalism has the greatest future applicability. This proportion will change in the future, and we do show a substantial increase in our regional program for fiscal year 1967, but this is simply a start along the road we will be traveling. 

NEW APPROACH TO PRIVATE INVESTMENT 

One other emphasis is in the field of private investment and private enterprise. This is a field which has posed a real dilemma for us. On the one hand it seems very clear that the prospects for development and increased production in Africa depend to a very large extent, on increasing the output of that part of the total economy which is under private management and the creation and growth of private activities in fields such as agricultural services and processing industries, marketing and distribution, light and medium industry to serve local consumption needs, etc. Some African countries have been a good deal more alert to the needs and opportunities in this area than others but most recognize it as a necessary aspect of total development. Yet the development of the private sector has proved to be one of the most difficult areas in which to provide outside stimulus and help. There has been a good deal of foreign private investment in Africa but, so far as U.S. investors are concerned, it has been largely

concentrated on petroleum, minerals, and certain major crops. Africa's own efforts to provide help and stimulus in this field have tended to center on the creation of government-sponsored development banks or similar sources of capital and credit to augment the inadequate supply and facilities for local private capital. These have been of some value, but one of the chief lessons from their activities to date is that it takes a good deal more than the availability of capital to enable local private business to develop to the point where it can play the role that it should play and that the countries need. 

The real need is for the kind of training and experience in business management and initiative which will enable private businessmen in African countries to recognize and capitalize on the opportunities that await them and to build, and manage successfully, enterprises which will serve the national development interest while yielding satisfaction and profit to the entrepreneurs themselves. 

One method we have tried to pursue for this purpose is to use AID funds to identify investment opportunities and to try to enlist the interest of U.S. investors who would work in partnership with local people. The techniques used for this-contracts with U.S. firms to make surveys and to approach potential investors, short-term trade and investment missions, and specifically organized trips by groups of U.S. businessmen-have met with varying but on the whole indifferent success.

We are now exploring a new technique which we think may have more promise. The essence of the idea is to induce Edge Act subsidiaries of U.S. commercial banks and investment houses to use their own staff and their own methods to pinpoint investment opportunities in Africa and then to communicate these opportunities to industrial investors here in the United States. Specifically, we contemplate financing the travel, salary, and per diem of representatives of these institutions during an extensive trip to a country. Each interested institution would concentrate on discovering the opportunities in a given country or small group of countries. These representatives would not be expected to perform a complete study, but rather, to ascertain those opportunities deserving of study. The home office of these institutions would then call these opportunities to the attention of industrial investors and suggest that it was worth their time and money to perform a feasibility study. AID will offer the industrial investors its feasibility survey participation grant as a further incentive. 

The basic concept is to mobilize that element of our private community most capable of locating investment opportunities and most respected by industrial investors. Two beneficial side effects are that this effort (1) will provide our banking community with a greater knowledge of Africa, and (2) will stimulate the flow of funds from our investment houses into Africa in connection with the financing of such projects as may develop. Preliminary contacts have shown a great degree of interest in this concept and sympathy with the proposed approach.

This is not, of course, anything like a complete answer to the problem of developing private enterprises in Africa-it deals only with one segment of that problem, the attraction of private U.S. investment. It is too soon to predict the results, but we think this new approach has enough promise, in a field replete with difficulties, to warrant a rigorous effort.

We have established, in AID's Bureau for Africa, a special Office of Private Enterprise which is pursuing this line of attack. 

I will be glad to go into greater detail on these or any other aspects of our program proposal, as the committee may wish.

Mr. HUTCHINSON. The Governor didn't talk very long in making his summary. I presume, however, he is saving his voice for other occasions. I don't have that necessity upon me, so I may talk just a little bit longer than he did, with the committee's permission, if I might.

It is a pleasure to be here, Mr. Chairman. This is my fifth year here, and it is always a pleasure to discuss our foreign aid program with this committee.

I would like to invite your attention to the material beginning at the bottom of the first page of my statement and just speak for a second about the characteristics of the AID program which we are carrying on in Africa.

First, one of the most important, striking characteristics of that program is the fact that the European and other countries, and the multilateral financing agencies continue to show a very significant interest in providing assistance to Africa, resulting in a situation in which the United States, while not an active partner in the programs which are being carried on in these countries, nonetheless does not play a dominant role in terms of the relative proportions of provision of aid as a whole, in the region, and, in fact, perhaps not in any particular country, with the possible exceptions of Liberia and Tunisia. 

The next major characteristic of the program which we are carrying on there is the fact that while we do have programs in most of the African countries, it is still a very heavily concentrated program, with some 60 percent of the aid over the years going to some eight countries, on the average.

The next major characteristic of our program is the fact that it is very heavily a technical assistance program related toward the training of manpower and the strengthening of the institutional base. 

These obviously have been and remain the most important and urgent needs of the African countries. Our programs are very heavily concentrated in these areas, to a degree you do not find in any other region. 

A corollary to this, perhaps, and another thing which characterizes the program, is the fact that our capital assistance, which also, in some degree, is related to this institutional building and manpower requirements-and considerable parts of it go into the capital cost of programs of this sort--is still a relatively smaller proportion of the total program than is the case with respect to programs in other areas of the world.

The last characteristic which I would like to mention is the fact that notwithstanding the very considerable number of extraordinary events which have occurred in Africa over the years, none of themvery few of them would be a better way to put it-have been of such a nature as to require the moving in with massive programs of support of an emergency nature. The only program of significance of that sort which has been and continues to be carried on is the one which is being carried on in the Congo (Léopoldville). This is quite a striking element of the program that we are carrying on. The supporting assistance element of our program has fallen sharply over the years as the nature of our interest in Africa has changed. 

This means that the program is heavily developmentally oriented as a result.

With respect to the size of the program, so far as the major appropriations categories are concerned, we are proposing technical assistance of $94 million, development lending of $100 million, supporting assistance of $27 million. While not directly a part of the funds which we administer, we are also proposing a continuation of the $5 million contribution to the United Nations support of personnel in the Congo (Léopoldville), for those people who are really essential to keeping the orderly processes of government moving in that country.

This represents an increase over the level of program which has been carried on in the past. The $94 million for technical assistance compares with an estimated $84 million this year. The $100 million for development lending companies with $46 million actually obligated

in fiscal year 1965, and with a figure which is shown in the books which you have before you of some $90 million in fiscal year 1965. 

If you pushed me hard, I would guess that we will probably not quite make $90 million this year, and so the $100 million is a bit more of a program stepup than these figures indicate. 

This arises primarily from the program of economic feasibility studies, sectoral analyses, taking the initiative to a degree ourselves in helping the countries with their project preparation work, their economic analysis and so on, which I mentioned when I was here last year that we planned to undertake during this fiscal year. It is beginning to show some results.

The next characteristic of the program that I would like to comment on for just a moment is this question of self-help which is mentioned so frequently in our discussions and which is heavily emphasized in the President's message. It has particular applicability, or shall 

I say "characteristic applicability" in the African context, in relation to the nature of the program which we are carrying on, as has been previously indicated.

First, the program that we are carrying on is a small proportion of the total assistance being provided to Africa and being provided to individual countries, and this makes it more difficult for us to relate specifically the undertaking of what we are doing on an overall basis to overall policies of the country as a whole. At least it makes it more difficult for us to do it bilaterally. 

We have tried to develop particular techniques to deal with this problem. Certainly we have examined the county's commitment to development in terms of growth, in terms of the resources that it is putting in itself, its ability pragmatically and flexibly to revise the programs it is carrying on in response to changes and circumstances, its general determination as exemplified by the level of taxation, the administration of the tax laws, and so on. 

In the African context in which we are not the major contributor of aid, we have done two other things to some considerable degree. 

One is to be quite insistent upon significant contributions to the particular project activity which we are carrying on. This is the minimum we think that is needed to be required in the way of self-help. 

The next thing that we are doing applies to a situation in which, while we are not a major donor insofar as the country as a whole, we are a major donor in a particular sector. This may be true in a country where we are going into agriculture and they too are going in quite heavily. In that case we feel as a condition of an individual project loan that we can discuss with them in some considerable and intimate detail the nature of their agricultural policies, the extent to which they are going to depend upon irrigated versus dry farming, whether or not they are organizing their projects, and their administration of this large irrigated undertaking in such a way as to let market forces bear on investment decisions, and things of this sort, before moving in a major way to an undertaking in such an area. 

Mrs. BOLTON. May I ask what country you are referring to? 

Mr. HUTCHINSON. Morocco, specifically.

The third and probably most significant way in which we try to apply the self-help criteria is multilaterally through trying to obtain agreement on the part of other donors as to the sorts of policies and the sorts of measures that the countries ought to adopt and then trying

to get an agreement that each of us in our individual programs will be saying the same things and requiring the same kinds of things from the country itself.

We do this in terms of bilateral discussions with individual countries, but even more significantly we do it through the IBRD consultative groups which the Bank itself may be taking the lead in establishing basic policy criteria for actions that the countries need to take in terms of their tax structure, their allocation of budgetary resources, pricing policies, and this kind of thing.

This is proving to be effective in those cases in which we have these consultative groups operative. It was particularly marked in the last two consultative group meetings, one on Tunisia and one on Nigeria. 

The President in his foreign aid message considerably emphasized, as you know, new initiatives in education, health, and agriculture. 

Insofar as the specific applicability of these new initiatives in the African context are concerned, I would like to say first that the nature of the African problem has been such that we were already very, very heavily into the agriculture and education areas with perhaps 30 percent of our aid going into each of these two sectors respectively even before the new initiatives.

With that kind of program being carried on already, we do not show in fiscal 1967 dramatic new increases in these two areas in the African program. We do, however, expect that there will be significant increases in both of these areas of the program and this is in large measure why our request for the technical assistance component of our program for fiscal 1967 is up by $10 million over our current fiscal year. 

In the health field there are specific new initiatives being taken. 

There is more in fiscal 1967 for water supplies related to health and then there are two specific programs in health itself which result in an increase in the health component of our program. 

The specific programs are the measles and smallpox inoculation programs. Measles doesn't sound like too much to us. We usually think of it as a minor irritant, I suppose, except for side effects of childhood. But in Africa it is a very, very virulent disease with a very high mortality rate. So we have undertaken a program in west 

Africa which eventually will probably result in the vaccination of some perhaps 20 to 30 million susceptibles. In the smallpox area, we are trying to combine this with the measles program. It provides a good administrative way for doing so and in cooperation with the WHO and some regional organizations in west Africa we think we can end up having a program over the next 6 years directly toward smallpox eradication in that part of the continent. 

This program would over the 6-year period, approximately vaccinate some 100 million susceptibles. These two programs together will cost us in excess of $30 million. We are providing some $7 million in our request for fiscal 1967 for these programs. 

Another major emphasis in the program is on regionalism in Africa. This has particular applicability to situations in which countries are small, their resources are limited, their markets clearly are not large enough to permit the development of significant industry, and the economic prospects of their standing on their own feet more or less alone are, shall we say, not the best. 

So we are going in those areas to concentrate very heavily on trying to develop projects which cut across country lines and which have

regional implications. This also has implications for those areas in which there exist regional organizations which could be built to be stronger, to permit regional cooperation. 

This is particularly true in east Africa where you already have the east African common services organization and where other organizations are proposed to come into being. 

We intend to do this in a number of ways. First, in our own operations to concentrate as heavily as possible on projects which have a regional rather than an individual country impact in and of themselves.

Second, we propose to provide technical assistance to a degree we have not done in the past to regional organizations in Africa. For example, we have already offered to provide significant technical assistance to the East African Development Bank in its organization, if they want it, in connection with establishment of procedures, to provide feasibility studies and so on. 

The third aspect of this will be that to the extent that these regional organizations develop the capability of doing so and to the extent that our interest makes it advisable to do this, we will provide funds to such institutions to permit them to finance projects. For example, we might make a loan to one such institution which they could be relending to other institutions.

The final aspect of our program which we would like to comment on is our attempt to get the private community involved to a greater degree and to try to do something about the question of private enterprise.

We have already expanded very greatly the involvement of the university community in our assistance programs to Africa. And we can already see a rise in the relative number of personnel coming in under programs which universities are administering for us. 

We are also working much more closely with the foundations these days and are getting cooperative programs going with them. In the field of the private business community we have tried a number of techniques, and I think as you well know and as we do, with varying success. Certainly not with complete success.

We have tried and will continue to try the approach of loans to development banks for relending to indigenous private enterprise. 

We have tried and will continue to try the device of having studies conducted by various firms of investment opportunities to see if foreign investment can be induced.

We will continue to use, and this has been a fairly successful effort insofar as projects in the minerals field and similar fields are concerned, our guaranty authorities as flexibly as possible in order to hold out these incentives to American investment in these areas. 

We are now trying a different approach on an experimental basis. 

It has not got underway in Africa, but our explorations in the States so far make us hopeful. It has had a very favorable reception in our discussions with the institutions involved. 

This is an approach whereby, instead of people just going out and trying to find possible investment opportunities and see if they can find an investor, we would get someone who knows enough about the American situation and the American scene to know what American investors are interested in in the first place, and then see if he can find that kind of opportunity.

This, in effect, means bringing the banks in pretty heavily to take a look at what investment opportunities there may be based on their knowledge of their customers' interest.

So we have been exploring with the Edge Act corporations and with the banks in New York, Chicago, and the west coast, their interest in making appraisals to the extent that it is necessary to determine whether they can see opportunities in which they think customers that they know of or contacts which their community gives them special insight into, would be interested in. 

There has been a very great interest shown in this. This is particularly true in the Midwest, in the Chicago banks, which we think perhaps may be ideal for the circumstances which exist in Africa because this is where you get your agro industries to a greater degree than perhaps in other areas of the United States. 

I started to say "enthusiasm," but I probably should calm that down a bit and say we are hopeful that some really worthwhile results may come from this effort. At least it is a new dimension to the program. 

It is a dimension which tries to relate what Americans might be interested in investing in to the investment opportunities, rather than trying to see in the abstract what the countries might be interested in or what opportunities there might, in the abstract, be in a particular country.

It is a new dimension that we do look forward to with some hope. 

Thank you very much. 

Chairman MORGAN. Thank you, Mr. Hutchinson.

Mr. Secretary, this morning the paper carries a story about the Government of Ghana seeking a $150 million loan. It might be to the advantage of the United States to offer a substantial amount of aid in order to support its new Government there. 

On the other hand, the history of its foreign aid indicates that the most wasteful efforts have been in countries where we went in too fast and had no plans. I have in mind some of our operations in the Philippines and in Panama. 

What is your opinion of this request from Ghana?

Mr. WILLIAMS. Mr. Chairman, I don't believe that we have had a specific request of that magnitude and we haven't completed our review of the new situation. I would feel this ought to be reviewed very carefully, that we should, as we have in the past, get together with the IMF so that we have a professional banking opinion on it and that anything we do ought to be in company with other lending countries in Europe.

As a matter of fact, previously we had already asked the IMF to review the situation so that the matter has some history. To summarize my answer, sir, I think that we should, as you suggest, get expert opinions such as the IMF and we should move only in company with other lender countries. I think if I might I would like to ask

Mr. Hutchinson to tell you what strategy they have developed. 

Mr. HUTCHINSON. The situation as we are seeing it definitely falls probably into three phases without clear lines among them, all merging together. One is the short-term, rather emergency phase in which the necessity will be of getting some staples on the shelf in the stores. 

In doing that, we think that Public Law 480 foodstuffs will be our major contribution. [Security deletion.]

The next phase is clearly going to be basically a phase of stabilization. The investments that were made by the previous regime were probably much too large, certainly they were in the wrong places. 

Certainly the terms of credit were much too burdensome so that the country has built up a debt burden that is intolerable. So the next phase almost certainly will be one of stabilization, debt renegotiation, which would be shored up by IMF drawings, again probably with some Public Law 480 from us [security deletion]. 

The next phase will be a developmental phase. This will merge into the stabilization phase I am talking about. This will probably again be under the IBRD leadership. They and the Fund already have teams who are studying the situation. We would expect that a program will develop which in from 12 to 18 months from now would be largely developmental in nature. 

This is the way we see the program shaping up: not an enormous program, not a large program in the near future, but a developmental program of some significance over time. 

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Hutchinson, the program you are presenting here today, as you said in your statement, is for $221 million, broken down $94 million for technical assistance, projected program of $100 million for government loans, and $27 million for supporting assistance. 

How does this compare in total amount to fiscal year 1966 ?

Mr. HUTCHINSON. Fiscal year 1966 as shown in our books is $203 million. 

Chairman MORGAN. This is an $18 million increase? 

Mr. HUTCHINSON. Yes, sir. This arises basically from two things.

One is a $10 million increase in the technical assistance aspects of the program, fundamentally as a result of the new initiatives that we had indicated in agriculture, health and education, and an increase in the loan component of the program as a result of the feasibility studies and sector analysis previously made. 

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Hutchinson, the program presented today includes most of the countries of Africa. I see that Libya is not in this program. Is there any reason why Libya is not in the program? 

Mr. HUTCHINSON. Yes, sir; we made a decision about a year ago, perhaps a little longer, that with the oil revenues which were coming into Libya, she could afford to pay for any external resources she needs. We would like to try to stimulate some help in the technical assistance area, but it seems to us she has the resources with which to meet the cost of this without the necessity of our moving in. 

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Hutchinson, according to the presentation book in front of us-the $100 million that you have programed for development loans you said some are not firm programs yet, but I see that you have listed about five countries. 

Are these programs that are listed in the book firm programs? 

Mr. HUTCHINSON. They are a good bit firmer, Mr. Chairman, than the $18 million for other loan programs and in fact, insofar as Ethiopia, Morocco, Nigeria are concerned, these are amounts assigned to specific projects the status of which we in fact know. The other $18.2 million is derived in this way. We have a very long list of specific projects in various stages of preparation. In fact, our total list adds up to just over $200 million, the odd figures slips my mind at the moment. We have taken the figures that you see for the countries listed www

out of that and then we have made an estimate based on past experience of what the likelihood of some of these coming to the fore are. 

This is the way we arrived at the $18.2 million. 

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Hutchinson, $27 million is for supporting assistance, and a large part goes to one particular country, which is the 

Congo. 

Mr. HUTCHINSON. [Security deletion] for the Congo (Léopold ville).

Chairman MORGAN. I believe that we have been supplying supporting assistance to the Congo during fiscal years 1965 and 1966. This is not a new program. 

Mr. HUTCHINSON. That is correct.

Chairman MORGAN. It is a continuation- Mr. HUTCHINSON. Of the existing programs. 

Chairman MORGAN. There is an increase this year in going to [security deletion].

Mr. HUTCHINSON. There is an increase over what was put in fiscal 

1965, Mr. Chairman. We anticipate that the program this year will be in the order of [security deletion]. It is a continuation of the same program that we are carrying on in this fiscal year. 

Chairman MORGAN. There was a great deal of propaganda carried on by AID here 3 or 4 years ago to the effect that we were moving away from this type program, but every year we still have supporting assistance hung around our neck.

I realize that a great amount of supporting assistance in this program-I think 80 percent-goes to South Vietnam. Yesterday we had 

Assistant Secretary Hare here, and he referred to the problem in Jordan; it has been with us a good many years. Now it seems that in your area of the world, the Congo is going to be around for a substantial amount of supporting assistance for quite a while. 

Either you or the Secretary can answer this question: Do we have any hope in the near future of getting rid of this burden of assistance for the Congo? 

Mr. HUTCHINSON. Mr. Chairman, so far as your general comment is concerned, I wish I had some charts here on supporting assistance in previous years, but we were providing supporting assistance to Morocco and Tunisia and some other countries which totaled almost $100 million alone just a few years ago. 

Chairman MORGAN. We had a reason. We had the airbases in 

Morocco. It was sort of a payment for our bases. We no longer have the bases, so we are rid of that. 

Can we get rid of this so-called free money, as Mr. Gross calls it, from this program? 

Mr. HUTCHINSON. That was going to be my next comment. So far as free money is concerned, we have put the Congo supporting assistance on a loan basis. This is not grant money. This is a loan. 

Chairman MORGAN. This is a loan? 

Mrs. BOLTON. Are you speaking of Brazzaville or Léopoldville?

Mr. HUTCHINSON. Léopoldville. We have no program in Brazzaville. The terms are 31/2 percent. If this country could get its organizational problem and security problem settled, its economic prospects are very good. 

Chairman MORGAN. I am glad to hear that. Thank you, Mr. Hutchinson.

Mrs. Bolton.

Mr. HUTCHINSON. The Belgians are providing aid to the Congo.

Mr. HUTCHINSON. The Belgians. They have this year agreed to provide amounts in excess of what they had been providing in the past. They are first providing large numbers of personnel. They are providing help in the military field, of personnel, and they have recently made available a credit of 20 million-I forget the unit of account which is quite an increase in the program which they were carrying before.

It isn't what we like. I have been to Brussels several times on this and my associate went and Governor Harriman went. They are now making a significant contribution. Likewise the European Economic 

Community is moving into the Congo, with funds which will get 

European money generally in.

Beyond that we have not been as successful as we would like in getting others to be providing assistance to the Congo.

Mr. HUTCHINSON. In the Congo?

Is Tanzania still permitting an illegal traffic in arms across the lake? Do you know anything about that?

Mr. WILLIAMS. I think this was true in the past, Mrs. Bolton, that arms did go across the lake into the Congo. I think some time ago that it was largely discontinued. 

I would be very happy to check into the latest report on what is happening.

Mrs. BOLTON. I would like very much to have a report on it. If you would be so kind.

(The information follows:) 

REPORT ON ILLEGAL ARMS SHIPMENT ACROSS LAKE FROM TANZANIA TO THE CONGO 

There is no firm information on the amount of arms which have crossed Lake Tanganyika from Tanzania to the rebels in the Congo. Neither is there any indication that Tanzania itself provided any arms although it apparently permitted them to transit the country. 

At the present time shipments, which may include arms, are crossing infrequently and sporadically and we have no reason to believe that Tanzania itself is aiding or abetting this traffic. 

Mrs. BOLTON. This consultation that you speak of, how does it really work?

Mr. HUTCHINSON. It usually works-the consultative group is what you are speaking of, I take it? 

Mrs. BOLTON. Yes.

Mr. HUTCHINSON. It usually works-the consultative group is what sends a team to the country to stay for sometimes several months, to make an appraisal of the development program of the country, its development efforts, the funds it is putting into development and so on, and their problems. Then the team comes back and writes a report, circulating the report to members who have shown an interest in providing aid to the country. These countries get together under the 

Bank chairmanship, with the aid-recipient country present, and discuss the country's programs, the problems they see in it, and what they believe the aid-receiving country needs to do in terms of its investments, its policies, and so on. The countries give some indication of what they are willing to do to help the country-not in pledging amounts of aid or anything of that sort; that is not done. It is in terms of what the country needs to do, in terms of its own "self-help" efforts, and how others can relate themselves to its developmental efforts.

I didn't have a chance to ask this morning.
