Mr. ADAIR. On page 186 of the briefing book in the second paragraph down from the top you say:

As an additional important step toward this greater regional emphasis is the United States plans to make available to the African Development Bank development loan funds for the financing of capital projects. 

We went into this question a little with Secretary Rusk and Mr. 

Bell, and were told it was not proposed to make contributions to the capital funds of that bank. This is your testimony, too? 

Mr. HUTCHINSON. This is my testimony, too.

Mr. ADAIR. They further told us that, however, it was proposed that we should contribute to consortium arrangements. What is your comment upon that? 

Mr. HUTCHINSON. We offered to participate in loans sponsored by the Bank on a case-by-case basis, Mr. Adair. The proposal that we have is that we would make a loan to the Bank, to be used for their special operations, as they call them, for relending to the countries concerned. We would expect to be talking to the Germans-in fact we talked with them the last time I was in Bonn in February about the possibility of their doing the same kind of thing. We have mentioned it to the British, as well.

This must be what the Secretary and Mr. Bell had in mind. We certainly don't see any formal- Mr. ADAIR. Would that be in the form of dollars that you are talking about? 

Mr. HUTCHINSON. Yes, sir.

Mr. ADAIR. What order of magnitude is contemplated now? 

Mr. HUTCHINSON. It hasn't been announced, and no statements have been made. We are contemplating the possibility of [security deletion]. 

Mr. ADAIR. The testimony that either the Secretary of State or Mr. 

Bell gave, or maybe it is in the book here--I guess that is here was to the effect there is presently in the capital assets of that Bank $32 million contributed by 27 countries. Doesn't it seem a little out of proportion if we lend to that Bank then [security deletion] in one fiscal year?

Mr. HUTCHINSON. The capital funds of the Bank are not available for these special fund operations; they are restricted to commercial hard-loan type of projects. So the availability of those funds does not free the Bank to get into this other area. Secondly [security deletion].

Mr. ADAIR. What sort of projects would you contemplate that they might use their $32 million for, and what sort of projects our [security deletion] for?

Mr. HUTCHINSON. My understanding is that they contemplate using their [security deletion] money for straight revenue-producing- 

Mr. ADAIR. That is the capital funds? 

Mr. HUTCHINSON. The $32 million; right.

The [security deletion], we see them making available for roads, for educational facilities, possibly some dams, although those might have some revenue associated with them in this kind of operation. 

Mr. ADAIR. I am not quite clear what you think of the $32 million. 

Would you give us some examples of how that might be used?

Mr. HUTCHINSON. My discussion with Mr. Beheiry is that he intends to make those funds available for things like railroads, private businesses, et cetera. He mentioned specifically toll roads, private businesses, government operations in the field of, let us say toll roads and communications for which charges are made. 

Mr. ADAIR. From what funds would the [security deletion] come if it is made available?

Mr. HUTCHINSON. They would come from the regular development loan funds. 

Mr. ADAIR. That would be dollars, you said?

Mr. HUTCHINSON. It would be included in the $100 million, if we obligated the funds in fiscal 1967. 

Mr. ADAIR. Upon what presumed terms?

Mr. HUTCHINSON. This we have not worked out with Mr. Beheiry. 

Certainly they would have to be softer than commercial terms, because he is going to have to pass some of his costs on to the ultimate borrower. 

Mr. ADAIR. What terms of repayment will there be? You would expect the loans eventually to be repaid in dollars with interest? 

Mr. HUTCHINSON. Yes, sir.

Mr. ADAIR. Presumably over a rather long period. 

Mr. HUTCHINSON. Yes, sir. 

Mr. WILLIAMS. May I add one point?

The Africans don't contemplate any non-African equity capital. 

Mr. ADAIR. That was made clear by the Secretary.

I am still a little bit concerned at the proposed functioning of that Bank if we don't have any of the equity capital in it. It looks to me like we are doing a great deal of financing if we do it in the ratio of 

32 to [security deletion]. 

Mr. WILLIAMS.

I think they hope to get up to $250 million by 

Mr. ADAIR. Capitalization? 

Mr. WILLIAMS. Yes. I think they have nearer $50 million than $30 million at the present moment. 

This is just their first tranche.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. O'Hara. 

Mr. O'HARA. Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

Governor Williams, I know that I speak for all my colleagues in saying that we are sad in realizing this is your last appearance before this committee. Pretty soon we will be shaking hands with you across the congressional campus. 

Mr. WILLIAMS. Thank you. I hope so, sir. 

Mr. O'HARA. Governor Williams, do you feel that during your 5 years in this area of service that the situation as regards our country and its relations with Africa has been improved? 

Mr. WILLIAMS. Yes, sir, I do, very much. I think one excellent evidence of this is the way in which the President's Special Peace 

Envoy on Vietnam was received. Fourteen chiefs of state in a period of 7 days received me, and they expressed great appreciation that the President of the United States would take them into consultation. 

They all agreed that our program was a viable one, and those who had an opportunity to contact the other side did so. 

I think that we have continued to improve our relations with these countries during the last 5 years. 

Mr. O'HARA. And that I think should be emphasized, the deepening in the warmth of our relations with those countries, highlighted by the reception that you received when you carried the peace message of the President. 

Mr. Secretary, I notice that on page 6, going to page 7, you give three reasons why the Communists have failed in Africa: 

First, the good sense of the Africans themselves. 

Second, the Communists overplayed their hand.

Third, that American policy toward Africa has proved to be a flexible tool in providing Africa with a friendly non-European and non- 

Communist association. 

Would you enlarge upon that? 

Mr. WILLIAMS. Yes, sir.

After the formal declaration of independence the African countries were very anxious to establish themselves as truly independent states. 

If the United States had not presented a third presence—that is, a presence other than the former European colonial power-then the Africans would have felt that their independence was somewhat illusory and only titular.

When the United States came in with an aid presence and when we recognized them by having embassies there, the African countries felt that they were being accepted as equal states in the world situation, and as a consequence they felt that they didn't have to turn to the Communists in order to establish their independence. 

Mr. O'HARA. Thank you for stressing that point, which seems to me very important.

I am sure that you were as disappointed as I in the finding of the Clay Commission that largely the responsibility for Africa and its development should be upon the former colonial masters. 

Mr. WILLIAMS. Yes, sir; I think it is essential that the United States try to continue to get the former colonial powers to continue to contribute, but we must have an equally important presence there. 

Otherwise the Africans will feel that independence has escaped them and they will turn back to the Communists in order to get a completely independent posture.

Mr. O'HARA. Now that you are about to leave the African field, do you feel we have done enough for Africa, or have we done altogether too little?

Mr. WILLIAMS. Sir, I think we have set goals with which the Africans are in agreement. I think sometimes in implementation we haven't moved as fast as the Africans would have liked to have seen us. I think that we should continue to increase our efforts insofar as we can within our budget, and insofar as it is technically possible. 

I think that in the future we are going to recognize that Africa, along with the rest of the underdeveloped world, is going to play a larger part in our future, and that we ought to program our efforts and our support consonant with that larger role. 

Mr. O'HARA. Thank you very much. 

I trust your words will be remembered long after you have left us.

I feel we must do more and more for Africa. In a sense, as Africa goes, so goes the world. 

Mr. Hutchinson, I don't find very much optimism in your report. 

Am I wrong in that? 

Mr. HUTCHINSON. Sir, I think you are, if I may say so. First, we do propose to increase our program pretty significantly. 

Second, we find that notwithstanding the dramatic events in Africa which get played up in the newspapers, we are not required to move in with massive emergency support, that the Africans usually come out of these things with their houses reasonably well in order. 

We do see there is movement in Africa, and we certainly expect our involvement there to parallel that movement on their part. 

While I haven't put any optimistic words in my statement, I would agree with you on that score, I would think it is an optimistic proposal, sir. 

Mr. O'HARA. I think you know of my high regard for you and your ability, your integrity.

I am not criticizing, but I am interested and a little concerned in the new program you suggest for attracting investments to Africa. 

You announce you are contemplating financing the travel, salary, and per diem of representatives of banking institutions during extensive trips to Africa. 60-929-66-pt. 1-11

How much are you going to allow them for per diem? How much money are you going to spend in this fashion?

Mr. HUTCHINSON. It will vary from country to country, depending on the allowance allowed by regular State and AID employees. would suppose this program in total for the entire African Continent might cost us perhaps $100,000, which is considerably less than we spend on hiring a firm to explore investment opportunities in a particular country. 

Mr. O'HARA. These will be American businessmen that you are sending?

Mr. HUTCHINSON. These will be American bankers, fundamentally. 

Mr. O'HARA. When do you start this program?

Mr. HUTCHINSON. We hope we can get someone out within the next 2 months. 

Mr. O'HARA. You will send how many, all told?

Mr. HUTCHINSON. This we don't know, sir, because we have not gotten the full depth of interest of the financial institutions here. We would hope to cover most of the African countries. 

Mr. O'HARA. As I understand it, you are sending American bankers to Africa, paying all their expenses, and they are going there to look up investments?

Mr. HUTCHINSON. To see if they can find opportunities in which they think customers which they have would be interested or which they think that, on the basis of their knowledge of people in their area, would be interested in going in there. 

Mr. O'HARA. Do you plan sending any to South Africa? 

Mr. HUTCHINSON. South Africa? No, sir. 

Mr. O'HARA. Definitely you assure us that none of them will go to 

South Africa? 

Mr. HUTCHINSON. I can assure you there will be none sent to South Africa. 

Mr. O'HARA. Do you think American money should continue to be invested in South Africa? 

Mr. HUTCHINSON. I must admit, sir, I am ignorant on some of the considerations on South Africa in this area since we don't have an aid program there.

I certainly am in violent disagreement with the South African Goverment's racial policies, but I am not expert enough on the South African situation to know where the balance of U.S. interest lies. 

Mr. O'HARA. We have no aid program in South Africa?

Mr. HUTCHINSON. No, sir. 

Mr. O'HARA. Have we ever spent a nickel in South Africa in the way of aid? 

Mr. HUTCHINSON. Not for the better than 4 years I have been Assistant Administrator for Africa, and I am not aware any has been spent there.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Frelinghuysen. 

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

I would like to begin by saying I share the concern expressed by Mr. Adair regarding the difficulty of getting the full picture. I am not simply referring to Public Law 480 when I say that.

I find it almost impossible to find my way around the presentation books, and to couple whatever material is available in the presentation books with the material that you gentlemen present to us.

We are not, in my opinion, developing a sufficiently coherent, comprehensive and detailed report for the average Member of Congress interested, for example, in how we are planning to spend money in Africa next year. How could we compare what we have been doing in the past with the present request? How can we find out what the changes of emphasis are going to be, and why we give aid to certain countries?

As a specific example, looking at these books and various pages, see you request substantial aid both for [security deletion] for supporting assistance. Could you give us an indication why we find it necessary to provide supporting assistance to those two countries? 

Mr. HUTCHINSON. Perhaps Governor Williams would like to speak on the political situation. I can speak on the AID aspects of it. [Security deletion.] The resources going in this effort to supplement the fund's money are coming in from us and from the Belgians. [Security deletion.]

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. I don't mean to interrupt-and my 5 minutes. are going to elapse whether or not I interrupt but I don't think we have a sufficient understanding both as to the nature of the U.S. responsibility to those two countries, and as to the significance of getting a toehold in [security deletion], or whether this has significance anymore.

I am not saying this request is wrong, but we have developed a sufficient factual basis either to define the international responsibility toward the problems of those countries, or how that meshes in with what the United States feels is its responsibility. We also need to know why, for political or other reasons, we feel a particular responsibility for these two countries.

You have indicated that we might also well be faced with the necessity of providing supporting assistance for Ghana. I would suppose that the contingency fund might be needed for some aspect of these activities. 

Is there no anticipation that you may use contingency fund money?

You have not used any this year, and you don't plan to use any next year, as I understand it, except for one brief reference. 

Mr. HUTCHINSON. There is a reference in the statement to the possibility of contingency funds in Ghana, Mr. Frelinghuysen. 

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. I have your statement open to that page, and it doesn't specifically refer to "contingency fund." Is this the reference on page 4? You mention the necessity for providing what you call "funds for emergency transportation needs for Zambia," and the possible need for other forms of support in Ghana for stabilization efforts.

I assume in both cases that the money comes from contingency funds? 

Mr. HUTCHINSON. That is right.

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. In Zambia this year? 

Mr. HUTCHINSON. To the extent we weren't able to find bits of money by cutting into other anticipated use, we took it from the contingency fund, and we would anticipate if there are significant requirements in Ghana and Zambia next year that it would come from the contingency fund. This was the purpose of my reference. 1 wwwww

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. If I may say so, I don't see any reference to an expenditure from the contingency fund for Africa on page 78 of the presentation book. It is probably found in three other places. 

Mr. HUTCHINSON. We can't program the contingency fund. If we did, we would put it in our request specifically. 

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. You can program it for 1966, because you have already spent it in 1966. 

Mr. HUTCHINSON. The contingency fund item for the Zambia emergency is shown on page 79, containing the remainder of the Africa figures, under the Africa regional program since it involves operations in several countries.

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. All I am saying is that these books are not self-explanatory. It is not easy to locate the information. The books aren't up to date. 

Mr. HUTCHINSON. I would be glad to come down and talk to you at length on these questions.

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. I am not seeking to be any more knowledgeable than anyone else on the committee. I would like to share my knowledge with them.

I would like to ask, if I could, Secretary Williams, about a statement on page 3. You say:

The stability of southern Africa is deeply affected by this rebellion [in Southern Rhodesia], as is the credibility of the Western position in independent Africa. 

I would like you to comment both to the degree to which stability in southern Africa is affected, and what areas you mean specifically, and also what you mean, Mr. Secretary, by saying that the credibility of the Western position is affected by what is going on there? 

Surely our own position isn't being attacked because of what is or is not happening in Southern Rhodesia?

Mr. WILLIAMS. I think unless the British are able to carry through with the commitments they have made with respect to Southern 

Rhodesia, our credibility will be affected. 

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. Our credibility for what? For being able to produce as much assistance to the British as we have been able to produce? Our credibility for what?

Mr. WILLIAMS. Our credibility for supporting self-government in Africa. 

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. Our credibility for supporting? You mean that we say that self-government is a good principle, but if we are not able to see to it that self-government takes place in Southern Rhodesia, Africans generally are not going to believe that we believe in self-government?

Mr. WILLIAMS. It is going to diminish the credibility of our implementing our policies.

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. What is our policy? You mean our policies in Africa?

Mr. WILLIAMS. Our policy in the United States and throughout the world, and in Africa, with which I am charged, is the support of the principle of self-government. This is the principle that the 

British are supporting in Southern Rhodesia. We are supporting the British there. If the British do not carry through, if we do not support the British in doing that, then the Africans are going to say,

"You talk a lot about self-government, but you don't do anything about it when it comes down to the crux." 

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. I don't suppose anyone is suggesting that we are not supporting the British. 

How could they question or doubt our credibility on that point?

Surely, we have made our own position clear. If the British are not successful in bringing the Smith government to its knees, this is going to affect us adversely throughout Africa? 

Mr. WILLIAMS. That is correct, sir.

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. I don't see how that is fair. How are we responsible for the success of the British effort to bring the Smith government to its knees? I don't see why you should make such a sweeping statement about the adverse reaction against us if this cannot be achieved. 

I am not saying that the result shouldn't be achieved. I am saying simply that I thought we have come out flat-footedly in support of what the British are attempting. Of course it may well not be sufficient to bring them to their knees. 

In that case, you are suggesting that the $220 million the United States is proposing to spend in Africa is, as I understand it, going to be wasted? You feel our whole position is undermined because we haven't been successful in helping one of our allies in achieving one of its objectives in one of the countries of Africa? 

Mr. WILLIAMS. I am sorry if I have given you to understand that if our credibility is attacked here that our $221 million program is of no value. I do say that if we do not continue to support the British in their expressed determination to bring down the Smith regime and move toward self-government, that this will affect our credibility. 

You say- 

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. If I may interrupt, that isn't what your sentence says. Whether or not we are going to continue to support the 

British position doesn't seem to have been seriously questioned. 

Individuals on this committee feel our policy is wrong, but surely you don't anticipate our Government's position on this matter is going to change. If it is simply a question of continuing support for the 

British, we should have no cause for concern at all. 

Of course, it is more than that, you argue that our credibility, whatever that is, our credibility is going to be adversely affected- Mr. WILLIAMS. Sir, I think I should point out historically that while we said we would support the British, we did not immediately impose all of the sanctions that the British did, partially because our laws do not provide for that, and that we had to negotiate for a voluntary control of our imports.

Ďuring that time, sir, is when there was some factual basis, which was expressed, that we were not supporting the British. 

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. But now you are going over past history. 

You are now saying that we were not vigorous enough in reaching our present position, or that we haven't gone far enough in your opinion to bring the Smith government to its knees. That is a different line of argument altogether.

It seems to me that what you are actually saying in this statement— 

I recognize, Mr. Chairman, my time has expired.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Murphy- 

Mr. MURPHY. Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

First of all, Governor, I want to state to you that it has been very enjoyable for me to have worked with you in the past 5 years. I enjoyed it very much. 

Mr. WILLIAMS. Thank you very much, Mr. Murphy. 

Mr. MURPHY. I believe it is due to your leadership as Assistant

Secretary for Africa that our policy in Africa was an important instrument in the recent repulsion of communism in Africa-especially 

Chinese communism.

I have many questions to ask but I do not have the time. Someday 

I would like to talk to some people in the Department and get answers to some questions I have in mind. 

I wonder if you would comment today with reference to our relationship with Ghana, Nigeria, Uganda, Sudan, Central African Republic, and Brazzaville-Congo.

Mr. WILLIAMS. I will begin at the end of the list. I am not sure I have all the countries down.

Mr. MURPHY. Take Uganda first. 

Mr. WILLIAMS. With Uganda, sir, we have normal friendly relations. They have just gone through a crihis in which the Prime Minister has seized additional powers, but during all of that crisis the relationships with the United States have been friendly, and, as a matter of fact, in the press the other day they had a very friendly editorial about my position in the Government. 

With respect to Nigeria, we moved cautiously toward the problem of recognition. However, we were satisfied that after careful examination that the new Government did represent the people, that in many instances the people were very happy to have this new Government, that they were going to be stable, and we gradually moved into a position of good and friendly relations with them, although we had never had an actual break in relations. 

With respect to Congo-Brazzaville, we do not have representation there now. Some indirect representations have been made to us about establishing our Embassy there. We are in the process of examining whether or not that should be done, but we haven't moved to even a tentative position of approval.

Mr. MURPHY. Does the Brazzaville Government have representa tives here? 

Mr. WILLIAMS. No, sir; I believe I haven't answered all the countries- 

Mr. MURPHY. Ghana. 

Mr. WILLIAMS. With respect to Ghana, sir, the new regime has been well received by the people. The military leadership has turned to the civil service for the operation of the Government. The civil service was British trained, is Western oriented, as is the Government. 

They are trying to return to the country a sound and stable basis. 

They tend to seek friendly relations with the West, and they have expelled Soviet and Chinese technicians. We do recognize them. We do have friendly relations, and we do hope that they are going to have a stable and successful government.

They do have very great difficulties, however, because the preceding government left them practically bankrupt, and it is going to be a difficult thing to get the economy back on an even keel. 

Mr. MURPHY. I was going to ask a question relative to supporting assistance in Guinea, but Mr. Frelinghuysen raised that point.lan 

What is our relationship with Guinea today, especially with the presence of Mr. Nkrumah in the Guinean Government?

Mr. WILLIAMS. [Security deletion.] Mr. MURPHY. Why was [security deletion] million scheduled in the fiscal year 1967 for supporting assistance? Why this particular kind of aid there?

Mr. WILLIAMS. This particular kind of aid-maybe Mr. Hutchinson can speak to it better than I-I think the principal problem in Guinea is that their economy has been in rather desperate straits. They don't have a really negotiable currency. They do not have sufficient goods on their shelves so that there is a continuous tendency therefore for inflation.

And we do have interest there, there are American firms interested in the bauxite developments [security deletion]. 

Mr. MURPHY. Thank you.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Broomfield. 

Mr. BROOMFIELD. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

Governor Williams, following up Congressman Murphy's questioning, on page 5 of your statement you list these countries of Africa that have been taken over by the military. You also cite the reasons why in many of these countries it has occurred. Is it the military that is actually making possible some progress toward democracy? 

Mr. WILLIAMS. I think the military has, for the time being, given them an opportunity to have a degree of stability which permits them to get their economies going and doesn't provide democracy, but does provide them with a bridge toward it. 

Mr. BROOMFIELD. Is there any indication in these countries that have these military coups that they are planning elections in the near future? 

Mr. WILLIAMS. Not in the immediate future. In some of these countries they do indicate they hope to turn over to civil governments. 

[Security deletion.] 

Mr. BROOMFIELD. Mr. Hutchinson, in the briefing book on page 215, regarding the Sudan, it says that the Sudan broke diplomatic relations with Western Germany, its major donor, and with the United Kingdom. The United States plans to use its influence in the assistance program to help bring the Sudan to a sounder basis through development efforts.

My question is, Why should the United States rush in to take up the slack being caused by the Sudan, and what influence does the United States have, other than assistance, to help sounder development efforts? 

Mr. HUTCHINSON. Sir, I certainly agree with the thrust of your point, that the United States should not rush in to pick up the slack that was left when the Sudanese broke relations with the Germans. 

In fact, we have not done so. 

What the reference there is intended to convey is that we are reappraising our aid program in the Sudan with a view to seeing whether bladdilar

we can make it more directly related to some of the particular developmental problems they are facing, more so than it is at the present time, and whether or not we can, in conjunction with others [security deletion]. But again the Governor could speak to this more specifically than I. 

Mr. BROOMFIELD. Would you like to comment, Governor?

Mr. WILLIAMS. In the Sudan, as you know, they have just gone through a change in government from a military government to a civilian government.

In the course of this the Communist Party played an important role and for a time looked as though they would seize the government. 

However, the government that is the present force was successful in isolating the thrust of a minority, but well-organized minority, that had crowds in the street and so on for the Communists. Consequently they have worked out a government which is rather middle of the road in its orientation.

By keeping up normal activities and providing a friendly relationship with them we have, in fact, strengthened their tendency to move in the direction that I have already indicated. 

Mr. BROOMFIELD. Mr. Hutchinson, the countries of Germany and 

Great Britain-there is apparently some consultation by our Government as to their aid programs. How effective are these meetings? 

Do they accomplish much? 

Mr. HUTCHINSON. I beg your pardon? 

Mr. BROOMFIELD. The consultations, in other words, the kind of meshing in of the programs that are being put forth by the Germans as well as the British?

Mr. HUTCHINSON. These consultations are at two levels, one in the field and one with our counterparts in London and in Bonn. 

In the first instance these were general exchanges of information which were helpful, but didn't accomplish too much in a pragmatic way. It has led us to a situation which over the last 2 or 3 months has enabled us to have specific discussions with them on the policy toward the country, what we want to expect of the country, and the kinds of things that we will do. 

This has been particularly helpful in Tunisia, in the Ghana situation, and it is even leading to joint financing of projects in some instances. We are hopeful for a good bit more of that in the future. 

Mr. BROOMFIELD. Mr. Hutchinson, in response to a question, I think directed by Mr. O'Hara of Illinois, I wondered if I understood you correctly about the aid to South Africa as far as your response was just for economic aid.

It had nothing to do with the military aspect as far as sales? 

Mr. HUTCHINSON. It had nothing whatsoever to do with that, sir. 

Mr. BROOMFIELD. Since 1961 through 1965, if I read these reports correctly, at least receipts from military sales to South Africa, totals some $6 million.

Mr. WILLIAMS. Commercial sales; but we did, of course, cut off the sale of all military goods to South Africa about 2 years ago. The only military shipments made after 1963 were for items committed under existing contracts made prior to the 1963 arms embargo. 

Mr. BROOMFIELD. With these military takeovers as you mentioned,

Governor Williams-probably I should withhold this question until

we have the military people-but I wondered if you were aware of any of these governments that have been taken over by the military, whether we are furnishing any military assistance?

Mr. WILLIAMS. In some of them, I can't give you the exact ones, we are supplying some military training, some transportation, jeeps and things of that sort, and some communications equipment. But the total amount involved is very small, indeed. 

Mr. HUTCHINSON. I would be glad to give it to you. (A classified memorandum was subsequently supplied in response to Mr. Broomfield.)

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Fraser. 

Mr. FRASER. Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

Governor Williams, I want to say that during your tenure as 

Assistant Secretary for African Affairs, as far as I am concerned, the policies of our Government have been on target. I can't say that about the other continents of the world, but I can say that about Africa. 

I think our policies have been enlightened, sensibly prudent and restrained. I know that you had much to do with this. 

I am confident history will bear out the wisdom you have brought to this job and the contribution you have made. 

Mr. Hutchinson, on the question of agricultural development, what capabilities does AID have to assist in agricultural development outside of technical advisers at the top level? 

Mr. HUTCHINSON. We have the ability to provide capital funds for equipment requirements, for the development of the infrastructure that is essential to agricultural development such as roads, water, harbors, this sort of thing, and then we in addition are able to call upon the resources of the Department of Agriculture under participating agency arrangements, with the university community, and with the remainder of the private community on a contract basis. 

We are using this very, very widely, sir. 

Mr. FRASER. That is all the capability that you require in order to effectively help these countries? 

I am talking about the agricultural sector, now.

Mr. HUTCHINSON. There is a need, sir, for a greater supply of trained personnel in the first instance. It is one of the greatest drags on our ability to provide the kind of assistance that we need. 

There is also a need for greater knowledge in the United States, in order to provide the assistance There is a need for—————— 

Mr. FRASER. On that point, what are we doing about that?

Mr. HUTCHINSON. We are doing a number of things. This is one of the purposes, as I think you are aware, of the new bill- Mr. FRASER. On international education. That will produce more university-trained people.

Mr. HUTCHINSON. This is one of the purposes of the bill.

Mr. HUTCHINSON. We need more managers, more economic technicians, economists, almost you name it; the personnel lists are very, very tight in all of these areas. 

Mr. FRASER. What are we doing about getting these other kinds of personnel?

Mr. HUTCHINSON. What we are trying to do is tap the private community to the maximum extent of our stability. We are also running some training courses on our own account, sending people to universities and training schools.

Mr. FRASER. I have the impression that our capabilities in agricultural development are very limited in middle-level manpower. I don't see anything happening to change that. Why doesn't AID, for example, have a rural development corps which provides a latticework in which people could make careers in international agricultural development, in rural development? 

Mr. HUTCHINSON. Sir, I certainly agree with you that our abilities here are limited. There is no doubt of it. As far as a corps is concerned, we do have a career for agricultural people within the Agency. And people are making a career out of it. 

There is consideration being given to the possibility of the establishment of specific corps directly related to specialties and so on. 

This may help us, if it turns out that the personnel people decide, and the Congress decides that this is a sensible approach. 

We do have careers in the Agency now in these fields. 

Mr. FRASER. What is the general food situation in Africa? Are they running behind with the population increase? I was noticing here in Nigeria, the production index per capita seems to be holding about even. Is that characteristic of the African nations? 

Mr. HUTCHINSON. If you were going to generalize, I would say it is characteristic. Africa by and large is not a continent which is faced with imminent starvation and with enormous population pressure. 

Nevertheless, there are areas in which this is becoming a quite serious problem. North Africa is a case in point. You have to make these exceptions.

Mr. WILLIAMS. There is a great protein shortage too, sir. It is not only the amount, but also the type of food available.

QUESTION OF ILLEGAL SHIPMENT OF CHROME FROM RHODESIA TO BRITAIN 

The question of the legality of the chromite shipment from Rhodesia on the SS Bingo was raised with the British Government during early February. The 

British Government assured the Department of State that no violation of British embargo regulations had taken place in this case.

I think certainly in some of these countries, Ghana particularly, that the military takeover will have beneficial results. Perhaps it will in the other countries too. There has been some indication in Nigeria, for example, that the business of government is moving ahead more rapidly, that many of the people of the country feel better about their Government and we hope there will be progress. 

Mr. HAMILTON. Do you think across Africa generally there are developing institutions and people so that we can reasonably hope for democratic governments in Africa? 

Mr. WILLIAMS. I think they are moving in that direction. I think today we think a democratic government is one including many facets. 

We used to think of a democratic government as including only the right to vote. Then we had additional civil rights that we added on to it and more recently we have been thinking about a man's right to a living. To get all of these at once is beyond the powers of these countries. They are trying to get such of these rights as they can. 

I think in some of the countries like Tunisia and Nigeria, the Ivory Coast, they will be able to get a group of these rights fairly early. In other countries it will take a much longer time.

Mr. WILLIAMS. More money would be more useful, but I think we can show you a record of individual projects on these $25,000 or $50,000 that the local AID director and Ambassador have had that have been very useful projects. In some instances they have been able to get a water system going in a local community, such as a well and some piping. They have been able to do a number of things which have created good will for the United States, all out of proportion to the dollars spent, and have given the local people somewhat better standards of living which they appreciated very much. 

I think it is absolutely essential that we do maintain in each of these countries some sort of aid relationship because it does improve the relations between our countries, does give them a feeling that they

are independent and separate countries, gives them some sense of national importance and finally most of these programs do perform a specific benefit for the individuals.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Zablocki. 

Mr. Fulton. 

Mr. FULTON. We are glad to have you here.

I would like to know for the record, what effect generally did the passage of the U.S. Civil Rights program have on the people of 

Africa and as to our assistance? (The following has been supplied for record:)

EFFECT OF THE PASSAGE OF THE CIVIL RIGHTS PROGRAM ON THE PEOPLE OF AFRICA AND OUR ASSISTANCE 

Passage of the Civil Rights Act has had a very salutory effect on the people of Africa where there is a special interest in progress toward full rights for all Americans. This is true not only because one-tenth of our Nation has African blood, but also because we share with Africa a common outlook toward freedom as exemplified in the philosophy of our Declaration of Independence. Thus, our recognition of the rights of men to freedom and dignity have had a broad impact on modern Africa and all Africans are in sympathy with the struggle of Negro Americans to achieve their full and equal rights. Africans are quick to applaud us when we take a major step forward, such as the recent passage of the Civil Rights Act. At the same time, they find it difficult to understand that progress is truly being made when they read of eruptions of racial discrimination in this country.

In our country, where our citizens tend to develop close cultural and other ties with the lands of their ancestors' birth, there is a healthy flourishing community of interest between Africans and Americans. 

John Davis of the American Society of African Culture has described the relationship between African freedom and full civil rights for Negro Americans: "The American Negro has perceived African liberation and the abolishment of caste restrictions in America to be one and the same process, and there has resulted an identity of feeling, a quickening of protest, a feeling of comradeship in the common struggle. This sense more than anything else explains the current identity of the American Negro with Africa and of Africans with the 

American Negro."

The progress in this country to insure equality of all citizens is recognized by the Africans who expect this progress to erase all forms of discrimination. 

At the same time that our aid program demonstrates to Africans U.S. interest and assistance in their progress, this would not be convincing to them if we did not have progress in civil rights. 

Mr. FULTON. Second, what is the general feeling of the African peoples as distinguished from the African governments, and their attitude on U.S. aid programs, our loans and our methods. 

(The information follows:) AFRICAN ATTITUDE TOWARD AMERICAN ASSISTANCE 

On the question of the attitude of the African public toward American interest in general, the United States does not have comprehensive opinion surveys or other devices which would permit a complete answer to this question. The general impression of most observers is that the feeling of the African people about the American interest in them is very positive and favorable. Evidence to support this point includes wide African press coverage of American aid activities; widespread public interest in one major country of a series of films on aid projects which were shown on television stations, in all schools, and in motion picture theaters; and similar reception reported to other films portraying particular U.S. aid projects.

Recent public opinion polls in a large African country show that the people of the country (the samples in one survey involved "literate citizens in a major city" and in the other, students at two large African universities) overwhelm-

ingly expressed esteem for the United States. Substantial majorities felt that the United States and the country concerned had common interests and looked favorably on U.S. efforts in Africa. The favorable opinions of the United States were almost entirely attributed to American aid to African states, with AID financial and educational assistance and Peace Corps assistance being singled out as especially influential. While U.S. civil rights problems provided a strong negative slant in the polls, this situation in the United States was seen as improving and the role of the U.S. Federal Government was strongly favored. 

With respect to the attitude of the African public on specific American loans or other aid projects, it is probably correct that the public in general is not aware of these activities in the preliminary planning and negotiation stages until the actual physical work and accomplishments start to become apparent in their vicinity as through new school buildings, the availability of new agricultural methods, supply of pure drinking water, vaccination of children against disease, etc. Then the reaction is favorable, with the degree of favor being pretty much in direct proportion to the personal effect on the individuals themselves. 

With respect to U.S. aid methods on such points as U.S. use of loans versus grants, the source of procurement, competitive bidding, the use of preliminary surveys before starting major projects and similar aspects of method and procedure, there is not much evidence that the public in general is either aware of or concerned with these questions. Their interest is in the results, not the methods though Government officials concerned with aid activities do have a keen concern with the methods.

The fact that most of the aid program in Africa consists of specific visible projects rather than general commodity imports or financial aid probably contributes to a heightened awareness of aid by the public in general. In a few countries the whole program is designed primarily with that objective in mind. 

Throughout Africa a very large part of total U.S. aid is in the form of technical assistance activities which involve much individual contact in those parts of the country and with the particular population groups affected. This is especially true of the small "self-help" funds administered locally by our missions or embassies in each country. The markings on aid commodities and equipment and the signs erected at each project site also contribute to this awareness as do the substantial Public Law 480 food for work projects in a number of countries and the Public Law 480 food distributed to needy groups through voluntary agencies in almost every country.

Mr. FULTON. I do want to comment on U.S. policy that I think is a move in the right direction. That is toward regional agencies and working on a multination basis in Africa. For example, the United 

Nations Economic Commission for Africa. 

When I was a U.S. delegate to the 14th General Assembly of the 

United Nations in 1959, then the U.S. State Department policy was to cut the total authorized annual budget for the United Nations Economic Commission for Africa by 20 percent. It was my cosponsoring an amendment, that restored this budget, because I feel that these regional agencies are vital to the future development of Africa. They are a necessary step as well as the country-by-country assistance that AID gives. 

Could you comment shortly on Mr. Robert Gardiner, the Secretary's work, and also on the establishment of the Iron and Steel Authority under the United Nations Economic Commission for Africa which I understand is now in process. You can put a statement in the record. 

(The information follows:) WORK OF ROBERT K. A. GARDINER, EXECUTIVE SECRETARY OF THE ECONOMIC COMMISSION FOR AFRICA Robert K. A. Gardiner as Executive Secretary of the Economic Commission for Africa is responsible for furthering economic and social cooperation in Africa. His staff, headquartered in Addis Ababa, consists of U.N. personnel plus

various other technicians on detail from U.N. specialized agencies such as the 

FAO, UNESCO, ILO, and ITU.

In planning for economic and social cooperation among the African states, the ECA Secretariat concentrates primarily on building intra-African institutions, training personnel to run those institutions, advising governments in drawing up and executing their development programs. It also conducts investigations at the request of individual governments or states although most of its effort is in multinational projects. Thirty-six African states are members of the ECA. In discharging his responsibilities, Mr. Gardiner maintains close contact with the leaders and planning authorities of ECA's member states. 

Under Gardiner's leadership, the ECA has encouraged African states in regional economic cooperation as a means of accelerating economic and social development.

The ECA played a leading role in the planning which resulted in the establishment of the African Development Bank and in stimulating consideration of a coordinated approach to the development of industries by member states in the various African subregions. 

THE IRON AND STEEL AUTHORITY 

West African States are being encouraged by the Economic Commission for 

Africa to establish a West African Iron and Steel Authority (WAISA) to coor dinate their planning and implementation activities for the introduction of one or more steel complexes into the area to serve a multistate or subregional market. 

Present West African interest in WAISA may be traced to an ECA sponsored iron and steel conference in Monrovia in October 1963. This was followed by another conference in Monrovia in August 1965 of West African States called by Liberia, with ECA's support. This conference, which was attended by 9 of the 

14 West African States, decided to create an Interim Committee of Experts which subsequently met in Abidjan in October 1965, with observers from the ECA and the African Development Bank in attendance. It reviewed and discussed preliminary reports on technical matters such as steel pricing policies that were within its terms of reference, and agreed that WAISA would be subject to the juridical control of a West African intergovernmental machinery for coordinating general industrial development. It also requested West African States to supply their respective current plans or programs for a local steel industry and invited their views on their possible financial participation in, and most suitable method of financing of, any new iron and steel venture. Discussion of the constitution, powers and functions of WAISA was deferred by the Interim Committee of Experts until the March 1966 meeting in Sierra Leone. Meanwhile, the 

ECA has provided some technical support of the committee. No report is available on the outcome of the Sierra Leone meeting as yet. No Africa-wide iron and steel authority exists.

Mr. FULTON. The other point I would like to make is if we are going to have regional development as we have found satisfactory in other parts of the world, then I would like to know what would be in prospect of using our United Nations Economic Commission for Africafor example, in communications, in transportation, as well as in manufacturing? 

(The information follows:) PROSPECTS FOR U.S. USE OF UNECA FOR COMMUNICATIONS, TRANSPORTATION AND INDUSTRIAL DEVELOPMENT 

The ECA is essentially a development planning body, stimulating African states to consider ways of promoting economic and social progress cooperatively in a wide variety of specialized fields. Through its program of regional conferences on industrial coordination, it has focused the attention of African states upon the limitations that the small national markets and limited national resources place upon their industrial development prospects. It has encouraged the consideration of merging national markets into larger trading areas and the coordination of national industrial development plans to serve such markets in order to secure economics of capital investment and reduced production costs 

---

associated with larger scale industrial plants. Considerable progress has been made toward the establishment of an Economic Community of Eastern Africa and conferences with this objective are scheduled to be held in other subregions this year. ECA has been successful in creating the framework for a number of autonomous regional institutions that are operational or in the process of becoming so. These include the Dakar Institute for Economic Development and Planning (a regional training body), the Senegal River Basin Commission, the Niger River Commission, Chad Basin Commission, and the African Development Bank. 

In the industry area, the ECA, has, through its staff experts or through outside consultants under contract, prepared a number of industry surveys, all of which are in the nature of pre-feasibility studies, requiring further technical and economic investigation to be useful for capital investment purposes. 

The ECA has been engaged in preparing plans, with the assistance of the technicians provided by the International Telecommunications Union, for an African telecommunications network to improve communications between all 

African capitals. Recently, ECA sponsored a conference of African experts in

Addis jointly with the Organization for African Unity to spur renewed African efforts to develop intra-African telecommunications links.

In the transportation sector, the ECA has enlisted the interest of a number of European donors in preparing surveys of the transport needs of the various African subregions. West German experts have completed a survey of a six-state area in the west African subregion while the French Government has agreed to survey the remainder of that subregion. Belgium has begun a study of the 

Central African subregion, and an Italian study of Somalia, Ethiopia, and the Sudan is well underway. The United Kingdom is understood to be arranging for surveys in the east African countries. The ECA plans to coordinate these reports into an overall report on African transportation needs, and the United States has agreed to help recruit a U.S. transportation expert to assist it in this task. 

The United States in principle supports regional cooperation leading to greater economic development and is prepared to encourage ECA planning activities along lines that are constructive, realistic, and feasible. Support is now provided through AID financed studies and technical assistance. The areas, in which future U.S. support is likely, include the coordination of African transportation, the promotion of an intra-African telecommunications network, and the encouragement of the coordinated development of industry as well as other fields such as agriculture and education. 

The Economic Commission for Africa is not presently equipped to make project evaluations or to perform operational activities. The United States considers its greatest value to be in the field of stimulating and coordinating action steps by African countries or other African regional bodies, acting as a catalyst. We understand that ECA itself looks to the subregional institutions and member states to carry out the action phases of development. Accordingly, although AID can support ECA's development planning efforts, it is anticipated that AID will work primarily either with the African Development Bank, with African subregional institutions, or with African states bilaterally in implementing any projects growing out of ECA plans in the telecommunications, transportation, industry, and other sectors that warrant support as a result of our further investigations.

LOCAL CURRENCY PROGRAMS FOR INDIA, FISCAL YEARS 1965 AND 1966 

In fiscal year 1965, local currency obligations for release to India for economic development purposes were $236.5 million worth of rupees; $92.8 million equivalent were for agricultural programs, $96.3 million equivalent for power and industry projects, and $47.4 million equivalent for education. Major agriculture projects and programs were multipurpose irrigation, flood control and power programs, Rihand Valley development, Agricultural Refinance Corporation, and foodgrains storage.

In fiscal year 1966, obligations of local currency will reach about $260 million equivalent an increase over fiscal year 1965 of $23 million equivalent. Agriculture will utilize about $93.6 million equivalent, $37.7 million equivalent will finance power and equipment maintenance training, about $98.5 million equivalent for education programs, and $30 million equivalent to support malaria eradication and other health programs.

The planned release of rupees in fiscal year 1966 for agriculture programs will support agricultural universities, rural public works schemes, and soil and water conservation projects. The relatively limited ability of the agriculture sector to rapidly absorb large increases in inputs, the Government of India's priority program to increase its own resources allocation to agriculture, and the time period required to develop sound, viable project activities have to some degree limited the speed with which AID has been able to step up its local currency inputs to agriculture this year.

Several fertilizer plant proposals are under consideration by the Government of India and AID. In the case of private sector plants involving U.S. private investment, AID will finance the local costs through a Cooley loan, as has been done in the case of the Coromandel fertilizer plant ($28 million equivalent).

ECA X-86-1, authorized providing up to $190,000,000 in wheat assistance to India on credit terms in accordance with the provisions of sec. III(c) of the Economic Cooperation Act of 1948. The loan agreement originally provided for semiannual repayment over a 35-year period with the 1st repayment on June 30, 1957. 

Interest was to be paid at 22 percent beginning in 1952. Payments of $3,417,363.28 in principal and $28,321,270.05 in interest were made by the Indian Government through June 30, 1958. The Government of India then requested a moratorium on such payments because of the heavy burden of servicing her external indebtedness. The United States agreed to defer payments of interest and principal due on the loan for 9½ years, payments to be resumed on Dec. 31, 1967.
