THURSDAY, MARCH 24, 1966 HOUSE OF REPRESENTATIVES, COMMITTEE OF FOREIGN AFFAIRS, 

Washington, D.O.

The committee met, pursuant to adjournment, at 10:25 a.m., in room 

2172, Rayburn Building, Hon. Thomas E. Morgan (chairman) presiding.

The committee meets this morning in executive session for a continuation of the hearings on H.R. 12449 and H.R. 12450. Before we begin the hearings this morning, I want unanimous consent to file the report on the American Research Children's Hospital in Krakow, Poland, as a House report.

If there is no objection, it is so ordered. 

Our witnesses this morning are the Honorable William Bundy, Assistant Secretary of State for Far Eastern Affairs, and the Honorable Rutherford M. Poats, Assistant Administrator for Far East, Agency for International Development. 

Mr. Secretary, you have a prepared statement, and you may proceed, sir.

STATEMENT OF HON. WILLIAM P. BUNDY, ASSISTANT SECRETARY OF STATE FOR FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS 

Mr. BUNDY. Mr. Chairman, it is a great pleasure to appear again before this committee on this matter. This is my sixth year in appearing before this committee. I have always valued the really great attention and knowledge that you have.

If I may, Mr. Chairman, I will submit my statement for the record and confine myself to a very short highlight oral summary, if that is satisfactory to you. 

Chairman MORGAN. Without objection, it is so ordered. (The statement of Mr. Bundy is as follows:)

STATEMENT OF HON. WILLIAM P. BUNDY, 'ASSISTANT SECRETARY OF STATE FOR FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS 

It is a great privilege for me to appear before your committee in support of the President's foreign aid program for fiscal year 1967. 

In his message of February 1, the President has said about these programs: "It is *** the fateful truth of our age * * * that our personal and national hopes hang in a balance affected by events and attitudes half a world away * * The human misery which infects whole nations with a thirst for violent change does not give way to mere slogans * * *. The works of peace require courage and foresight. The need knows neither national boundary nor narrow ideology.' 99 181

Few of the things we-the United States-do in the world today could be more important. This is particularly true in Asia-a region of the world in the grip of revolutionary change.

I would like to discuss with you this morning how our economic and military assistance programs contribute to, and why they are vital for, the achievement of our political and strategic objectives in Asia.

PROPOSED PROGRAMS FOR THE FAR EAST 

The economic programs we propose for the Far East in fiscal year 1967 total $799.1 million. They represent nearly one-third of the total proposed prorgam of economic assistance. The major components of these programs are: Vietnam

The amount of $550 million to support the military effort against the Vietcong and carry forward programs of social and economic reform and reconstruction. Korea [Security deletion] to support that country's encouraging progress toward a viable, self-sustaining economy. 

Thailand and Laos [Security deletion] essentially to support programs of rural welfare and development in Laos and northeast Thailand and assist in the stabilization of the 

Lao economy burdened by heavy outlays for defense. Other [Security deletion] to support rural development programs in the Philippines and certain regional programs in southeast Asia. One of the most important of these latter are phase II of a preinvestment feasibility study of Pa Mong, the large multipurpose dam proposed on the Mekong between northern Laos and Thailand. [Security deletion.] 

In his statement to this committee, the Secretary has already discussed the proposed title for multilateral and regional programs in southeast Asia. As the 

Secretary indicated, we do not contemplate, at this time, a request for appropriation of funds. The intent of this new title is to provide an authorization that will make it possible to carry forward the realization of the President's vision stated in his speech at Johns Hopkins University on April 7 of last year and reaffirmed many times since then-the vision of a great international cooperative effort, responsive to initiatives from the Asians themselves, designed to accelerate the social and economic development of southeast Asia-an effort which, in the 

President's words, "would replace despair with hope and terror with progress."

This new title will authorize assistance in southeast Asia through multilateral institutions, and to projects serving regional development needs, such as special funds under the new Asian Development Bank to promote agricultural development and education. Passage of this title will confirm the sense of Congress that rapid social and economic progress in southeast Asia is as important an objective of U.S. policy as our military effort to make aggression unprofitable in Vietnam. 

I should like, at this point, to say a word about the separate military assistance and sales program. These programs, totaling $387.3 million, make a vital contribution to the security of the Far East. They enable the free countries of Asia-Korea, the Republic of China [security deletion], Thailand--to main tain military forces far larger than those they could support from their own resources. The proposed military assistance program complements our efforts in the economic field and plays a vital role in the maintenance of the military power in the Far East. I urge its approval. 

​U.S. POLICY IN THE FAR EAST 

The single most important development in the Far East this past year has been the escalation of Communist aggression in Vietnam and the response of the United States. It is clear that this aggression is not just another attempt by a neighbor to the north to gain by force the domination that it was unable to achieve by peaceful purposes. All we know about Communist Chinese motives and thinking confirms that Peking considers the conflict in Vietnam a test case of its version of world revolution-of "wars of national liberation"-which it hopes will sweep the underdeveloped world. If Communist aggression succeeds

in Vietnam, it

Communist will encourage the partisans of violent political change in the to seek to extend their particular method of instilling communism all over the underdeveloped world. This is the historic significance and dimension of our struggle in Vietnam. 

What are the objectives which our assistance programs serve?

The objectives of our policy in the Far East are clear. They are, as they have been for many years— 1. To support with U.S. resources, both military and economic, the effort to persuade Peking and Hanoi that aggression is unprofitable. 

2. To persuade the governments and peoples of the region that by associating themselves with other countries in the non-Communist world they can insure their national safety and improve the prospects of achieving their social, economic, and political aspirations. 

3. To endorse and assist needed social and economic reforms, offering the peoples a constructive alternative to the Communist model and holding out hope that the achievement of social justice and economic progress does not require violent revolution. 

4. To support the efforts of governments and peoples in the region to create economically viable, self-confident, and politically stable societies. 

5. To encourage a sense of regional solidarity which will lead to increasing cooperation among the countries of the area-economic, political, and military.

In historical perspective, we face in Asia today, as in other parts of the world, a revolutionary situation. It is an environment in which nationalism and the compulsion to modernize have become deeply rooted. The ultimate objective of our assistance policy is to channel this compulsion to modernize into constructive channels to achieve, in the Secretary's words, "revolution in freedomharnessing and carrying out the revolution of modernization without sacrifice of independence and freedom."

This summary of the focus of our assistance strategy also defines the substance of our policy for dealing with Communist China's threat to the security of free Asia. The object of our policy is to hold the frontier of freedom. To secure this frontier, we want to help the peoples of the area build a community of viable, self-confident, and independent nations, dominated by no one, immune alike to the blandishments of Peking's destructive ideology and to its threats of violence and subversion. 

DEVELOPMENTS IN THE FAR EAST 

I have just recently returned from an extensive trip through the Far East. 

I was much encouraged by what I saw. I was struck once again by the vital contribution which our assistance programs make to stability and progress of free Asia. I was impressed that, while the fight for freedom continues in Vietnam, there are many encouraging signs pointing toward a better future-greater political stability, progress toward economic self-reliance, and an evolving sense of community among the nations of the area. Let me mention just a few of these developments. 

1. On my trip I found a new sense of confidence among the leaders and the peoples of the area-a sense of confidence growing from the conviction that we will remain in Vietnam as long as necessary to bring a halt to Communist aggression and that we will assist other nations with all the resources necessary to thwart Communist designs for subversion of the area. 

2. Economic expansion of the Far East continues. Per capita income continues to rise at an encouraging rate in such countries as South Korea, Thailand, Malaysia, and Taiwan, resulting in slow but steady improvement of the standard of living of the people. 

3. Commercial and cultural ties are gradually becoming stronger among the countries of free Asia. There is increased interchange of peoples and ideas. 

Trade is expanding steadily-among the countries of southeast Asia as well as between these countries and Australia and Japan. 

4. A new group of Asian leaders is rising, interested in the possibilities of regional cooperation. The substantial oversubscription by regional countries to the capital of the Asian Development Bank is one manifestation of this increasing interest. The growing concern with the activities of ECAFE-such as the 

Mekong Coordinating Committee and the proposed Asian Council for Industrial

Development the meetings of southeast Asian central bankers to review common problems of economic development, and the Korean proposal for a Far East 

Foreign Minister's Conference, are others. 

5. Most importantly, throughout the Far East and particularly in Japan, there is today a better awareness among the peoples of their stake in the struggle in Vietnam, the causes of the conflict and the U.S. position. 

This brings me to the problem of Japan's role in the Far East. I believe that in many ways we may be standing today at a watershed in Japanese attitudes and policies in the postwar period. The Japanese people are regaining their national self-confidence, and on the basis of a prosperous economy at home are increasingly turning their attention abroad. Prime Minister Sato and the Ministers of his government have spoken publicly in recent months of the need for Japan to adopt a more positive role in support of Free World goals in Asia and elsewhere. Japan's distinguished chairmanship of the U.N. Security Council during February was a symbol of its new role on the world stage. 

Japan's leaders recognize the special importance of Japan's role in Asia. 

While a very large portion of Japan's population continues to oppose any Japanese military involvement in Asia, there is today among Japan's leaders a growing interest in the future of southeast Asia. These leaders recognize the strategic importance of this region to Japan's national security. They are cognizant of the economic complementarities between their country and southeast Asia. Long preoccupied with the tasks of economic reconstruction and development at home, they are increasingly turning their attention to the problem of how Japan can contribute to meeting the development needs of the area and to the creation of conditions favorable to political stability; and how new commitments of Japanese resources to this end should be balanced against Japan's own domestic requirements in the economic and social field. 

Japan today provides considerable aid in Vietnam-medical supplies, relief to refugees, and long-term development assistance. It participates actively in the work of the Mekong Committee. It has formed the Overseas Volunteer 

Service Corps, the Japanese equivalent of our Peace Corps. 

It has normalized relations with Korea and agreed to provide, over the next 10 years, up to $800 million in public and private capital resources to that country. 

It has subscribed $200 million to the capital stock of the Asian Development Bank, a subscription equal to that of the United States, and a Japanese is a prominent contender for the presidency of the Bank, It has called a ministerial meeting of regional countries for April 6-7 to discuss the development prospects of southeast Asia and is considering new initiatives in agriculture and port development. 

In sum, we believe that the outlook for an active role by Japan in the search for solutions to the problems of the area is increasingly hopeful. 

I now would like to discuss with you briefly our policy and assistance strategy in some of the major aid-receiving countries of the area. Vietnam Our policy in Vietnam has been examined extensively before this committee only a short time ago. I therefore can be very brief. 

The war in Vietnam is a political as much as it is a military struggle. Without the support of the people, the Vietnamese Government and the United States could win all the military battles and still lose the political war. Aid to the peasantry and maintenance of urban living standards are essential components of our efforts to counter and defeat the Vietcong. These aid efforts also are a concrete expression of the concern of free societies for individual human life and of our determination, with the South Vietnamese Government, to improve the life of the people.

In the declaration of Honolulu, the Government of South Vietnam and the United States rededicated themselves to new efforts, even while the military conflict continues, to eradicate social injustice, establish and maintain a stable, viable economy, and commence the building of a better material life for the people of Vietnam. Since 1962 AID programs have been progressively increased and expanded to give ever greater emphasis to these objectives. The visits of the 

Vice President, of Secretaries Freeman and Gardner and their groups of experts to Vietnam have translated the discussions at Honolulu into specific recommendations in the fields of agriculture, health and education. Work is now underway on the best methods of approach for carrying forward these recommendations. 

While this work is going forward, we and the Government of Vietnam have also embarked on a broad program to contain the inflationary pressures stem-

ming from increased Vietnamese and United States spending and the interference with the country's production and distribution system by the Vietcong. 

To counter these pressures, we are financing a greatly enlarged commercial import program. For example, we have imported large quantities of rice. 

The Government of Vietnam, under a parallel program, does its part to increase commercial imports from its own exchange resources. We are continuing discussions with the Vietnamese Government on a series of other measures designed to curtail inflation, including internal tax increase. Korea U.S. economic assistance remains vital to Korea's progress toward a viable self-supporting economy. U.S. assistance enables Korea to maintain the momentum of economic growth while also supporting one of the largest military establishments in the Far East.

I will summarize Korea's situation under three headings: 

Economic.-Korea has demonstrated the capacity to utilize its economic potential effectively and to progress through its own efforts. 

At present the Korean Government is effectively carrying out a broad economic stabilization program. It has reformed its foreign exchange system and adjusted its interest rate structure. It is balancing its budget, has restricted credit, limits increases in the money supply, and vigorously promotes exports. 

The results of these policies are reflected in its economic growth. Industrial production rose by 16 percent in 1965; exports, valued at $180 million, were 50 percent larger than in 1964; domestic savings, less than 1 percent of GNP in 1962, rose to 8 percent in 1965; price increases were substantially brought under control after years of inflation; real growth has averaged almost 9 percent in each of the last 3 years.

This satisfactory growth has been reflected in Korea's assistance requirements. These requirements have slowly tended to decline and are now significantly below the levels of, say 1958-59. Reflecting Korea's growing economic strength, there has been a gradual shift from grant to loan assistance. 

Military.-Korea plays an increasingly important role in the overall security position of the Far East. Its [security deletion] army division and [security deletion] air force fighter squadrons are an important deterrent to aggression from the north. One of these divisions now fights in Vietnam, a second will be deployed there by the Government of Korea later this year. Maintenance of these forces far exceeds Korea's own capacities. 

Political and diplomatic.-Notable progress has also been made by Korea in its foreign relations. On December 18, 1965, Korea exchanged instruments of ratification of a basic relations treaty with Japan. Under the accompanying economic agreements, a flow to Korea of some $800 million in public and private capital over the next 10 years is assured, beginning this year. Korea is also playing an active role in promoting regional cooperation in free Asia and has strengthened its relations with Africa and Western European countries. Domestically, the disturbances of last summer over the settlement with Japan have given way to a period of political stability. 

In sum the outlook for Korea's political and economic institutions is in- .creasingly hopeful. Korea is making a vigorous attack on its problems but we must take care that our aid remain at levels adequate to enable these efforts to succeed. 

Republic of China

The political situation in Taiwan remains stable. The cohesion of Chiang 

Kai-shek's government has been demonstrated by his reelection on March 21 for a fourth 6-year term as President. Seventy-eight years of age, he chose as his running mate Premier C. K. Yen, the youngest (61) of the candidates for Vice President. The National Assembly-which has the power to amend the Constitution as well as to elect the President and Vice President-just prior to Chiang's reelection granted extensive powers to the President to reorganize the executive branch of the Government. How these powers will be used remains to be seen.

Taiwan's remarkable economic progress continues. The mainsprings of this growth are the modernization and diversification of Taiwan's agriculture, the rapid increase in agricultural productivity, and a rapidly expanding and diversifying industrial base. Real GNP has now for several years been increasing at an annual rate of 8 percent. Exports have reached new highs-$491 million

in 1965-and borrowing from abroad on conventional terms has replaced U.S.

concessional aid. [Security deletion.] The Government of the Republic of 

China has commenced its own economic assistance program in such countries as the new African states, where it is able to contribute valuable technical assistance to agricultural development. 

Nevertheless, there remain potential weaknesses in the economic position of the Government of the Republic of China which should not be overlooked. I shall mention here only three. 

1. The termination of U.S. economic aid, which had benefited Taiwan's balance of payments by roughly $80 million a year, imposes a considerable burden of adjustment on Taiwan's economy. The inflow of private capital and international loans will mitigate but not relieve altogether the need for this adjustment. 

2. World market prices for Taiwan's principal export item-sugar-are weak. 

3. The Government of the Republic of China carries one of the heaviest defense burdens in the Far East. [Security deletion.] 

Under the circumstances, the continuation of military assistance remains of vital importance. U.S. assistance is being used to rebuild and strengthen the 

Government of the Republic of China military forces for the defense of those areas to which the Government of the Republic of China and the United States are committed under the Mutual Defense Treaty of 1954. [Security deletion.] 

It will insure that the military burden carried by the Government of the Republic of China will not reach a level endangering the social and economic progress on Taiwan-those advances which continue to serve as a reminder to the mainland of the benefits of freedom. Laos Laos, one of the least developed countries in the world, is the third largest recipient of U.S. economic aid in the Far East. [Security deletion.] 

The objective of our policy in Laos is to preserve the independence and integrity of the Government in Vientiane, help it maintain security in key areas of the country, maintain the allegiance of its people by economic and social welfare programs, and create the conditions for economic development. 

The objective of the 1962 Geneva agreements was to reestablish peace. It became quickly clear, however, that the Pathet Lao and its supporters, the North Vietnamese, were unwilling to live up to their commitments. North 

Vietnamese support for the Pathet Lao continued and prevented a government of national reconciliation as envisaged in these agreements. On the contrary, it enabled the Pathet Lao to seize substantial territory along the borders of North Vietnam. North Vietnam, in violation of the 1962 agreements, introduced its own troops into Laos and continues to use Lao territory to supply personnel and supplies to the Vietcong.

The response of the Laotian Government has been to build up its own forces to a level sufficient to hold the Pathet Lao in check, to undertake limited clearing operations, and to commence a rural reconstruction and village cluster program designed to improve security in the countryside and maintain the loyalty of the rural population.

Over the past year, Prime Minister Souvanna Phouma has consolidated his position [security deletion] he retains the legal framework of a tripartite government, a position which helps insure international and domestic support for the Government of Laos. 

On the military side, fighting between Royal Laotian Government military forces and Pathet Lao-North Vietnamese units has been fairly continuous for many months in north, central and south Laos. Communist armed forces remain in control of the strategic Plaine des Jarres in the north and of the eastern part of the Lao panhandle. To date, the Lao armed forces have captured 32 North

Vietnamese who were soldiers in the regular North Vietnamese Army. Their presence in Laos in violation of the 1962 Geneva agreements is under investigation by the ICC.

The economic burden caused by heavy defense and security expenses confronts the country with budgetary and balance-of-payments deficits, which threaten the financial stability of the country. Free world support continues to be essential to the Government's capacity to maintain itself against Communist military pressure.

The main elements of the U.S. assistance program are (1) financial support, together with that of other free world countries, for the importation of essential commodities to increase the supply of goods and offset inflationary pressures; (2) technical assistance designed to improve agricultural productivity and develop the educational system; (3) rehabilitation and settlement of hill tribes and refugees, the victims of Pathet Lao aggression [security deletion]. Thailand

The During the past year, Thailand continued its impressive rate of progress. 

Government, headed by Prime Minister Thanom Kittikachorn, is articulately anti-Communist. 

Politically aware Thai recognize the need for a strong, purposeful, effective administration. While based on the armed forces, the Thai Government includes strong civilian personalities who influence general policy as well as running their own ministries-such as Foreign Minister Thanat Khoman and Minister of Na tional Development Pote Sarasin. 

Thailand has, for a developing country, an exceptionally strong economy. It has good prospects of doubling its GNP within 10 to 15 years. Strong prices for rice, advances in agricultural productivity, and diversification of crops have steadily improved its export position. Thailand is willng and able to play a major role in the future development of the southeast Asian region. 

U.S. assistance has played an important part in these developments, and in giving Thailand the confidence to play a vital role of supporting and assisting free world effort to thwart aggression in southeast Asia. [Security deletion.] 

The favorable prospects for Thai economic development and political stability are endangered by the growing threat of Communist subversion and insurgency, aimed at overthrowing the Thai Government and sponsored by Communist China. This threat creates problems and uncertainties which will require a substantial investment of free world assistance in the years ahead. This investment is essential to meeting the Communist challenge.

The provinces of the northeast, where the subversive effort has centered, are remote from the fertile soils of Thailand's central valley but contain one-third of the population. This area has not shared fully in the economic growth of the country. Widespread poverty, the presence of sizable minority groups, the remoteness from Thailand's centralized administrative structure, combine to create a potential for insurgency. These problems have been intensified by the stepped-up Communist activity, including an increasing number of acts of terrorism, organization of subversive and anti-Government groups, and training of cadres for guerrilla bands.

The Government of Thailand has taken steps to deal with the situation. Our economic assistance programs are being redirected to helping the Government succeed in this area by assisting efforts to bring effective administration to all levels of the government, improve the security and police system to protect villagers, and launch new efforts in rural education, health, and infrastructure projects. Our military assistance program helps train and equip the armed forces to cope with the threat of incipient insurgency [security deletion] and to play a role under various SEATO contingency plans. 

NEW CHALLENGES AND OPPORTUNITIES 

These are the main elements of the assistance program proposed for the Far East. These programs are tight and austere; they meet the needs as they exist today-no more.

Over the coming year the developing situation in several countries may present new challenges and opportunities for U.S. policy which we must be prepared to seize. I should like here to mention a few:

The Philippines [Security deletion.] 

The new President has shown a heartening determination to attack major internal Philippine problems. He has ordered establishment of a special branch of the coast guard to deal with the national problem of smuggling through which the Government each year loses substantial revenues, appointed a new customs commissioner; and made sweeping transfers of officials out of traditional smuggling areas. The President's order of priorities is internal and

external security, law and order, more administrative efficiency, economic progress, and social change. [Security deletion.] 

New vigor is emanating from the Philippine "White House," in which Filipinos have found new hope for progress. 

[Security deletion] the [security deletion] performance of the Philippines' 

economy. [Security deletion] has been characterized by alternate bursts of growth and stagnation. While the Philippines are well endowed with resources and the potential for growth is considerable, at present the economy's growth stays barely ahead of one of the highest rates of population increase in the world. The state of the Government's finances is serious, leaving urgent development projects without access to public funds. 

Our economic aid, programed at $3.5 million in fiscal year 1967, will continue to stress those activities most conducive to urgently needed reforms in such areas as tax collection and land distribution. At the same time we will remain receptive to proposals for additional development assistance-provided the Government will demonstrate to us its resolution and capacity to marshal its own resources. We are also now negotiating with the Philippines on the uses of the $28 million special fund for education (arising out of war damage claims. The purpose of this fund is to support educational programs promoting the general development of the Philippines, these programs to be jointly determined between the two Presidents.

President Marcos has started to reorganize and modernize the Philippine armed forces and has placed before us several requests for military assistance. He has made clear his determination to engage the Philippines, side by side with Vietnam and the United States, in the Vietnamese war effort the success of which he regards as vital to Philippine security. 

[Security deletion.] 

Other countries 

[Security deletion.] One other country on our minds is Malaysia. [Security deletion.]

Malaysia has just completed a new 5-year development plan (1966-70) whose objectives and major assumptions have been endorsed by the World Bank 

We are convinced that achievement of this plan is essential if Malaysia is to maintain its momentum of economic growth and social progress. 

[Securtiy deletion.] CONCLUSION 

In sum, Mr. Chairman, we believe that our proposed assistance programs are essential for the achievement of our policy objectives in the Far East. We request that the committee approve these programs.

As regards the economic programs, Vietnam, of course, is overwhelmingly the largest-the military program is now being covered in the defense budget-followed by Korea, Laos third, and Thailand fourth. All the rest add up to not more than 3 percent. [Security deletion.]

My statement, sir, summarizes the situation in each of these countries. That is a very fundamental change, I am sure you will recognize, from the type of situation that this committee confronted 5 years ago, let us say, or even 3 or 2 years ago. It is evidence of the kind of progress that is possible once a government takes hold of its situation, institutes the necessary self-help measures, and so on. 

I would say the program we have in Korea is an outstanding example of the principle enunciated by the President of rewarding self-help.

In Laos the situation is principally one of sustaining that Government in its effort to contain the North Vietnamese military presence which is related to the continuing infiltration of men and supplies through Laotian territory into South Vietnam. 

Thailand, which I also visited on my recent trip, is basically in good shape economically, and is now relying for its development financing needs principally on the World Bank and commercial credits. However, there is a grave and continuing need for the type of assistance program that falls under the heading of a counterinsurgency effort. 

Accelerated rural development, the mobile development units and the civil police improvement program, and all that goes with it are funded under the economic assistance program and there is a continuing need in that area.

Mr. Chairman, these are the four major economic assistance programs in the Far East. The fifth major program is on the military side and concerns the Republic of China, which must maintain major military forces. [Security deletion.]

STATEMENT OF HON. RUTHERFORD M. POATS, ASSISTANT ADMIN- ISTRATOR, FAR EAST, AGENCY FOR INTERNATIONAL DEVELOP- MENT 

Mr. POATS. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. I also propose to submit my statement for the record and summarize it as briefly as I can. 

Chairman MORGAN. Without objection, it is so ordered. 

(The statement of Mr. Poats is as follows:) STATEMENT OF HON. RUTHERFORD M. POATS, ASSISTANT ADMINISTRATOR, FAR EAST, AGENCY FOR INTERNATIONAL DEVELOPMENT 

Mr. Chairman, I appreciate the opportunity to report to the Foreign Affairs

Committee on our current economic aid activities in the Far East and to present our proposed programs for fiscal year 1967. 

Members of this committee are familiar with the historical bases of the U.S. present role in the Far East. You have repeatedly recognized that properly managed economic assistance programs are essential to our interests in that region-to help poor and vulnerable peoples maintain their independence, resist Communist attack and subversion, and build progressive, free societies. 

The economic aid programs designed to meet these objectives in the Far East can be divided into four major categories: (1) Large-scale counterinsurgency efforts, as in Vietnam and Laos; (2) Preventive efforts, as in northeast Thailand and Laos, to reduce the vulnerability of depressed or alienated rural areas to future Communist subversion or insurgency; (3) long-range economic and social development, coupled with security and stabilization measures, as in Korea today and in Taiwan in the past; and

(4) Programs and projects designed to promote regional integration and multilateral support of economic and social development in southeast Asia.

These broadly descriptive categories are not mutually exclusive. Elements of several of these categories may be found in a single country program. 

The common theme of emphasis in all our country programs is rural development-improving the "break" of the rural people, inducing governments to shift more of their investment and welfare spending to the rural areas, directing more AID and food-for-work resources into agricultural production, favoring rural education, health, and security needs over city needs in AID advice and assistance. We believe this makes good sense in both developmental and political terms. (At the same time, we must recognize that economic stabilization or military budget support necessitates putting nonproject assistance through the commercial channels of assisted countries, and these channels begin with the private importer in the metropolis.)

The President's message and Mr. Bell's testimony have announced increased attention to agriculture, education, and health. These three program areas have been addressed by expert teams visiting Vietnam in the past 6 weeks-an agricultural mission headed by Secretary Freeman and a health and education team headed by Secretary Gardner. Their recommendations will lead to initiation or intensification of AID projects in these fields. Our planned programs for fiscal year 1967, reflected in the presentation books before you, show an increase of some $15 million in country programs and about $10 million in regional programs in these three fields over the fiscal year 1965 levels. 

KOREA 

Other cardinal principles of our economic assistance-insistence upon specific and measurable self-help actions in all developmental programs and promotion of private enterprise-will be seen in the country program details which are described in the presentation books. I should like now to review the highlights of our major Far East programs and plans for fiscal year 1967. 

The increasing willingness of the Korean Government to make and carry out important self-help decisions has changed Korea from a country needing longterm external support for survival to a bright developmental prospect. During the last year the Korean Government not only settled its differences with Japan and sent substantial forces to fight in Vietnam, but in response to U.S. recommendations it also strengthened its fiscal and monetary stabilization programs, adopted indirect monetary controls, reformed its interest rate structure to encourage domestic savings, liberalized its system of allocating foreign exchange to permit the play of market forces, and floated its exchange rate.

The results of these actions and those of prior years are clear. In 1965, Korean exports rose over 50 percent to $180 million. For the third consecutive year, GNP increased about 8 percent, and both gross investment (up 17 percent) and domestic revenues (up 35 percent) rose markedly in real terms. The stabilization program was effective in 1965; wholesale price increases, a high 27 percent in 1964, were held to 6½ percent in 1965. 

With these achievements, Koreans are now manifesting a new confidence in their own future. They are setting ambitious goals for themselves. If these trends continue, South Korea can demonstrate that hard and intelligent work can over time bring a country with apparently dismal prospects to self-support. ****The Korean prospects are, however, mainly still prospects. It still has a large balance-of-payments deficit, a heavy defense burden, and low per capita incomes (estimated at $120). But the direction and pace of movement are excellent. 

The AID fiscal year 1967 request includes [security deletion] $7.6 million for technical cooperation. 

Reflecting Korea's progress, this program continues the increased emphasis on loans, while decreasing grants. The high lending level results from our confidence in Korea's growing ability to make productive use of development aid and an increasing capacity to service AID loans. Almost all of the loan funds will be used for specific projects, in such fields as power, industry, and water. 

The World Bank has agreed to head the formation of a multilateral consultative group on Korea, with the first meeting to be held in London in May. We are

hopeful that the group will help make effective the efforts of the United States, Japan, Germany, the World Bank, and others interested in Korea's development. 

Supporting assistance will continue its steady decline in fiscal year 1967. This decline has successfully stimulated increased export earnings. We also condition supporting assistance releases on Korean agreement to and compliance with essential terms of annual economic stabilization programs. Program loans also are being tied to achievement of specific, quarterly stabilization goals. [Security deletion.] 

Continued technical advice in Korea is essential to maintain the development momentum. U.S. advisers are important to Korea's planning process, its export and revenue performance, and to the development of its economic policies. U.S. technicians are also involved in Korean efforts to increase agricultural production to achieve basic self-sufficiency in grains by 1971. A substantial portion of the fiscal year 1967 funds is for a new Institute of Industrial Technology & Applied Science, to provide technical knowledge which will ultimately accelerate Korean industry's output of marketable products and rationalize business investment decisions. [Security delection.] VIETNAM 

AID is engaged in Vietnam in two crucial aspects of the war-the fight against Communist subversion and insurgency, primarily in the rural areas; and the fight against inflation and critical commodity shortages in the marketplace, primarily in the cities and in isolated towns. 

Described in another way, the program may be divided into six parts: (1) "rural construction" or village political and social development; (2) refugee relief; (3) police training, equipment, and advisory assistance; (4) longer term development; (5) transport and logistics; and (6) basic economic support through grant financing of essential imports sold on the market. 

A third way of thinking of the program is to break it into two parts: project and direct support operations (the first five items in the list above), and the commercial import program.

In fiscal year 1966 the program, including the supplemental appropriations, totals $540 million in AID funds and about $100 million in Public Law 480 surplus commodities.

For fiscal year 1967 we estimate a requirement for $550 million or 72 percent of the total supporting assistance request in the President's budget. This is twice the size of the program in fiscal year 1965. In addition a Public Law 480 program of approximately the same magnitude as that of fiscal year 1966 is planned. Attachments A, B, C, and D show a breakdown of the cost components. 

Although activities previously funded with "Technical cooperation" appropriations are continuing, no "Technical cooperation" funds are proposed for fiscal year 1967 since these activities can only arbitrarily be distinguished from other aspects of the AID project program. 

The mission staff by the end of the current fiscal year will exceed 800 direct hire AID employees (plus perhaps 100 in training), about 400 contract or participating agency U.S. employees, and about 200 nationals of other Asian nations. In both funds and staff, the Vietnam operation is larger than all other 

Far East AID programs combined.

This is, obviously, not a normal AID program. It is a key element in an unprecedented war effort. Vietnam is torn by a new form of aggression, the 

Communists' "natural war of liberation," which combines covert invasion and local insurrection, using terror, propaganda, guerrilla raids, economic warfare, and conventional military action. At root, the war is a struggle for the support of the peasant villagers, who make up about 80 percent of the population, and of key opinion-makers in the towns and cities. The winner must convince the people that his system of government and social organization offers something worth fighting for.

It is, therefore, a political struggle waged with words, constructive and destructive deeds at the local level and small scale armed action, now overlaid by intensifying major military combat. It is being fought in a situation of the 

Communists' choosing, a country which had never had time to achieve full national unity, a country of sharp religious and regional jealousies, a country whose leadership elite had been weakened by years of Communist assassination and intimidation and political disaffection. The Communists have been able to

ม exploit the historical absence of effective and responsive local government and the peasants' and ethnic minority groups' strong distrust of Saigon, and of all governmental authority.

The U.S. Government's civil program in Vietnam must enable the Vietnamese 

Government at all levels to create, in the face of these handicaps and armed attack, a common bond with the people which is strong enough to resist the Vietcong demands for cooperation or support. This effort is not merely a negative resistance to communism, certainly not a defense of the status quo. It is a positive and constructive effort to build a nation from the villages up, with a political and economic-social system which actually produces a better life for the common man.

Obviously, this revolutionary change must be carried out by the Vietnamese, in their own way. In most fields of AID work which touch on this essentially political reform, Americans can assist, support and discreetly advise, but if they take over and do the job there will be no change in the peasant's doubts about the Vietnamese Government or his future under it. 

We can, within this limitation, conduct some essentially American operations, such as providing direct medical care, providing transportation, building major facilities, providing emergency relief. We can train and advise technical and professional people, such as teachers, health workers, doctors, police, and to a limited degree administrators and rural cadremen. We can lend a hand in the provincial program of "rural construction," the recently refined and improved form of village-level restoration of government authority, security, and services. 

We can provide the budget support and basic commodities such as roofing, cement, school equipment, medicines, fertilizer, pesticides, etc., which local government needs to respond to the peasant's pressing needs. 

Ultimately, however, our success will be no better than the Vietnamese administration, the personal dedication of the Vietnamese engaged in the effort, and the security provided by armed forces.

Guerrilla war imposes demands on the defender many times greater than the guerrilla's effort. Military, paramilitary, and police forces of the Vietnamese Government, although increased to a total of more than 600,000, still are not sufficient to cope with perhaps 200,000 Vietcong and North Vietnamese regulars. A few man-hours of sabotage or a few dollars worth of explosives can destroy thousands of dollars worth of railway or powerlines, and when repeated around the country can cause millions of dollars worth of repair or alternative system cost. The South Vietnamese economy and population cannot support this effort alone. Shortages and budget deficits produce inflationary threats which can undermine the whole effort. Our economic aid, both direct and through the expanded import-financing program, like our introduction of more operating manpower, helps close these dangerous gaps. 

The introduction of U.S. military forces has prevented military defeat, but it also has added economic problems. Troop spending and an enormous U.S. military construction program are pumping inflationary piasters into an economy already ripe for serious inflation. To avoid disastrous economic dislocations, we and the Vietnamese Government must increase imports, limit piaster spending wherever possible, increase Vietnamese tax revenues and other withdrawals of money supply. To deal with local shortages caused by the wartime overloading of normal transportation, Communist interdiction of transportation or refugee flight to the towns, we must provide additional coastal vessels, rapidly improve ports, provide airlift, build up security stocks, build warehouses, and provide trucks wherever they can be operated. 

This outlies has, I hope, suggested some of the dimensions and elements of the AID program in Vietnam. I will be glad to go into it in greater detail if the committee wishes.

LAOS 

In Laos continued U.S. assistance is required to prevent a Communist takeover and to assure [security deletion] the strengthening of Laos' still frail nationhood. 

Torn by continued insecurity and far behind its neighbors in education, agriculture, and industry, Laos nevertheless has, with our aid, made a degree of economic and social progress, particularly in primary education and teacher training, roadbuilding, and rural development. The United States is assisting the

Lao Government to create more effective and responsive local government in those areas under Government control, to improve rural agricultural production, health and education standards, and to build the foundations for future economic and social development. We are also helping the Lao to enlist financial and moral support from free world nations in the attempt to maintain the country's independence.

Most of the total proposed program [security deletion] in fiscal year 1967 [security deletion] is for direct security operations [security deletion] for activities promoting economic stability in the face of large deficits required to support the security forces. The security activities include [security deletion] the provision of rice, cloth, and handtools to [security deletion] hill tribesmen and their families. Now that political conditions permit, we are attempting to reestablish, equip, and train an effective Lao civilian police in urban areas. 

Most of the economic project activity is centered in strategic rural areas, specifically in 11 village clusters where AID advisers and young men and women of the International Voluntary Services work with local leaders and the people to help them improve health, sanitation, farming methods, education, transportation, and local government. We also are conducting substantial education, health, agriculture, and transportation projects on a national basis. 

THAILAND 

U.S. assistance to Thailand increasingly has been focused on the problems of 

Communist subversion and insurgency and concentrated in the threatened areas of the northeast and north. AID's program in Thailand for fiscal year 1967 is designed to help strengthen and extend the capacities of local government to provide security, greater economic opportunity, and health and education services. 

The programs are also designed to help develop a sense of participation in the processes of government on the part of the Thai villager. 

The Thai Government has recognized the nature and urgency of the Communist threat. It is making a determined effort to meet that threat with a combination of economic, political, and security programs, and has welcomed the U.S. advice and participation in this undertaking. 

The United States and Thai Governments are working jointly to improve the capability of the Thai civil police to provide security to the rural population and root out the subversive bands before they can establish themselves firmly. 

Under the broader umbrella of the accelerated rural development program, the United States is helping the northeast and northern Provincial governments-with National Government support-expand services to the villagers by building and improving roads and small irrigation works, developing new domestic water resources, and intensifying agricultural, health, and educational programs. We will help the Thai establish an integrated network of health centers, midwifery centers, public health laboratories, and regional tuberculosis control centers in the sensitive areas. Education programs will be directed toward meeting needs of the rural northeast by providing village schools with basic textbooks and teaching aids, upgrading village teachers' capabilities, and assisting Thai education officials to improve the administration of rural education.

For fiscal year 1967, $13.5 million in technical cooperations funds are being requested for Thailand for further development of human resources as outlined above. In addition [security deletion] in supporting assistance funds are being sought for urgent capital development in the rural areas and improvement of the nation's civil security forces.

BURMA 

Prevailing conditions in Burma do not warrant substantial new economic aid commitments. It is, however, necessary to continue technical supervision of 

AID projects which were initiated in the past if we are to insure their completion. 

Consequently, technical cooperation funds of $200,000 are proposed to be used solely for limited technical advice to assure adequate monitoring of prior-year projects.

SOUTHEAST ASIA REGIONAL COOPERATION 

President Johnson last April called upon Asian leaders and the United

Nations Secretary General to take initiatives toward regional cooperation which might offer peaceful alternatives to war in southeast Asia. We are encouraging now the development of a regional framework for Asian nations to work together in achieving economic and social progress and long-range political stability. The 

United States supported the formation of the Asian Development Bank whose charter was signed in December 1965. The Bank, the result of a major Asian initiative under the auspices of the U.N.'s ECAFE, is receiving the full backing not only of most Asian nations but of a number of nonregional industrialized nations as well. With its $1 billion authorized capital now subscribed by the participating nations, the Bank is expected to commence operations toward the end of this calendar year.

The United States is also encouraging the work of the Mekong Development Committee, consisting of the four countries along the banks of the Mekong, in formulating plans to accelerate development of this great and relatively unexploited river basin.

Eight countries last week formally pledged $24 million to construct the Committee's highest priority project--the Nam Ngum Dam in Laos. This project will link northeast Thailand to a source of relatively cheap power in Laos, and will provide valuable experience in practical measures of cooperation, a prerequisite for extensive development of this great natural resource. Other countries-Japan, the Netherlands, Canada, Thailand, Denmark, Australia, and New Zealand-contributed half the cost on the same basis as the United States, an untied grant to be administered by the World Bank. This action encourages hope that these and other countries will expand their economic assistance in southeast Asia. It certainly justifies the confidence of the Congress in providing funds last year which served as an inducement to secure matching contribution. We are now studying several additional projects from a number of proposals submitted by the Mekong Committee. We are continuing to assist with three basic planning requirements for Mekong development: water systems analysis of the entire lower basin being performed by the U.S. Corps of Engineers; a cartographic inventory of the natural and human resources of the basin by the TVA and the Army Map Service, and a comprehensive feasibility survey of a multipurpose mainstream dam.

Unfortunately, the countries of southeast Asia have virtually no tradition in political and economic cooperation. It is encouraging, however, that in the months following the President's Baltimore speech several meetings have been called by Asian leaders to explore prospects for greater regional cooperation. 

Japan is hosting a meeting of southeast Asian ministers in April to discuss economic cooperation, and there have been separate meetings of southeast Asian ministers of education and central bankers. [Security deletion.] It is our hope that the Asian Development Bank and other regional institutions such as the Mekong Committee will gradually foster a habit of regional cooperation which will eventually extend into the political field. 

Twenty million dollars is proposed for the regional program for fiscal year 1967, half of which is to go to multilateral programs of development in the lower Mekong River Basin. Much of the rest is earmarked to expand and transform the existing regional graduate school of engineering in Bangkok into an internationally administered graduate school to provide the engineering manpower for the Mekong and other regional development programs in southeast Asia. 

PHILIPPINES 

In the Philippines the new administration of President Marcos may enable us to broaden our rather limited program in that country, although the economic policies and the development programs of the new administration have not yet been made clear, nor the degree of self-help measures to be undertaken. We hope the new administration there may be better able than its predecessors to capitalize on abundant natural resources and a number of other positive elements already existing in Philippine society. [Security deletion.]

In the meantime, a program of $3.4 million in 1967 is proposed for technical assistance. Our aid is in these main areas. In rural development we will continue advising the land reform program and supporting pilot development projects in selected rural areas. AID will complete existing mineral, forest, and energy surveys and will undertake additional resource surveys in other sectors. In the area of internal security we will give advice and technical assistance designed to strengthen and orient law enforcement agencies to deal more effectively with the growing threats of lawlessness and crime in the Philippines. We are taking advantage of the similarity of our government structures and the desire of President Marcos for such help, to make available technical aid in public administration, particularly in tax administration and customs. INDONESIA

Lastly, I should take some note of events in Indonesia. The only U.S. assistance currently programed for Indonesia is the donation by private charitable organizations of U.S. surplus commodities for distribution to needy people. This humanitarian assistance, valued at about $2 million annually, is made possible under title III of Public Law 480, and the food is donated directly to, and distributed by three American voluntary agencies operating in Indonesia. No new program of economic or developmental assistance is included in this presentation.
