NOTE.--Additional statistical information was submitted but has been deleted for security reasons.

Mr. POATS. I would like to invite the committee's attention to certain passages in the statement.

Mr. Chairman, the various programs of economic aid in the Far 

East respond to a variety of situations of considerable interest to the United States. These may be considered for handy reference, in four categories: Large scale counterinsurgency efforts in Vietnam and

Laos; preventive measures against insurgency, as in northeast Thailand-programs designed to reduce the vulnerability of depressed or alienated rural areas to Communist subversion or insurgency; longterm economic and social development programs, as in Korea and in the past in Taiwan; and programs and projects designed to promote regional integration and multilateral support of economic and social development in southeast Asia.

The common theme of all of these programs is rural development: 

Improving the lot of the rural people; inducing governments to shift more of their investment and welfare spending to the rural areas;

= 2 rea directing more aid and food into agricultural production; and favoring rural education and health over expenditures in the cities. 

The President's message and previous testimony have emphasized the theme of increased attention to agriculture, health, and education. 

You will find these themes reflected in an expansion of our programs in these areas. Approximately and this has to be quite rough at this stage approximately $15 million of fiscal year-fiscal year 1967 country programs' bilateral assistance represents an expansion over previous efforts in these three fields.

In addition, the regional program contains about $10 million, out of the total of $20 million proposed, which can be attributed to these three fields of emphasis. We are continuing, of course, the cardinal principles of self-help and promotion of private enterprise. As Mr. 

Bundy has mentioned, both of these principles are very well reflected in the successful program in Taiwan and in the encouraging progress in Korea.

The Korean Government has in the past 2 years made and carried out very important self-help decisions which have changed Korea from a country which seemed to need support indefinitely for survival to a promising development situation.

During the past year, for example, the Korean Government has settled its differences with Japan; sent substantial forces to fight in Vietnam; and, in response to our recommendations, has strengthened its fiscal and monetary stabilization programs, adopted indirect monetary controls, reformed its savings rate structure, and has floated its exchange rate. 4 The results of these measures are summarized very briefly on page of my statement. We are proposing therefore to increase our developmental lending in Korea and to continue the reduction of supporting assistance grants.

We believe that there is opportunity here to speed the process of achieving self-support through an intensive development effort just as was the case in Taiwan. The program will increasingly use program loans which are tied to specific commitments of self-help such as increasing domestic savings, increasing tax revenues, balancing the Government budget, maintaining adequate foreign exchange reserves, and so on. These are quantified and specified over periods of time and we release funds against performance as measured by these targets. 

The committee has reviewed the Vietnam program extensively only recently in connection with the supplemental appropriation request. 

I will not therefore take your time in going over the material in my statement on this except to point to the tables, attachments A through D, which summarize the amounts involved in this program. 

It is, as the committee knows, not a normal AID program. It is an element in an unprecedented war effort. The winner in this effort, of course, must demonstrate that his method of social organization offers something worth fighting for. Our programs in Vietnam must enable the Vietnamese Government at all levels to create, in the face of great handicaps and armed attack, a common bond with the people which is strong enough to resist the Vietcong demands on the peasantry for cooperation and support. This is not merely resistance to communism-it is a positive program of change from the village

up a change that will create political and economic and social systems which actually produce a better life for the common man. 

I have summarized in this statement the components of the Vietnam program and its main themes. Obviously the South Vietnamese economy cannot support this effort alone. Shortages of resources and budget deficits produce inflationary threats which can undermine the whole effort. Our aid helps close these gaps. 

In Laos we have a program which is growing to meet growing problems. We have expanded substantially our operations. [Security deletion.] This has added to the program. 

You will note, however, that the nonproject support of the economy remains at [security deletion] whereas [security deletion] operations have risen [security deletion] in the proposed program. In addition, we have expanded our developmental activities in health, education, and agriculture.

We are attempting to expand the opportunities for secondary school education in Laos because one of the great shortages-the great impediments to any achievement of self-support-is the level of qualified manpower. The Lao are receiving help from other advanced countries: the British, French, Japanese, Australians, New Zealanders, and Germans. Their total assistance is in the vicinity of $10 million annually.

Most of our project activity is centered in strategic rural areas, particularly in 11 village clusters and in some forward areas extending out from these clusters.

Young men and women of the International Voluntary Service and AID employees work directly with local leaders and people to improve health, sanitation, farming methods, transportation, local government.

In Thailand we have, as I have said, been engaged for the past several years in a preventive effort to prevent the development there of the sort of Communist insurgency, now openly threatened by the Chinese Communist leaders, which we are experiencing in Vietnam. 

Our program in 1967 will be designed to help strengthen and extend the capacities of local governments to provide security, greater economic opportunity, health, and education services. 

The programs are also intended-and the Thai Government is making considerable progress in this respect-to increase the sense of participation in the process of government by the Thai villager. We are engaged there essentially in two areas of activity: to improve the capacity of the civil police of Thailand to provide local security in the threatened areas primarily in the north and northeast; and, second, to carry out under the term "Accelerated Rural Development,' a series of operations to establish greater local autonomy, greater capacity in local government to respond to local needs, a greater devotion of national government effort to the problems of the villagerswho, as you know, are a mixture of Thai, Lao, and Vietnamese ethnic origins. 

In 1967 we propose a program of $1212 million in technical assistance and [security deletion] in supporting assistance for this program.

In Burma we are merely completing projects funded in previous years, with a small staff to monitor the completion of these projects.

1 be htt деп ex ter exte men sens er Tore ur st; pm gre se et for pre pro These include a teak mill, the completion of the Rangoon University facilities, and a water project in Rangoon. 

The bilateral programs meet a substantial part of the problem we face in Asia. But the President last April recognized the need for a more vigorous effort to promote regional cooperation in southeast Asia, to find alternatives to the hostility which has torn that area ever since these countries achieved independence. 

We have encouraged the formation of regional institutions which would promote this cooperation, this working together. The principal one of these is the Asian Development Bank which, of course, has a broader base than merely southeast Asia. This was truly an 

Asian initiative to which we responded. That bank is now virtually fully capitalized and will come into existence the latter part of this year.

We have also encouraged the work of the United Nations Mekong 

Development Committee. Eight countries last week pledged $24 million to construct the committee's priority project, the Nam Ngum Dam in Laos. That project fits the specifications of an appropriate regional project and it will provide power to Thailand as well as Laos. It will involve loan contributions from Thailand as well as from the advanced countries. The other countries that are assisting contributed their funds on a united grant basis to be administered by the World Bank. We are contributing half the cost and seven other countries are contributing the other half. We are supporting other regional developments and I can go into detail if the committee wishes.

With respect to the Philippines, I can offer at this moment only the hope that the new administration will undertake the monetary, the fiscal and economic policy measures which are necessary to enable it to arrest the serious deterioration of economic life [security deletion].

We are proposing at this time to continue [security deletion] program of technical assistance, concentrating in rural development, surveys of raw material resources, internal security assistance, and programs of technical aid in public administration, particularly in tax administration and customs. 

[Security deletion.] 

Mr. POATS. Mr. Chairman, that concludes the brief summar of my statement. I will be prepared to answer any questions. 

Chairman MORGAN. Thank you, Mr. Poats. 

Mr. Hays has another engagement and has to leave. I am going to yield my time to him so that he can ask his questions and I will take his turn to ask my questions. 

Mr. HAYS. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. I have only one question, gentlemen. Can you tell the committee anything about what has been done or is being done to stop this black market out in Vietnam and get some of these people home who are doing it, or at least get them out of the country?

Mr. POATS. Yes; black marketing, of course, occurs in situations when there is a large disparity in the official and the free market value of the currency. We have enforced and are continuing to tighten-

Mr. HAYS. If you will let me interrupt you. I am speaking about the people who are sending large amounts of currency back home. 

I am aware of what causes the black market and it exists. After 

I mentioned this to Secretary Rusk, I understood that the Inspector General and others were sent out there. I have heard rumors that they have uncovered quite a sizable number of people, and have revoked the passports of some and sent them out of the country. Do you know anything about that?

Mr. POATS. That is true. One case in front of me is that of a person working for an engineering firm employed by the Defense Department who was found to have sent excessive amounts of money home. He has been removed from the country and his work there terminated. 

Mr. HAYS. Is that the only case you know about?

Mr. POATS. That is the only case about which I have a detailed record. I know there have been a number of cases investigated of apparent use of the mails to violate the currency regulations. I am not aware of the names or the disposition.

Mr. HAYS. I don't want the names nor do I want them in the record. 

All I would like is an indication, I would hope this investigation is going on, that something will continue to be done about it. 

Mr. POATS. All the operating agencies in Vietnam have expanded their staffs for this work and are conducting very intensive investigations of every piece of information brought to their attention. 

Mr. HAYS. That is very heartening because you know when I first made this more or less off-the-cuff statement, and it got in the press, the Embassy denied that anybody had ever told Mr. Crawford and me this. We knew they had. I am very glad that something is being done about it. 

I yield my time. 

Mr. MONAGAN. Was this an American firm?

Mr. POATS. The case I refer to is an American engineering firm employed on contract doing Defense Department work in Vietnam. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Can Mr. Poats advise the committee to what extent they have been discovering the diversion of commodities brought in under the commodity import program? I understand that silver nitte is being diverted for uses other than intended. Apparently silve nitrate can be used by the Vietcong for military purposes. It is also possible and apparently economically feasible to reduce it to its silver content and this, too, is being done. 

Mr. POATS. We have been investigating this question of the increased imports of silver nitrate, including a chemical analysis of the silver nitrate. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. You are aware of these possible diversions, then?

Mr. POATS. Yes, indeed. We have had chemical analyses conducted, and we have been examining the monetary advantage of converting the product to different forms. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Is the diverting being done by Americans or Vietnamese?

Mr. POATS. We are not yet sure there is any diversion. We are tracing down the actual use of the silver nitrate in the economy to see if it is being converted to silver for hoarding.

Mr. HAYS. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. I appreciate it.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Frelinghuysen. 

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. I would like to begin, Mr. Bundy, by asking you about the proposed new title VIII in the bill. First of all, in order to understand why this title should be necessary at all, we presently have provisions in the law which enable us to contribute funds to regional and multilateral programs, do we not? Section 201 (b) is the language that comes to my mind. 

Mr. BUNDY. I suppose, Congressman, that provision might provide a legal basis for relieving this. The major purpose of the new title is to give emphasis to this program and provide more comprehensive authority. In effect, it provides a statement of policy in this area, serves as a basis for whatever might become necessary in the future. 

I concede you could find other legal ways of doing it. However, this provision would be the way that would most dramatically indicate the support of the Congress for the principles that the President stated on April 7, and that is its main purpose.

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. Your dramatic support for the President's policy looks to me suspiciously like a very blank check. I am not sure why we should go this far. As an example, it is open ended. 

You are not even expecting-you are not requesting any funds this year under this title. I don't particularly like a grandstanding approach to these problems unless there is some necessity for it. 

We have had discussions about the value and meaning of certain resolutions that Congress passed with respect to southeast Asia, because they have been used as a basis for action taken later which some feel is not wise. I would think that Congress might well be more cautious than this title would suggest in establishing a willingness to go along with proposals to expand the regional approach. 

Mr. POATS. May I say in comment on that question, Mr. Frelinghuysen, that our principal purpose here is to establish an authorization base for requesting appropriations to the special funds of the Asian 

Development Bank. The President authorized Eugene Black to state during the organizing meetings of the Bank that we share the organizers opinions that the Bank would need and could well administer special funds contributed by the advanced countries for major projects, particularly in those areas in which softer terms than the conventional terms planned by the Bank would be needed. 

Mr. Black stated we would provide up to $100 million over a period of years, for that purpose, if other countries came forward and made contributions to the special funds. We considered for a time the proposal of a specific amount for the first year of such contributions. We also considered the possibility of requesting a $100 million authorization for this purpose.

But we were not at all sure at the time this presentation, this legislation, was prepared whether the Bank would be prepared to handle these funds in fiscal year 1967 or 1968, or whether other countries would come forward. We did want, however, to have a record of congressional endorsement of the concept so we could begin seeking contributions from other countries to such special funds. This is the purpose of the authorization request.

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. I should like to clarify in my own mind just what authority the administration has and how it has been exercised.

You say the administration-Mr. Black has already pledged $100 million from the United States for special funds for the Asian Development Bank?

Mr. POATS. Mr. Black stated that the President would request of 

Congress up to $100 million conditioned on the extent to which other advanced countries came forward with contributions. We would expect that the pay-in of that $100 million or less would be over a period of several years. 

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. Would you consider this a pledge?

Mr. POATS. It was not a pledge. Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. It is certainly a model-if a leading spokesman for our Government makes a statement like that, Congress would be repudiating its position if we didn't support it. 

Mr. POATS. It was carefully couched in terms of the President's willingness to propose such a contribution to the Congress. are

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. Maybe we should take a look at what we getting into. I am not saying that we shouldn't be moving in this direction, but we need to know the reasons for seeking congressional support. We need to know whether it is needed or not, before we simply endorse a proposal as broad as this. 

What specifically in terms of money do we anticipate this whole Mekong River development program is going to cost both us and others who participate? Have we any indication at all of the nature of- Mr. POATS. There are small projects and large ones. The smaller projects, such as the Nam Ngum Dam, might be financed on a 50-50 sharing basis which we consider quite an accomplishment. Our contributions to special funds of the Asian Development Bank would be roughly on a matching basis with other advanced countries. [Security deletion.]

These are things which we need to encourage. As to the amounts of money that may be involved in the Mekong, there could be enormous projects coming along in the next decade, but not likely in the 1960's. We are conducting a study, which is a 7or 8-year job in itself, of the feasibility of a major main stream dam at Pa Mong on the border of Thailand and Laos. That project might cost in the order of a half billion dollars.

We are not at all sure if it is feasible; we are conducting studies to determine that. It would have to be financed by major contributions from many different countries over a number of years. We do not see projects of that scale coming forward in the remainder of this decade.

For the remainder of this decade, for the period covered by this authorization, we would expect programs somewhat larger than [security deletion] that we are estimating for fiscal year 1967 but not in very large amounts.

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. It isn't reassuring simply to know that other countries are willing or being encouraged to participate. What I am concerned about is the extent of the commitment on the part of the United States and the degree to which Congress should hand over to the executive branch the responsibility for making the determination as to whether we should participate.

Where did the money that the United States has pledged for this 

Nam Ngum Dam come from?

Mr. POATS. It was in the supplemental request we made to the 

Congress last June; it was specifically proposed in the amount of $13,500,000.

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. You would anticipate before any further distribution is made that there would be a specific request? I will answer my own question. This is already pledged, in effect, this $100 million for the special funds of the Asian Development Bank, without any authority and any decision on your part to request authority specifically for these purposes?

Mr. POATS. Mr. Black, as I said, stated an intention of the executive branch to support one of the main features of the Asian Development Bank charter, which is the management of special funds in addition to the regular capital of the Bank. He made it quite clear, and we have done so in every statement, that this was subject to presentation to the Congress of our proposal for an authorization for this sort for use of AID funds.

We are withholding any further discussion of special funds in any formal setting at all until we have an expression of intent by the Congress to support the expansion of multilateral channels of assistanceas this Bank would be.

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. This title VIII would give you the legal justification for making more specific explorations of what our commitment should be?

Mr. POATS. That is the way I interpret it, yes. 

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. You would not suggest, in fact you are not advocating, any dollar ceiling on the authorization for this regional multilateral approach?

Mr. POATS. We have been unable to arrive at a meaningful and proper ceiling.

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. Again it seems most unwise, if you are unable to define any ceiling at all, for us to give you the authority to proceed anyway.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Zablocki. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

I want to join with my colleague in his concern about the need for an open-end authorization under title VIII. Mr. Bundy, personally, at least, I am deeply interested and very pleased that title VIII is included in this bill. Mr. Bell, in replying to a question of mine, said that this committee would be consulted, and kept fully informed as to the future requests and utilization of the funds under this authorization. 

Mr. BUNDY. That is correct.

Mr. ZABLOCKI. On the other hand, it has been amply demonstrated that Congress, when it is presented with a worthwhile program as in the case of the Asian Development Bank, can and does act promptly.

That agreement was signed in December, and the Congress has already approved U.S. participation in that institution. 

Therefore, if we have presented to us a worthwhile project the money will be made available promptly. I am sure the Congress will approve the necessary authorization and appropriation. I think it might be unwise to give a blank check. I agree with my colleague, this

new title VIII gives you an open-end authorization; therefore, I have some reservations about it.

Couldn't the executive branch come up with a more concrete proposal so the committee will know exactly what they need and how they intend to use the money?

Mr. POATS. Mr. Zablocki, we would come to this committee and describe the proposed appropriation that we would seek. We would explain the program and this committee would have a voice in determining what the Congress did with the appropriation request. 

Our difficulty is that this is a 5-year authorization request. This is a wholly new field of activity. We do not know the degree to which other countries will come forward and share in it. 

Furthermore, this will be influenced by many variables such as the solution of the war in Vietnam, the attitude of the European countries toward participation in the area. There are so many uncertainties that we have been unable to properly describe the likely requirements for a reasonable program over the 5-year period in the proposed authorization.

We have stated in the proposed legislation a number of criteria for determining what we should do: the extent of participation by the potential donor countries, peaceful cooperation by the peoples in the area, acceptance of multilateral institutions, and the performance of these multilateral institutions. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Is my understanding correct that the Agency intends to come before this committee and explain the program fully prior to seeking the appropriations? 

Mr. POATS. Yes, indeed. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. That is very clear?

Mr. POATS. Very clear. A provision in the language of the proposed act requires the executive branch at the direction of Congress to come before this committee and describe its entire program regardless of whether there is an open-ended authorization or not. 

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. Would the gentleman yield?

Mr. ZABLOCKI. I note that section 272(b) of the bill specifically exempts funds to be used by the Asian Development or the IBRD Bank from the requirements of the Foreign Assistance Act, as amended. 

Mr. POATS. It exempts them from the AID type of detailed analysis of projects and from the requirement that management of the project is by an American engineer. These are restrictions which couldn't be accepted by the World Bank or the Asian Development Bank in the use of funds which are commingled from many sources. 

Mr. BUNDY. This is a standard provision.

Mr. POATS. This type of exemption is in the present act with respect to the World Bank and with respect to the Indus River development project. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Would elimination of this exemption make the rest of title III undesirable? 

Mr. POATS. If it were eliminated by the Congress it might be interpreted as an expression of disapproval of the transfer of funds to the Asian Development Bank or World Bank to administer under their own rules. I would urge the committee not to strike this from the proposal. I think it would be impossible for such agencies to administer funds from Japan, the United States, Britain, Germany, Nether-

lands and so on with the same type of restrictions that are presently imposed on the administration of AID funds in purely bilateral projects. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Mr. Chairman, if I have time, I want to ask a question concerning the Nam Ngum Dam in Laos. 

Have the necessary arrangements been negotiated to run transmission lines between Laos and Thailand? What is Thailand's attitude about being dependent upon power from another country? 

Mr. POATS. An agreement has been reached, whereby initially power from Thailand will be used in the construction of the Nam Ngum Dam and later on, power from Nam Ngum will flow across the river to Thailand. The Thai Government has demonstrated its support of this project by contributing a $1 million loan for cement to be used on the project this loan to be repaid as a deduction from charges on the power Thailand buys from Laos. The power will be a reserve, low-cost supply for Thailand-not a supply on which Thailand must depend.

This project is managed by the World Bank. 

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Broomfield. 

Mr. BROOMFIELD. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

Mr. Poats, on page 238 on the regional book on Laos, 1(c) states the United States reimburses the Government of Laos for essential foreign exchange expenditures abroad for such purposes as higher education, medical treatment, official expenses of the Governmenthow much is the United States spending for the Lao Government official expenses in the free world?

Mr. POATS. [Security deletion.] Mr. BROOMFIELD. Under 1 (c) the United States reimburses the Gov ernment of Laos for essential foreign exchange expenditure abroad for such purposes-I want 1(a), as far as U.S. import programs are concerned. What commodities are we bringing into Laos under the import program and do these move through the private channels in 

Laos? What controls do we have over there for distribution?

Mr. POATS. Under the import program we are bringing in rice, machinery, spare parts, trucks, and petroleum, oils, and lubricants.

They move through the commercial channels with the exception of rice, which, I believe, is entirely run through a Laos Government commissary for sales to Laos Government employees. 

Mr. BROOMFIELD. In your prepared statement on attachment C there is an interesting item: public safety. I notice a decline in the amount being requested. In fiscal 1966 it was $27 million [security deletion]. 

Why the decrease in the public safety program for Vietnam?

Mr. POATS. The 1966 figure represents a sharp increase from the rend line resulting from our decision to equip a police field force of 

8,500 men. We also have in 1966 financed the provision of boats for operation of a resources control unit, a large unit of about 4,000 men on the Mekong River and its tributaries and the canals in the Mekong area.

This one-time expenditure for capital equipment will not need to be repeated in 1967, you rodin

Mr. BROOMFIELD. Getting back to the Mekong River Basin part. 

What part is Cambodia playing?

Mr. POATS. It is a member of the four-nation Mekong Committee. 

Investigations by Australian, Japanese, and perhaps other engineers have been conducted in Cambodia for the last few years on potential projects. Three of these projects have been identified: Battambang, Prek Thnot and Tonle Sap. The Cambodians have expressed interest in going ahead with the Prek Thnot project. They have been talking with the French Government, and the Australian Government, I believe, about financing for that project. 

[Security deletion.] 

Mr. BROOMFIELD. One final question I would like to ask is in regard to the statement on page 252 of the briefing statements; the $7 million to expand and transform the existing regional graduate school of engineering in Bangkok into an internationally administered graduate school of engineering.

Could you give us an explanation of what the school presently is and the nature of the proposed transformation? 

Mr. POATS. This school was organized under SEATO. It is assisted by the United States and several other countries. Our assistance is largely in the form of provision of faculty advisers under contract with the University of Colorado Research Foundation. The members of SEATO have concluded that this institution would be more useful and would be more effective in promoting regional interchange of ideas and development in the area if it were on a broader geographic and political basis than is SEATO. We are considering with the countries of the area the possibility of making it a truly regional school with its operating expenses assured by an endowment perhaps from foundations or other sources; possibly to expand its plant, to encourage countries of the whole of Asia to send professors and students to the institution. 

We think it is one of the more promising areas of regional development. We do not have authority under U.S. law to provide endowment to insure the long-term tenure of faculty and other operating costs. 

We are thinking therefore of assistance possibly in the form of capital improvements, assuming, of course, that adequate plans are developed. 

If we can get at the same time we make that decision assurance of endowment support, long-term commitments from foundations and other governments, then we would have a package that would be viable. 

We don't have all those assurances.

Chairman MORGAN. Mrs. Kelly. 

Mrs. KELLY. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

[Security deletion.] Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Thomson. 

Mr. THOMSON. Mr. Poats, on page 3 of your statistical attachment, I guess in relation to contingency funds in southeast Asia for Laos, $6 million, and then in Thailand, contingency fund, southeast Asia, $7.5 million. 

Where did that money come from? Was that from the supplemental that was just approved? 

Mr. POATS. That is correct. 

Mr. THOMSON. Then why isn't the $7.5 million for Laos?

Mr. POATS. I beg your pardon. I spoke too quickly. That is drawn from the supplement to the AID bill which was introduced before the bill was completed in the Congress last June.

We proposed $89 million in additional assistance in southeast Asia, primarily Vietnam. These funds were presented to the Congress in detail at that time. 

Mr. THOMSON. Then why isn't the additional $7.5 million for each of these countries shown that was contained in the latest supplemental that was passed?

Mr. POATS. Let me see if I see which passage you are referring to. 

You mean the fiscal 1966 ?

Mr. THOMSON. Right. 

Mr. POATS. In the case of Laos, $6 million is from the amount appropriated in the first supplemental southeast Asia contingency fund last June; $7.5 million is the amount for Thailand, [security deletion]. 

Mr. THOMSON. It seems your answer is contradictory. You say it was funded last summer Mr. POATS. $6 million of the southeast Asia contingency fund appropriation requested last summer was for Laos. In addition, $7.5 million of the current supplemental supporting assistance request for $415 million has been designated for Laos and is contained in the [security deletion] figure in the fiscal year 1966 column. 

Mr. THOMSON. Has the Inspector General ever been dispatched to 

Vietnam to check into the misuse of AID funds? 

Mr. POATS. Yes; the Inspector General has been in Vietnam three times, I believe, in the past 12 months. He was there in February. 

His deputy is going out next week. He or his deputy has been there quite frequently lately.

The General Accounting Office also has a team in Vietnam now. 

The Moss subcommittee of the House Government Operations Committee is going out. 

Mr. THOMSON. What are we doing in relation to buying in Korea? 

Are we buying steel in Japan?

Mr. POATS. No; we are not. There has been some confusion in the public description of our policy. We are undertaking a pilot procurement procedure a 90to 120-day trial-permitting Vietnamese importers to buy either directly from the United States or from those less developed Asian countries which agree to accept in payment an AID letter of credit tied to purchases from the United States of steel products, tools with a high iron and steel content, and a stipulated quantity of scrap.

These Asian producers such as the Koreans or Taiwanese-two countries which have accepted this letter-of-credit arrangement may buy the semifinished materials which go into galvanized sheet or pipe, from any free world source. The Koreans or Chinese receive payment in a letter of credit tied to procurement of the U.S. products that I mentioned.

The consequence of this, we believe, will be that Taiwan and Korea will increase their purchases of iron and steel products from the United States in amounts greater than the amount they expend in Japan-if they buy from Japan-on semifinished steel. 

Mr. THOMSON. Are we really trying to help our fighting ally Korea economically by the military and economic purchases there?

Mr. POATS. In a sense, yes. This policy resulted from our inability adequately to enforce the componentry rules which apply on most commodities.

In a homogeneous commodity like a sheet of steel, it is difficult to determine where the components came from. We found the componentry rule hard to enforce. The Koreans argued they were complying because they were buying in the United States scrap valued at more than the value of the scrap that went into the semifinished black plate purchased in Japan.

We disagreed with that interpretation and suspended procurement of steel products from Korea. This happened shortly after the 

Koreans sent troops to Vietnam.

The Koreans at that time were seeking to expand their sales to Vietnam. They resented this denial of a market to them which they thought they held legitimately. I was also concerned that to suspend procurement of this important item in the commercial import program would have a damaging effect on prices in Vietnam and would cause further inflationary pressures.

We worked out a scheme which we felt certain would assure continued rapid supply of low-cost galvanized sheet to Vietnam, would offer Korea a chance for exports, and. at the same time, would not damage the U.S. steel industry or the U.S. balance of payments. 

I presented this scheme to representatives of the steel industry in this country, and they endorsed it. 

Subsequently, after there was some controversy about it, I had a meeting with representatives of the Iron & Steel Institute. They expressed no opposition to the plan. There has been opposition to it by some salesmen of American steel products who I think, frankly, simply don't understand that the ultimate consequence will be that these dollars will flow back to the U.S. iron and steel industry. 

Mr. THOMSON. I asked Mr. Bell the other day if his appeals for civilians to go into the program to stabilize the villages had produced any response other than from members of the U.S. Congress. Could you give us your view on how people are volunteering to assist?

Mr. POATS. We are getting many volunteers. We have conducted several recruiting campaigns in major cities. One is going on now in New Orleans. We are attempting to insure that we get good people, people who can learn the Vietnamese language, people who can adapt to a village situation and work well with the military forces and the local government.

We are expanding our staff. The staff in the provinces will be nearly doubled by this coming June over last June. We are putting many recruits into training. This causes temporary shortages of people in the field. This is a hard decision to make but we think it is the right one.

We have set up a training program at the University of Hawaii to concentrate our training of these village level workers for Vietnam, Thailand, and Laos.

Far East area, which I assume is what you are addressing yourself to at the moment [security deletion].

[Security deletion.] Chairman MORGAN. Supporting assistance in Laos has been in creasing since 1965. Mr. POATS. I don't see any possibility of getting out of grant assistance in Laos as long as it is regarded in the U.S. interest to maintain the existence of an independent Laos nation. 

If the security threat is reduced, if there were a political settlement in Laos which removed the threat of insurgency by the Communist elements there supported by the Vietminh, then the problem would be greatly reduced. [Security deletion.] We have an increasing cost for air support operations, flying relief supplies and people, medical equipment, food and so on, to the otherwise inaccessible tribal units stretching across northern Laos [security deletion]. Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Monagan. 

Mr. MONAGAN. [Security deletion.] Mr. Bundy, I have noticed some suggestions lately from different sources, some of them in the Government, some of them out, that it might be advisable to have a rapprochement with Communist China. Is there any sentiment in the 

Department in this direction? What is your feeling about this? 

Mr. BUNDY. Congressman, I do not see that anything would be gained by going beyond the general line of policy that we are pursuing, which does involve making it clear that there is the possibility of contacts, particularly on an unofficial basis, the doctors, public health scientists, scholars, newsmen, all those channels that are open. 

On trade they have made it pretty clear that they would not trade with us.

For us to take any public initiative in this area would be construed wrongly by them and to some extent by other nations in the area and would lead nowhere.

I think these are matters on which we should keep an open mind. 

On the diplomatic side, I don't think we should change in any way our position on the United Nations [security deletion]. I think we ought to proceed on the basis of protecting the Republic of China's position, continuing to oppose Peiping's initiative. 

It is clear they have placed very harsh conditions on their accession to the U.N. Any desire to participate in the U.N., if it exists at all, is not constructive but relates to their desire to gain a major prestige victory. In the United Nations they would be a most disruptive influence. I think there are abundant reasons to continue to oppose their accession. I don't find persuasive the argument that this would have a significant effect on their attitudes and policy. 

What is sometimes described as the neurosis theory-the theory that their attitude has been triggered by their isolation-this theory doesn't account for many facets of their behavior. This is a matter we were discussing in the subcommittee the other day. The theory does not account for their attitude toward the Soviet Union and the Afro-Asian world; it doesn't account for their very, very cold reception to the kind of initiative that France took 2 years ago. 1

We must continue to maintain contacts-the sort of dialog we are having at Warsaw-but we have to take these steps one at a time.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Fraser. 

Mr. FRASER. Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

Mr. Poats, I would like to just spend a minute on the organization of AID as seen from your operation. In looking at some of the components of aid to Vietnam, I identify such things as agriculture, public safety, and so on. In AID is there some service or division of AID that produces a special reservoir of know-how and resources such as on public safety?

Mr. POATS. There is an Office of Public Safety. 

Mr. FRASER. Is this true of agriculture?

Mr. POATS. There are agricultural experts in each of the regional bureaus and a central agricultural chief. 

Mr. FRASER. Education? 

Mr. POATS. That is correct.

Mr. FRASER. Transport? 

Mr. POATS. To a lesser extent. 

Mr. FRASER. In your statement, particularly on page 8, you stressed the importance of political and social development. Is there such an agency or division specializing in the political side in AID?

Mr. POATS. We don't call it political development but the planning officer and some of his subordinates in each of the regions is concerned with political development. In Vietnam, for example, a good part of the work that we lump under the category of provincial operations is actually in guiding the creation of effective village government, working with Vietnamese cadre and so on. 

Mr. FRASER. I am not talking about what goes on in the field. I am talking about central AID operation. Is there any particular grouping or division which concerns itself with the techniques of encouraging local government?

Mr. POATS. Yes, we have a public administration adviser and we also have in the planning office of the Development Planning Division of my bureau a political scientist and two assistants in this area. 

Mr. FRASER. I am told that about 2 years ago the new AID director to Vietnam wanted to do away with the IVS personnel. It didn't happen, but this is what he wanted to do. 

Mr. POATS. That is an overstatement of his position, as I recall it. 

He did not want to expand an autonomous IVS operation. He wanted them to be integrated into our provincial operations. There was some dispute with the team chief on this point but it was settled amicably and now we are expanding the U.S. staff in Vietnam. 

Mr. FRASER. The impression I got from the conversations was that he wanted them out, maybe it was a separate agency, I don't know- Mr. POATS. That was an overstatement of his view. 

10 Mr. FRASER. From what you say now, we are talking about increasing the IVS personnel?

Mr. POATS. Yes, we have been working with Arthur Gardner. He is recruiting. We hope to have a substantial expansion. IVS/can do much in English teaching, community development, programs similar to those of the Peace Corps, and refugee work. We are ask-

ing them to provide staff down to the district level in several more secure provinces for agricultural development work. 

Mr. FRASER. In your discussion of Thailand, for example, in contrast to your discussion with Vietnam, there is no reference to the political and social side.

Mr. POATS. Only a brief reference, Mr. Fraser. I do see a great deal of hope in two or three parallel developments that have taken place there. There have been organized seminars at the tambol level in training the tambol council how to operate, what the opportunities are, the problems are, to significantly address these problems and to call for help.

There also has been for 3 years, at our suggestion, a program of village leader and district leader training. This is a combination of political orientation and actual on-job training. This program has now reached practically all of the lower level of leadership in the rural areas. 

Mr. FRASER. Is this AID supported?

Mr. POATS. In a way. The tambol program produces requirements on the government for assistance at the village level which we partially meet with our project in accelerated rural development, education, agriculture, health, so on. We are expanding our village medical program, as is the Peace Corps, to respond to the kind of requirements that come forward from this tambol council. 

Mr. FRASER. Why is it that you don't have the division in AID which would be maybe not comparable to public safety, but where you would have people who are studying, reviewing, and examining this problem of political development in the rural areas. 

Mr. POATS. With respect to both Thailand and Vietnam, we do have people studying such problems. We have an academic group, the 

Southeast Asian Development Advisory Group, looking at the area as a whole. We have at UCLA a group to do exactly this for Thailand, to give us the longer term, deeper analysis of the sociological and political aspects of the problem we are coping with. 

Mr. FRASER. Why don't you have that in AID itself?

Mr. POATS. I think we have found it is hard to assemble in AID a few people who can provide this expertise for a whole group of countries. We have found it better to work on a country-by-country basis and to tap the facilities of the academic community. We do have the generalists that I have referred to in our planning office. 

Mr. FRASER. For example, one of the success stories with local government is in East Pakistan. To what extent is this experience known to the people in the Far East area and utilized by them? 

Mr. POATS. It is known in the sense that there are periodic exchanges of information by the program and planning people on an agency-wide basis. We have had reports circulated on the work in 

East Pakistan to all of our people.

Mr. FRASER. I had a talk with one of your top people in one of the southeast Asian countries who discounted the political side. He seemed to think everything turned on security. 

Mr. POATS. I believe that is not the view of either the military leaders, or the political leaders of the United States who are directly involved.

Mr. FRASER. I have a strong feeling that AID has been forced to accept responsibility for this political side of the conflict and yet it has not organized itself adequately for the job, although it may have now

Mr. POATS. We have organized- 

Mr. FRASER. In terms of the long pull.

Mr. POATS. We have pulled together recently in Vietnam, on an interagency basis under the direction of Deputy Ambassador Porter, the main elements of the rural reconstruction program. He has on his immediate staff information, local government, and development people. They in turn produce the general guidance for our operations in the field.

Mr. FRASER. Isn't it likely to be important not just in Vietnam but in other places in the world?

Mr. POATS. I think it is. We are applying that in Thailand. 

Mr. FRASER. What about assimilating and refining this information and feeding it back to the program planning in the other regions? 

Is there any mechanism in AID for doing that?

Mr. POATS. We have done it in the form of reports, surveys, and studies. There will be a steady proliferation of people from Vietnam to other areas; we are attempting to retain them on a career basis. 

Mr. FRASER. You don't retain this focus of concern and emphasis of responsibility the way you do for public safety and agriculture and education and so on.

Mr. POATS. I think there might be some advantage in your suggestion that there be a fixed body assembling this information and systematically passing it on to all the operators. 

Mr. FRASER. I saw an imaginative proposal. One of the fellows in the local development area went into the Dominican Republic. 

This isn't your area, but I want to illustrate my point. He wrote a report which I thought was extremely imaginative, a way to bring to the Dominican Republic some form of local government including techniques for establishing boundaries.

It was a very sophisticated study, but it just sort of sank to the ground in terms of its acceptance by others, I would judge, partly because nobody has thought hard about this technique which I think is quite important in our efforts around the world. 

Mr. POATS. We are talking about something here which is essentially an internal matter, of a society, its government. We are talking about intervention in the form of its politics, local administrationg about a delicate area and hard for an outsider to move in on [security deletion].

Mr. FRASER. Unless you have people sensitive to this, it is like rural development which is just now coming into major focus. One would have hoped if there had been enough insight there would have been emphasis on this in years past. 

In India we have the problem of famine. It is partly due apparently to crop failures. There hasn't been brought to bear, as I understand it, sufficient emphasis on the problem of agricultural development until the crisis grew to such proportions that it was virtually forced on everyone.

I have the same impression about Vietnam. The problem of the political side of the struggle is forced on us in a way we can't escape

it. I don't get the sense of the full lesson that is being drawn, the lessons are being assimilated so that we are able to translate this into program planning in other parts of the world. 

I agree about the sensitivity. But it means nothing if your AID mission has no interest or sophistication or understanding about this. 

As you know around the world the characteristic situation is that in the rural areas 70 percent of the people live, and after 10 years of aid you find no visible impact on their lives. 

Mr. POATS. Take the Philippines for example. The two major departures in our regular programs there have been in these very areas. 

We have two provincial programs working with local governments to change the situation and channel popular aspirations, create organizations which will work with local leadership. The two selected provinces were ones where the local government wanted this kind of activity. We can't go into a province where we are not welcome and can't get to first base with that local leadership.

Mr. FRASER. You can't do this with any of the programs, tax reform or anything else unless you persuade the people or they show an interest in it. Sometimes it is a matter of dialog. 

Mr. POATS. In Korea, I think we have been guilty of not facing up to the importance of local institutions in the total development process. We have worked with and not attempted seriously to influence the Koreans to alter an agricultural credit system. We have in the last year put our people out in the provinces. We have people in training programs to go into that work.

Secretary Freeman is very interested in trying to find ways to involve the Agriculture Department in this sort of local level operation in partnership with AID. It is now being considered under the foodfor-freedom bill.

Mr. FRASER. I will close with one comment. Mr. Poats, it is my view, you are far more experienced than I in this, this concern about what happens to people in the rural areas in terms of their opportunity to participate in what happens to them at the local level, the formalization of local government, and all this ought to stand near No. 1 on the list of our AID programs around the world.

Yet there is no division or central point in AID today that is concerned with this. I know there is a lot going on. I know sophisticated work is going on. It seems to me the priority this is accorded is not sufficient, and considerably more needs to be done unless we have a lot more Vietnams.

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. That argument is of interest to me. saying the next 12 months are so uncertain that you don't feel there is any point in providing for a larger contingency fund than $70 million, even though more than three times as much will be needed in the current fiscal year.

In the same breath you request Congress to authorize this foreign aid program open-ended for a 5-year period. 

I would think the very uncertainties about the nature of the international situation, and the type of reaction likely on the part of AID and the Government generally is so impossible to define, that you

would not want as much freedom as you are asking. It would seem to me self-evident that even if you want such freedom that the Congress should not provide it. 

We are not trying to hamstring you. I feel as Mr. Monagan does that it may well be necessary for you to have reasonable leeway; that is, not the same as to give you a blank check. In effect you say if you come back and report on what you are planning to request from the Appropriations Committee every year, that such a report should be enough to keep our committee out of your hair.

It doesn't strike me as a logical argument. This approach is more likely to make us hostile to what you are doing because we have no way of participating, taking any responsibility, or sharing in the understanding of problems and why we should react in a certain way. 

In other words, I can't help but think this is very ill-advised. You ask us to be shortsighted about the contingency fund and yet ask for complete freedom to operate with far larger amounts in really the entire world.

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. The open-ended flavor permeates this new program. You suggest not only a 5-year term but in almost every case the lid is off as far as the dollar amounts are concerned. I would think for psychological reasons if for no other, it would be wise to suggest that we should limit you to $100 million, for example, for the multilateral and regional efforts. As a practical matter, that amount is what is indicated as the type of support that you feel we should provide.

I am not saying that we should probe now, into everything you think should be done. However, that is not the same thing as saying, as you do: "Give us complete freedom to decide for ourselves and don't you worry about it." This is not your sole responsibility. We have to provide the money and, of course, the appropriations process is one check on what you do.

But our committee has an understandable and legitimate interest in sharing the responsibility assigned to us. I can't help but compare the language in the present law, section 201, and your proposed section 271. You, as Mr. Bundy pointed out, are certainly suggesting something far more comprehensive with respect to your freedom in section 271 than Congress actually provided in section 201. Yet it would seem that both sections are attempting to provide reasonable tools for you to operate.

I would like to see something on record to justify the detailed authority which you are requesting in section 271. 

Mr. POATS. I will be happy to submit that for the record. 

(The information follows:) INFORMATION ON TITLE VIII 

The new title underscores the special significance of the expanded southeast Asia development program which the President proposed in his speech delivered at Johns Hopkins University on April 7, 1965.

Section 271, whch relates to general provisions, is the first section of the new title. Congressional approval of the title will reaffirm the importance the United 

States places on economic and social progress in achieving foreign policy objec tives of peace and stability in southeast Asia, placing special emphasis upon cooperative efforts to solve social and economic problems in the region.

1 Section 271 authorizes the President to furnish assistance to southeast Asia through multilateral institutions and through multilateral and/or bilateral programs or projects which serve regional development purposes. The furnishing of assistance under this title would, of course, be subject to appropriation requests to the Congress, based on specific programs. 

While it is not possible at this time to indicate specific magnitudes of funds for which appropriations will be sought under the proposed authority, the executive branch considers favorable action by the Congress to be of particularly timely importance.

We feel that the establishment of a separate title and special authority for assistance to southeast Asia has the unique advantage of demonstrating this emphasis as a new departure thereby drawing the interest of other countries and encouraging their contributions to multilaterally administered funds for development in southeast Asia. Increased involvement on the part of developed nations in the future of southeast Asia is an important objective of the President's new approach to the problems of the region. 

The criteria set forth under section 272 of the new title are designed to encourage Asian initiatives in development and to stimulate cooperative efforts in solving common problems. Thus, funds used for the purposes of this title could be made available to the International Bank for Reconstruction and 

Development and the Asian Development Bank under their supervision, employing their criteria and procedures instead of those of AID. This would insure that these two Banks could operate as truly independent international institutions and administer effectively multilateral funds made up of contributions from more than one country.

Section 273 of the new title authorizes appropriations to the President for each of the fiscal years 1967 through 1971 of such funds as may be necessary to carry out the purposes of this title. However, no appropriation under this title is being requested at this time for fiscal year 1967. The executive branch will seek funds under this authorization as additional programs are developed. In fiscal year 1967 the initial portions of the expanded southeast Asia development program will continue to be carried out on a limited basis with technical cooperation and supporting assistance funds, to the extent those funds are available for such activities.

The executive branch would, of course, be available for further examination of the program by the committee. This would permit the committee to make in- ✰ formed recommendations regarding the course of AID programs under title

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

I would like to join Mrs. Bolton and express my appreciation for the testimony that was presented to the committee this morning, and the fact that the statements were made available without being labeled "Secret" or "Confidential." I don't believe our AID program is getng enough attention in the press. We read a lot about our military activities in Vietnam but not enough of what the United States is doing in the economic, psychological, and political field. 

To that extent I am very happy that the chairman has agreed to permit the Subcommittee on the Far East and the Pacific to hold hearngs after the Easter recess specifically on the question of U.S. activities in these fields-in Vietnam and southeast Asia. The hearings will be specifically limited to economic assistance and matters other than military. What we do and how well we use the lessons we learn in 

Vietnam is most important. I feel we can win military battles and still lose the political war.

Mr. Chairman, I would like permission to insert in the record a letter that was sent to the USOM personnel in Vietnam by a resident of Wisconsin which I believe is indicative of the attitude of most Americans when they realize what we are doing there. 

Chairman MORGAN. Without objection, it is so ordered. (The letter of Maureen Rinzel, above referred to, is as follows :) 

"GERMANTOWN, WIS. 

"DEAR FELLOW AMERICANS: After reading an excellent article about the tre mendous job the men of USOM are performing in Vietnam, I decided to take some time and extend my praise to you personally. All of you are doing work which certainly cannot be measured in true worth on this earth. It is through your actions and blood that the war in Vietnam shall be won; and as a fellow 

American my thoughts are constantly over there with you and my prayers are always behind you. "I think one has to be dedicated to the betterment of society before one can be truly convinced that all wars aren't won by bombs, militia, guns, etc. Being a teacher myself, I know what a great feeling it is to see a child grin from ear to ear over some accomplishment. Being a home economist, I see even better how the improving of the social and economical problems can open the door to a really better place a better Vietnam. This truly would be a great accomplishment. I, myself, hope that someday I can give my services such as you are doing. God love all of you; you're a great group of Americans. "Sincerely, 

"MAUREEN RINZEL." 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Secretary Bundy, concerning the $28 million fund from the Philippine War Damage Claim Act to be used for joint education programs. I would appreciate if you would put in the record a statement how this money really should be used and a status report on the negotiation for its use?

Mr. BUNDY. I am thoroughly familiar with it, of course. This is a matter that under the appropriate legislation calls for an agreement between the two Presidents on the use to be made of this money. 

We have put a broad memorandum of understanding, draft memorandum in front of the Philippine Government, and have not had their firm response. 

My understanding is that extensive efforts are underway in the Philippines to get a picture of what use it might go to as between pub-

lic universities and private universities, various types of projects that will fall within the spirit and intent of the provision. 

Just as soon as we get that draft memorandum worked out, we can proceed to wrap up the projects. How it will proceed from there I don't know. We are going to operate within the legal provisions that require ultimate consent by our President to the uses that are proposed. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Are we going to accede to the recommendations of the conference that the $28 million be made available to the Philippine 

Government for its sole programing?

Mr. BUNDY. I don't think we have legal power to do so, nor would we think that would be a wise provision. This is a question where from to time the question of our depositing money in the interim without putting it under their control to assist them, the Treasury believes this is not within the powers as the money was appropriated, so this is not being done. Mr. ZABLOCKI. Mr. Chairman, just one brief question of Mr. Poats. 

At the top of page 12 of your statement you say that U.S. AID personnel are working in the rural areas of Laos. Can you tell the committee if any Lao Government personnel are working with AID and IVS personnel in these villages and what do they do? This you can supply for the record if you would. 

Thank you. 

(The information follows:) LAO GOVERNMENT PERSONNEL INVOLVEMENT IN VILLAGE CLUSTER PROGRAM 

AID community development advisers and IVS personnel work closely at provincial levels with provincial Governors and district officials in the development of rural programs. Tassangs, the equivalent of our elected township supervisors, and Naibans (village chiefs) are often the center of planning activity. Agents of several Royal Lao Government ministries provide services in the villages. 

Among these are the Agricultural Extension Service, the Lao Veterinary Service, the Forestry and Water Service, and the Public Works Department. In addition to the provision of teachers for the school system, the Education Ministry provides trained "basic educators" who work with villagers in the areas of adult education, sanitation, simple construction, and home economics. Public Health 

Ministry medics provide basic medical services. The Lao Army also runs some civic action programs in rural areas which are similar to those of the basic educators.

One of the main objectives of the rural development program in Laos is to maintain and increase popular support for the Royal Lao Government. Lao officials are intimately involved in the direction of the program at all levels. An indication of the interest in this area is that the Prime Minister also holds the rural affairs portfolio.

The Royal Lao Government is working rapidly to strengthen its extension programs in rural areas. Due to a lack of skilled manpower, many of these programs are currently on a pilot basis and do not yet cover all of Laos. In fact, Lao Government participation in development is actually more intensive in areas where a formal program such as the AID-assisted village cluster program operates.ia
